How To Use This Booklet

4 questions
P0

The priority system — read this first

This booklet is built for an iOS developer with ~3 years of experience. Every question carries a priority tag that tells you how likely you are to be asked it and how badly it hurts to miss it.

P0 — Must know cold. Asked in almost every iOS interview at your level. If you hesitate, stumble, or give a textbook-but-wrong answer, the interview is effectively over. There are about 90 P0 questions in this booklet. Learn these to the point where you can answer while distracted.

P1 — Very likely. Asked in most interviews. These separate "we'll pass" from "we'll hire". You should be able to answer with a concrete example from your own work.

P2 — Differentiator. Senior signal. Asked by strong teams, product companies, and anyone hiring above your current band. Missing one is survivable; nailing one moves your offer up.

P3 — Deep / rare. Shows real depth. Nobody rejects you for missing these, but they create the "this person actually knows the platform" impression that gets you the higher title.

How to study with this: first pass, read only P0 across all chapters — that's your survival kit and takes about two days. Second pass, P1 in the areas the job description emphasises. Third pass, P2/P3 in your two strongest areas so you have somewhere to go deep when the interviewer asks "what do you know really well?"

P0

What a 3-year iOS interview actually looks like

Most companies run four to six rounds. Knowing which round tests what stops you from over-preparing the wrong thing.

Round 1 — Screening (30–45 min, recruiter or engineer). Rapid-fire language and platform questions. Pure P0. Struct vs class, ARC, retain cycles, GCD vs async/await, @State vs @StateObject, why weak self. They are checking that you are not lying on your CV. Answer in 30–60 seconds each, no rambling.

Round 2 — Core iOS deep dive (60 min). One or two topics taken all the way down. Typically concurrency, memory, or UI internals. Expect "why" chains: Why does that leak? → Why does unowned crash? → When would you deliberately choose unowned? This is where P1 lives.

Round 3 — Coding / machine coding (60–90 min). Either LeetCode-easy/medium in Swift, or — increasingly common in India and at product companies — a "machine coding round": build a working feature in 60–90 minutes. Image-loading list with caching, paginated feed, debounced search, offline-first list. Chapter 15 covers these end to end.

Round 4 — System design for mobile (45–60 min). Design a chat app, a feed, an offline sync layer, an analytics SDK, an image cache. They want layering, trade-offs, failure handling — not UIKit trivia. Chapter 14.

Round 5 — Architecture / code quality (45 min). MVVM vs MVC vs TCA, dependency injection, testability, modularisation, how you'd review a bad PR. Chapter 6 and 9.

Round 6 — Behavioural / hiring manager (45 min). Conflict, ownership, a production incident, a technical decision you regret. Chapter 18. At 3 years this round is genuinely weighted — they are deciding whether you are a mid-level engineer or a senior-shaped one.

P0

How to answer so you sound senior

There is a shape that good answers have, and interviewers notice it immediately.

1. Lead with the one-sentence answer. Not "so there are a few things…". Say the answer, then expand. "Structs are value types with copy semantics, classes are reference types with identity and ARC — I default to struct and reach for class when I need identity, inheritance, or Objective-C interop."

2. Give the mechanism, not the slogan. "weak avoids retain cycles" is a slogan. "weak stores the reference in a side table so ARC can zero it when the object deallocates, which is why it must be optional" is a mechanism. Mechanism answers get you the next level.

3. Name the trade-off. Every real answer has a cost. Say it before they ask. "Actors remove data races but introduce reentrancy, so state can change across an await — I re-read state after every suspension point."

4. Ground it in your own code. "We hit this in our checkout flow when…" is worth more than any definition. Prepare three stories: a performance problem you fixed, a crash you debugged, an architecture decision you made.

5. Say "I don't know" fast and well. "I haven't used that directly. My mental model is X — is that close?" This scores far better than bluffing, and interviewers actively test for it.

P1

The 20 things you must be able to write on a whiteboard

If you can write these from memory, you can survive almost any 3-year iOS interview: a retain cycle and its fix; a generic Result-returning network call with async/await; an actor with a cache; a Task with cancellation; a diffable data source snapshot apply; a UITableViewCell reuse + async image load without flicker; a debounce with Combine and with Task.sleep; an @Observable view model wired to a SwiftUI view; a protocol-based dependency injection with a mock; an XCTest/Swift Testing async test; Codable with custom keys and a date strategy; a Core Data background-context save; URLSession with retry and exponential backoff; an LRU cache; a NSCache-backed image loader; a coordinator; DispatchQueue barrier for a thread-safe array; withCheckedThrowingContinuation wrapping a callback API; an AsyncStream from a delegate; and an equatable/hashable value type.

Every one of those appears in this booklet with full code.

1

Swift Language Core

29 questions
P0

Struct vs class — the full answer

One-liner: structs are value types copied on assignment with no identity; classes are reference types with identity, inheritance, deinit, and ARC-managed lifetime.

The detail that separates good answers:

struct PointS { var x = 0 }
final class PointC { var x = 0 }

var s1 = PointS(); var s2 = s1; s2.x = 10
print(s1.x)            // 0  — independent copy

let c1 = PointC(); let c2 = c1; c2.x = 10
print(c1.x)            // 10 — same instance
print(c1 === c2)       // true — identity exists only for classes

Storage is not the headline. "Structs are on the stack, classes on the heap" is the answer that gets you downgraded, because it is only sometimes true: a struct captured by an escaping closure, stored in a class property, or boxed in an existential lives on the heap too. The correct framing is semantics: value semantics (independent copies, no shared mutable state) versus reference semantics (shared identity).

Other real differences:

structclass
Mutationneeds mutating; let instance is fully immutablemethods mutate freely even on let
Inheritancenone (compose with protocols)single inheritance
deinitnoyes
Memberwise initfreemust write it
Thread safetyinherently safer — each copy is localshared state needs synchronisation
Sendableautomatic if all members are Sendablemust be final + immutable, or @unchecked

When I choose a class at 3 years of experience: I need identity (a session, a coordinator, a cache); I need a deinit to release a resource; I need reference semantics deliberately (one view model observed by several views); I'm subclassing UIKit; or the type is huge and copied constantly and I don't want copy-on-write complexity.

Follow-ups they will ask: "Does a let struct with a var property allow mutation?" (No — let on a value type freezes the whole value.) "Does a let class instance allow mutating its var properties?" (Yes — let only freezes the reference.)

P0

What is copy-on-write and how would you implement it?

Swift's standard library collections (Array, Dictionary, Set, String, Data) are structs, but they hold a reference to heap buffer. Copying the struct copies only the pointer; the expensive buffer copy is deferred until someone mutates a value while the buffer is shared. That gives you value semantics at reference-copy cost.

var a = [1, 2, 3]
var b = a          // O(1) — buffer shared, refcount 2
b.append(4)        // now O(n) — b copies the buffer before mutating

Implementing it yourself — this is a common P1 follow-up:

struct Bitmap {
    private final class Storage {
        var pixels: [UInt8]
        init(_ pixels: [UInt8]) { self.pixels = pixels }
        func copy() -> Storage { Storage(pixels) }
    }
    private var storage: Storage

    init(pixels: [UInt8]) { storage = Storage(pixels) }

    var pixels: [UInt8] { storage.pixels }

    mutating func setPixel(_ index: Int, _ value: UInt8) {
        // The key line: only copy if someone else holds this buffer.
        if !isKnownUniquelyReferenced(&storage) {
            storage = storage.copy()
        }
        storage.pixels[index] = value
    }
}

isKnownUniquelyReferenced(_:) returns true when the refcount is exactly 1. It only works on class references and only when passed inout. Note it returns false for Objective-C classes, so COW boxes must be pure Swift.

Gotcha they love: capturing the array in a closure, or holding a second copy anywhere, silently turns every mutation into a full copy — a classic cause of "why is my loop O(n²)". This is why array[i] += 1 inside a loop is fine but let snapshot = array held elsewhere can wreck performance.

P0

Explain optionals, and every way to unwrap one

An Optional<Wrapped> is an enum with two cases:

enum Optional<Wrapped> { case none, some(Wrapped) }

String? is sugar for Optional<String>; nil is .none. That is the whole thing — there is no null pointer, it is an ordinary generic enum with compiler sugar.

let raw: String? = "42"

// 1. if let / guard let (shadowing shorthand since Swift 5.7)
if let raw { print(raw.count) }
guard let raw else { return }

// 2. Nil-coalescing
let value = raw ?? "default"

// 3. Optional chaining — result is itself optional
let count: Int? = raw?.count

// 4. switch / pattern matching
switch raw {
case .some(let s) where s.isEmpty: print("empty")
case .some(let s):                 print(s)
case .none:                        print("nil")
}

// 5. map / flatMap — transform without unwrapping
let n: Int?  = raw.flatMap(Int.init)   // Int? not Int??
let up: String? = raw.map { $0.uppercased() }

// 6. Force unwrap — crashes on nil
let forced = raw!

// 7. Implicitly unwrapped optional — for @IBOutlet and two-phase init only
var label: UILabel!

When force-unwrapping is defensible: @IBOutlets after viewDidLoad, resources you ship in the bundle (UIImage(named:)! for an asset you control), and programmer-error invariants where crashing is better than silent corruption. Everywhere else it's a crash waiting for a bad server response. A good line to say: "I allow ! where nil means the build is broken, never where nil means the network was."

Follow-up — what is try? vs try!? try? converts a throw into nil (discarding the error, which is usually a smell); try! traps on throw.

P0

Difference between map, compactMap, flatMap, filter, reduce

let strings = ["1", "two", "3", "4"]

strings.map { Int($0) }         // [1, nil, 3, 4]        -> [Int?]
strings.compactMap { Int($0) }  // [1, 3, 4]             -> [Int], drops nils
[[1,2],[3,4]].flatMap { $0 }    // [1,2,3,4]             -> flattens one level
strings.filter { $0.count == 1 }// ["1","3","4"]
[1,2,3,4].reduce(0, +)          // 10
[1,2,3].reduce(into: [Int]()) { $0.append($1 * 2) }  // [2,4,6] — avoids copies

Key distinctions interviewers probe:

  • flatMap on a sequence of sequences flattens. flatMap on an Optional chains (returns T? not T??). The sequence-of-optionals overload was deprecated in Swift 4.1 in favour of compactMap precisely because it was ambiguous.
  • reduce(into:) is the performance answer: it passes the accumulator inout so you avoid a copy per element. For building collections it's meaningfully faster.
  • These are all eager. For large or infinite sequences, lazy defers work and fuses the operations:
let first3 = (1...1_000_000).lazy.map { $0 * $0 }.filter { $0 % 3 == 0 }.prefix(3)
// only evaluates until 3 elements are found
P0

What are closures, and explain capture semantics

A closure is a self-contained block of functionality that captures references to variables from its defining scope. Functions are closures with names.

func makeCounter() -> () -> Int {
    var count = 0                 // captured by reference, boxed on the heap
    return { count += 1; return count }
}
let next = makeCounter()
next(); next()   // 1, 2 — `count` outlives makeCounter

Capture lists control what and how you capture:

var x = 10
let byReference = { print(x) }        // sees later changes
let byValue     = { [x] in print(x) } // captures 10 at closure creation
x = 20
byReference()   // 20
byValue()       // 10

Escaping vs non-escaping. A closure parameter is non-escaping by default: the compiler guarantees it does not outlive the function call, so it can be stack-allocated and you need no self.. @escaping means it may be stored or called later — it is retained, so capturing self creates a strong reference that can become a cycle.

final class Loader {
    var completion: (() -> Void)?
    func load(then done: @escaping () -> Void) {
        completion = done               // stored => must be @escaping
    }
    func sync(_ body: () -> Void) { body() }  // non-escaping, no self. needed
}

@autoclosure wraps an expression in a closure automatically so it is evaluated lazily — how ?? and assert avoid evaluating the right-hand side:

func lazyLog(_ message: @autoclosure () -> String, if condition: Bool) {
    if condition { print(message()) }
}
lazyLog(expensiveDescription(), if: isDebug)  // expensive call only runs if needed
P0

Explain weak, unowned, strong and when to use each

  • strong (default): increments the retain count; keeps the object alive.
  • weak: does not retain; is automatically set to nil when the object deallocates; must be var and Optional. Implemented via a side table — the object gets a side table with a list of weak references, so deallocation can zero them. That indirection is why weak access is slightly slower and is not free.
  • unowned: does not retain and is not zeroed. Accessing it after deallocation is a crash (or worse, unowned(unsafe) is undefined behaviour). Can be non-optional and let.
final class Customer {
    var card: CreditCard?
    deinit { print("customer gone") }
}
final class CreditCard {
    unowned let owner: Customer   // a card cannot exist without an owner
    init(owner: Customer) { self.owner = owner }
}

final class ViewController: UIViewController {
    var onDone: (() -> Void)?
    func setup() {
        onDone = { [weak self] in self?.dismiss(animated: true) }
    }
}

Rule I state in interviews: use unowned only when the reference is guaranteed to outlive the holder — a child pointing back at a parent that owns it, or self inside a closure the object itself owns and destroys in deinit. Everywhere else, weak. The practical cost of a wrong unowned is a production crash; the cost of an unnecessary weak is one optional check.

[weak self] + guard let self else { return } is the idiom. Since Swift 5.8 you can write guard let self else { return } without = self.

P0

What is ARC and when does it fail?

Automatic Reference Counting inserts retain/release calls at compile time. When the strong count hits zero, deinit runs and memory is freed. It is deterministic — unlike a tracing GC there is no pause and no collector thread, but also no cycle collector, which is the whole problem.

Retain cycle — the canonical example and its fix:

final class Node {
    var child: Node?
    weak var parent: Node?    // <- breaks the cycle
    deinit { print("dealloc") }
}

// Closure cycle:
final class Timerish {
    var timer: Timer?
    func start() {
        // LEAK: self -> timer -> block -> self
        timer = Timer.scheduledTimer(withTimeInterval: 1, repeats: true) { _ in
            self.tick()
        }
    }
    func fixed() {
        timer = Timer.scheduledTimer(withTimeInterval: 1, repeats: true) { [weak self] _ in
            self?.tick()
        }
    }
    func tick() {}
    deinit { timer?.invalidate() }
}

The four cycles that actually ship in real apps:

  1. Closure stored on self capturing self (completion handlers, sink, Timer).
  2. Delegate declared strong instead of weak.
  3. Parent ↔ child both strong (coordinator holding VC, VC holding coordinator).
  4. NotificationCenter.addObserver(forName:object:queue:using:) block capturing self and never removed.

How I find them: Xcode Memory Graph Debugger (the purple cycle icon), deinit breakpoints / print statements, and Instruments' Leaks + Allocations with "mark generation" to catch abandoned memory (which Leaks will not show, because abandoned memory is still reachable).

P0

Protocols, protocol extensions, and protocol-oriented programming

protocol Identifiable { var id: String { get } }
protocol Persistable: Identifiable {
    func save() throws
    static var storageKey: String { get }
}

extension Persistable {
    static var storageKey: String { String(describing: Self.self) }  // default impl
    func save() throws { print("default save for \(Self.storageKey)") }
}

Protocol extensions give you default implementations and let you add behaviour to any conforming type retroactively. That is the core of POP: compose capability via protocols rather than inherit it via base classes, which avoids the fragile-base-class problem and works with structs and enums.

The dispatch trap they will test you on:

protocol Greeter { func hello() }          // in the protocol requirement list
extension Greeter {
    func hello() { print("protocol hello") }
    func bye()   { print("protocol bye") }  // NOT a requirement
}
struct S: Greeter {
    func hello() { print("S hello") }
    func bye()   { print("S bye") }
}
let g: Greeter = S()
g.hello()   // "S hello"      — witness table, dynamic dispatch
g.bye()     // "protocol bye" — static dispatch on the *static* type

hello() is a protocol requirement, so it goes through the witness table and dispatches to the concrete type. bye() exists only in the extension, so the call is resolved statically from the declared type Greeter. This bites people constantly. Fix: declare it in the protocol body if you want overriding to work.

P0

some vs any — opaque types vs existentials

protocol Shape { func area() -> Double }

func makeOpaque() -> some Shape { Circle(r: 1) }  // one specific concrete type, hidden
func makeAny()   -> any Shape   { Bool.random() ? Circle(r: 1) : Square(s: 2) }
  • some Shape (opaque): the compiler knows the single underlying concrete type; the caller does not. Static dispatch, no boxing, preserves type identity — some Shape == some Shape compiles when both come from the same function. You cannot return different concrete types from different branches.
  • any Shape (existential): a box holding any conforming value. Requires dynamic dispatch and, for values larger than three words, heap allocation. Allows heterogeneous arrays. Loses type identity.
let shapes: [any Shape] = [Circle(r: 1), Square(s: 2)]   // heterogeneous — needs any
func sum(_ xs: [some Shape]) -> Double { xs.map { $0.area() }.reduce(0,+) } // homogeneous, fast

Where this matters for real: SwiftUI's some View exists to avoid boxing a deeply nested generic view type (VStack<TupleView<(Text, Button<Text>)>>) in every signature. AnyView is the existential escape hatch and is slower, because SwiftUI loses structural identity and re-creates the subtree.

Since Swift 5.7, any P also unlocked protocols with Self/associated-type requirements in existential position, and some P is allowed in parameter position as sugar for a generic.

P0

Generics: constraints, associated types, and type erasure

func firstIndex<C: Collection>(of element: C.Element, in c: C) -> C.Index?
where C.Element: Equatable {
    c.firstIndex(of: element)
}

protocol Repository {
    associatedtype Model: Identifiable
    func fetch(id: Model.ID) async throws -> Model
    func save(_ model: Model) async throws
}

A protocol with an associatedtype (or Self requirement) historically could not be used as a type — you had to erase it. Since Swift 5.7 you can write any Repository, but you still cannot call methods that mention the associated type in a way the compiler can't resolve, so type erasure is still the practical answer:

struct AnyRepository<Model: Identifiable>: Repository {
    private let _fetch: (Model.ID) async throws -> Model
    private let _save:  (Model) async throws -> Void

    init<R: Repository>(_ r: R) where R.Model == Model {
        _fetch = r.fetch
        _save  = r.save
    }
    func fetch(id: Model.ID) async throws -> Model { try await _fetch(id) }
    func save(_ model: Model) async throws { try await _save(model) }
}

This closure-boxing technique is exactly how AnyPublisher, AnyView, AnyHashable, and AnyIterator work. Being able to write it on a whiteboard is a strong senior signal.

Primary associated types (Swift 5.7) removed a lot of the need:

protocol Container<Item> { associatedtype Item; var items: [Item] { get } }
func show(_ c: any Container<String>) { print(c.items) }   // constrained existential
P1

Value semantics with a reference inside — the classic bug

final class Buffer { var data = [Int]() }
struct Wrapper { var buffer = Buffer() }     // struct holding a class!

var w1 = Wrapper()
var w2 = w1                 // "copy"
w2.buffer.data.append(1)
print(w1.buffer.data)       // [1]  — shared! The copy copied the *pointer*

A struct only has value semantics if all of its stored properties do. A struct containing a mutable class is a reference type wearing a costume. This is asked a lot because it happens constantly with cached images, NSMutableAttributedString, or a legacy manager object stored in a model struct.

Fixes: make the inner class immutable (let properties, final), implement copy-on-write around it, or make the inner type a struct.

P1

enum power features: associated values, raw values, indirect, CaseIterable

enum NetworkError: Error, Equatable {
    case offline
    case http(status: Int, body: Data?)          // associated values
    case decoding(underlying: String)
}

enum Plan: String, CaseIterable, Codable {       // raw values + auto Codable
    case free, pro, enterprise
}
Plan.allCases.map(\.rawValue)                     // ["free","pro","enterprise"]

indirect enum Expr {                              // recursion needs indirect (boxes it)
    case value(Int)
    case add(Expr, Expr)
}
func eval(_ e: Expr) -> Int {
    switch e {
    case .value(let v):      return v
    case .add(let l, let r): return eval(l) + eval(r)
    }
}

Why indirect: an enum's size is the size of its largest case; a directly recursive case would be infinitely large. indirect boxes the payload on the heap, breaking the recursion.

@frozen matters for library evolution: a frozen enum promises no new cases, so clients can exhaustively switch without @unknown default. Standard-library enums are frozen; your framework enums should not be unless you mean it.

P1

Error handling: throws, Result, rethrows, typed throws

enum ParseError: Error { case badFormat(line: Int) }

func parse(_ s: String) throws -> Int {
    guard let n = Int(s) else { throw ParseError.badFormat(line: 0) }
    return n
}

do { _ = try parse("x") }
catch let e as ParseError { print(e) }
catch { print("other", error) }

// rethrows: only throws if the closure throws
func transform<T>(_ xs: [String], _ f: (String) throws -> T) rethrows -> [T] {
    try xs.map(f)
}

// Typed throws (Swift 6): the error type is part of the signature
func strictParse(_ s: String) throws(ParseError) -> Int {
    guard let n = Int(s) else { throw .badFormat(line: 0) }
    return n
}

Result vs throws: throws is for synchronous control flow where the caller handles it immediately; Result is for storing an outcome, passing it to a completion handler, or when you need to defer handling. With async/await, Result largely went away — but you still see it for caching outcomes and in Task.result.

let r = Result { try parse("42") }        // Result<Int, Error>
let mapped = r.map(String.init)
try r.get()

defer runs on scope exit in reverse order — the right place for cleanup, unlocking, and closing file handles.

P1

Property wrappers — what they are and how to write one

A property wrapper factors out the get/set logic of a property into a reusable type. @State, @Published, @AppStorage are all property wrappers.

@propertyWrapper
struct UserDefault<Value: Codable> {
    let key: String
    let defaultValue: Value
    var container: UserDefaults = .standard

    var wrappedValue: Value {
        get {
            guard let data = container.data(forKey: key),
                  let v = try? JSONDecoder().decode(Value.self, from: data)
            else { return defaultValue }
            return v
        }
        set {
            container.set(try? JSONEncoder().encode(newValue), forKey: key)
        }
    }

    // $property gives you this
    var projectedValue: Self { self }
}

struct Settings {
    @UserDefault(key: "hasOnboarded", defaultValue: false) var hasOnboarded: Bool
}

The compiler rewrites @UserDefault var hasOnboarded: Bool into a stored _hasOnboarded: UserDefault<Bool> plus a computed hasOnboarded forwarding to wrappedValue. $hasOnboarded maps to projectedValue.

Gotchas: wrappers can't be applied to let, to computed properties, or in protocols; a wrapper on a struct property makes the setter mutating, which is exactly why @State in SwiftUI needs its own storage box.

P1

Result builders (@resultBuilder) — how @ViewBuilder works

@resultBuilder
enum StringBuilder {
    static func buildBlock(_ parts: String...) -> String { parts.joined(separator: " ") }
    static func buildOptional(_ part: String?) -> String { part ?? "" }
    static func buildEither(first: String) -> String { first }
    static func buildEither(second: String) -> String { second }
    static func buildArray(_ parts: [String]) -> String { parts.joined(separator: " ") }
}

@StringBuilder
func greeting(name: String?, formal: Bool) -> String {
    "Hello"
    if let name { name }
    if formal { "— sincerely" } else { "!" }
}

The compiler transforms the statement list into nested buildBlock/buildOptional/buildEither calls. That is exactly why SwiftUI's if inside a ViewBuilder produces _ConditionalContent<A, B> — and therefore why an if changes view identity and resets @State, while .opacity(x ? 1 : 0) does not.

Limit that gets asked: buildBlock historically capped at 10 children — hence "break your view into subviews". Variadic generics in Swift 5.9+ removed the hard cap in newer SDKs, but the advice stands for compile-time reasons.

P1

Access control levels

open > public > package > internal (default) > fileprivate > private.

  • open: public and subclassable/overridable outside the defining module. Only meaningful for classes and their members.
  • public: visible outside the module, but not subclassable/overridable outside it.
  • package (Swift 5.9): visible across modules in the same package — the answer to "how do you share code between modules in a monorepo without making it public API". This is a nice P2-flavoured detail to drop.
  • private: the enclosing declaration and its extensions in the same file (that extension carve-out landed in Swift 4 and surprises people).
  • fileprivate: the whole file.
P1

mutating, inout, and nonmutating

struct Counter {
    private(set) var value = 0
    mutating func increment() { value += 1 }   // implicitly reassigns self
}

func bump(_ x: inout Int) { x += 1 }
var n = 0; bump(&n)                            // copy-in, copy-out (not a pointer)

inout is not pass-by-reference in the C sense; it is copy-in / copy-out, with the optimiser sometimes using in-place mutation. That's why you cannot pass the same variable to two inout parameters (exclusive access), and why capturing an inout parameter in an escaping closure is illegal.

Exclusive access to memory (Simultaneous accesses to ... but modification requires exclusive access) is the runtime error people hit with array.append(array.count) in older Swift or with nested inout.

P1

static vs class members, and final

class Base {
    static func s() {}          // cannot be overridden
    class func c() {}           // CAN be overridden by subclasses
    final func f() {}           // static dispatch, cannot be overridden
}

static on a class is final class. final enables static dispatch (devirtualisation) and is a cheap performance win — and, more importantly for interviews, final class + immutable let properties is the recipe for a Sendable class in Swift 6.

Dispatch table you should be able to recite:

ConstructDispatch
struct / enum methodsstatic
final class methodsstatic
non-final class methodsdynamic (vtable)
protocol requirementsdynamic (witness table)
protocol extension-only methodsstatic
@objc dynamicdynamic (objc_msgSend)
extensions on classesstatic (cannot be overridden)
P2

Macros (Swift 5.9+) — @Observable, #Preview, and writing one

Macros run at compile time and expand into ordinary Swift. Two kinds:

  • Freestanding: #warning, #Preview, #expect (Swift Testing).
  • Attached: @Observable, @Model (SwiftData), @Test.
@attached(member, names: named(description))
@attached(extension, conformances: CustomStringConvertible)
public macro Describable() = #externalMacro(module: "MyMacros", type: "DescribableMacro")

The implementation lives in a separate SwiftSyntax-based target that the compiler runs as a process. In interviews, you rarely have to write one — but you should know: macros are hygienic, fully expandable in Xcode ("Expand Macro"), and they are why @Observable can rewrite every stored property into a computed one that calls access(keyPath:) / withMutation(keyPath:).

P2

Key paths and dynamic member lookup

struct User { let name: String; var age: Int }

let kp = \User.name                    // KeyPath<User, String>
let wkp = \User.age                    // WritableKeyPath<User, Int>
users.map(\.name)                      // key path as a function
users.sorted(using: KeyPathComparator(\.age))

@dynamicMemberLookup
struct Box<T> {
    var value: T
    subscript<U>(dynamicMember kp: KeyPath<T, U>) -> U { value[keyPath: kp] }
}
Box(value: User(name: "a", age: 3)).name   // "a"

Key path flavours: KeyPath (read), WritableKeyPath (value types, mutable), ReferenceWritableKeyPath (classes), PartialKeyPath (root known, value erased), AnyKeyPath (both erased). \.self is the identity key path.

P2

Equatable, Hashable, Comparable, Identifiable — correct implementations

struct Money: Hashable, Comparable {
    let amount: Decimal
    let currency: String

    static func == (l: Money, r: Money) -> Bool {
        l.currency == r.currency && l.amount == r.amount
    }
    func hash(into hasher: inout Hasher) {
        hasher.combine(currency); hasher.combine(amount)
    }
    static func < (l: Money, r: Money) -> Bool {
        precondition(l.currency == r.currency)
        return l.amount < r.amount
    }
}

The contract: if a == b then a.hashValue == b.hashValue. Violating it breaks Set and Dictionary silently — elements go missing. Never hash a mutable property and then mutate it while the value is in a set.

Synthesis is free when every stored property conforms — but not for classes (you must write == yourself, and decide whether it's identity or value equality) and not when you have a property you want excluded.

Identifiable just needs var id: Hashable. In SwiftUI, using array index as id is the classic bug that makes rows animate wrong on insertion — always use a stable model ID.

P2

Codable in depth

struct Article: Codable {
    let id: UUID
    let title: String
    let publishedAt: Date
    let tags: [String]
    let authorName: String

    enum CodingKeys: String, CodingKey {
        case id, title, tags
        case publishedAt = "published_at"
        case author
    }
    enum AuthorKeys: String, CodingKey { case name }

    init(from decoder: Decoder) throws {
        let c = try decoder.container(keyedBy: CodingKeys.self)
        id          = try c.decode(UUID.self, forKey: .id)
        title       = try c.decode(String.self, forKey: .title)
        publishedAt = try c.decode(Date.self, forKey: .publishedAt)
        tags        = try c.decodeIfPresent([String].self, forKey: .tags) ?? []
        // nested container — flatten author.name into authorName
        let a = try c.nestedContainer(keyedBy: AuthorKeys.self, forKey: .author)
        authorName  = try a.decode(String.self, forKey: .name)
    }
}

let dec = JSONDecoder()
dec.keyDecodingStrategy = .convertFromSnakeCase   // alternative to manual CodingKeys
dec.dateDecodingStrategy = .iso8601

Things they test:

  • decodeIfPresent vs optional property: an optional property already tolerates a missing key, but decodeIfPresent also tolerates explicit null in some shapes — and gives you a place to apply a default.
  • Decoding heterogeneous arrays: decode a type discriminator first, then switch.
  • keyDecodingStrategy = .convertFromSnakeCase conflicts with manual CodingKeys — the strategy runs first, so your keys must be the converted form. This trips people up.
  • Making decoding resilient so one bad element doesn't kill the whole array:
struct Lossy<T: Decodable>: Decodable {
    let elements: [T]
    init(from decoder: Decoder) throws {
        var c = try decoder.unkeyedContainer()
        var out = [T]()
        while !c.isAtEnd {
            if let v = try? c.decode(T.self) { out.append(v) }
            else { _ = try? c.decode(AnyDecodable.self) }  // skip the bad one
        }
        elements = out
    }
}
P2

String is a collection of Character — why indexing is O(n)

Swift String stores UTF-8 and exposes extended grapheme clusters (Character), which are variable-width. So there is no O(1) integer subscript — you must walk from an index.

let s = "héllo👨‍👩‍👧"
s.count                      // 6 — the family emoji is ONE Character (ZWJ sequence)
s.utf8.count                 // 24
s.utf16.count                // 12  <- what NSString / NSRange use
let i = s.index(s.startIndex, offsetBy: 2)
s[i]

Where this bites in real apps: NSRange from UITextFieldDelegate and NSAttributedString is UTF-16-based, so you must convert:

func textField(_ tf: UITextField, shouldChangeCharactersIn range: NSRange,
               replacementString string: String) -> Bool {
    guard let text = tf.text, let r = Range(range, in: text) else { return true }
    let updated = text.replacingCharacters(in: r, with: string)
    return updated.count <= 10
}
P2

lazy properties, computed properties, and didSet/willSet

final class Screen {
    lazy var formatter: DateFormatter = {          // created on first access
        let f = DateFormatter(); f.dateStyle = .medium; return f
    }()

    var items: [Item] = [] {
        willSet { print("about to set \(newValue.count)") }
        didSet  { if items != oldValue { tableView.reloadData() } }
    }

    var isEmpty: Bool { items.isEmpty }            // computed — no storage
}

Facts they check: lazy is not thread-safe (two threads can both initialise it); lazy cannot be let; observers don't fire during init or from inside didSet of the same property (no infinite loop); a computed property has no observers; and lazy var in a struct makes the getter mutating.

P3

Memory layout, existential containers, and MemoryLayout

MemoryLayout<Int>.size          // 8
MemoryLayout<Bool>.size         // 1  (stride 1)
MemoryLayout<(Int, Bool)>.size  // 9, stride 16, alignment 8

An existential (any P) is a 5-word container: 3 words of inline value buffer, 1 metadata pointer, 1 witness table pointer. Values larger than 3 words get boxed on the heap — the "existential boxing" cost you mention when arguing for some over any in hot paths.

P3

~Copyable / noncopyable types and ownership (borrowing, consuming)

struct FileHandleWrapper: ~Copyable {
    private let fd: Int32
    init(path: String) throws { fd = open(path, O_RDONLY) }
    consuming func close() { Foundation.close(fd) }   // consumes self
    deinit { Foundation.close(fd) }
}

func read(_ h: borrowing FileHandleWrapper) { /* no retain, no copy */ }

~Copyable gives you linear types — a value that cannot be duplicated, enforcing "close exactly once" at compile time. borrowing takes a read-only loan without retain/release; consuming takes ownership and ends the caller's use. Swift 6.4 adds borrow/mutate accessors for computed properties so large values aren't copied on access. This is a strong P3 to know exists.

P3

@dynamicCallable, @objc dynamic, and method swizzling

class Analytics: NSObject {
    @objc dynamic func track(_ e: String) { print("real", e) }
}
// Swizzling (Obj-C runtime) — used by analytics/APM SDKs, almost never in app code
let orig = class_getInstanceMethod(Analytics.self, #selector(Analytics.track(_:)))!
let swiz = class_getInstanceMethod(Analytics.self, #selector(Analytics.swizzled(_:)))!
method_exchangeImplementations(orig, swiz)

dynamic forces objc_msgSend dispatch, which is what makes swizzling and KVO work. Say clearly in an interview that swizzling is a last resort — it breaks on subclassing, ordering is undefined across libraries, and it is a common source of "works in debug, crashes in release".

P3

Mirror and reflection

let m = Mirror(reflecting: user)
for child in m.children { print(child.label ?? "", child.value) }

Read-only, slow, and loses type information — fine for debug descriptions and test helpers, never for production logic. Interviewers ask this to see if you'll reach for reflection when a protocol would do.

P3

Operator overloading and custom operators

infix operator ±: AdditionPrecedence
func ± (l: Double, r: Double) -> ClosedRange<Double> { (l - r)...(l + r) }

extension CGPoint {
    static func + (l: CGPoint, r: CGPoint) -> CGPoint { .init(x: l.x + r.x, y: l.y + r.y) }
    static func += (l: inout CGPoint, r: CGPoint) { l = l + r }
}

Fine for maths types; the correct interview answer is that custom operators hurt readability everywhere else.

2

Memory Management & ARC

14 questions
P0

Walk me through what happens in memory when a view controller is pushed and popped

This is the single most common "do you actually understand ARC" question. Answer it as a lifecycle story.

On pushViewController(vc, animated:), the navigation controller appends the VC to viewControllers, taking a strong reference. The VC's view is loaded lazily on first access (loadView → viewDidLoad), and the VC holds its view strongly; the view holds its subviews strongly; each subview holds a weak superview pointer back. Outlets are weak by convention because the view hierarchy already owns them — except top-level nib objects, which must be strong.

On pop, the navigation controller removes the VC from viewControllers, dropping its strong reference. If nothing else retains the VC, its retain count hits zero, deinit runs, it releases its view, and the whole subtree deallocates.

It does not deallocate when: a closure stored on the VC captured self strongly; a Timer is scheduled with a strong target and never invalidated; a NotificationCenter block observer captured self; a child object holds a strong delegate back-pointer; a Combine AnyCancellable stored on self has a sink capturing self; or a Task stored on self captures self and never finishes.

How I prove it in review: put deinit { print("💀 \(Self.self)") } in every VC/view model during development. If the print doesn't appear on pop, there's a leak. This one habit catches most of them.

P0

Retain cycle in a closure — show the leak and every fix

final class FeedViewModel {
    private var cancellables = Set<AnyCancellable>()
    private let service: FeedService
    var onUpdate: (([Post]) -> Void)?

    init(service: FeedService) { self.service = service }

    // LEAK 1 — stored closure captures self strongly
    func brokenObserve() {
        service.publisher
            .sink { posts in self.handle(posts) }      // self -> cancellables -> sink -> self
            .store(in: &cancellables)
    }

    // FIX A — weak capture (most common)
    func fixedWeak() {
        service.publisher
            .sink { [weak self] posts in self?.handle(posts) }
            .store(in: &cancellables)
    }

    // FIX B — capture only what you need, never self
    func fixedCaptureLess() {
        let handler = onUpdate
        service.publisher
            .sink { posts in handler?(posts) }
            .store(in: &cancellables)
    }

    // FIX C — unowned, only if the closure's lifetime is strictly bounded by self
    func fixedUnowned() {
        service.publisher
            .sink { [unowned self] posts in handle(posts) }
            .store(in: &cancellables)
    }

    func handle(_ posts: [Post]) {}
    deinit { print("💀 FeedViewModel") }
}

Important nuance most candidates miss: a closure passed to a non-escaping parameter, or one that runs and is released immediately (e.g. URLSession's completion handler after the task completes), does not create a permanent cycle. [weak self] in a one-shot dataTask completion isn't fixing a leak — it's controlling whether the VC is kept alive until the request finishes, which is a policy choice, not a correctness one. Saying this out loud is a strong senior signal.

P0

weak vs unowned — when does unowned actually crash?

final class Owner { var pet: Pet?; deinit { print("owner gone") } }
final class Pet { unowned let owner: Owner; init(owner: Owner) { self.owner = owner } }

var owner: Owner? = Owner()
let pet = Pet(owner: owner!)
owner = nil            // Owner deallocates
_ = pet.owner          // 💥 EXC_BAD_ACCESS / "Fatal error: Attempted to read an
                       //    unowned reference but object was already deallocated"

unowned (the safe default, unowned(safe)) keeps an unowned reference count so the runtime can detect use-after-free and trap with a clear message rather than corrupt memory. unowned(unsafe) is the raw pointer version — undefined behaviour, never use it.

Cost comparison to state: weak requires a side table lookup and atomic load on every access, plus zeroing work at dealloc. unowned is cheaper. In a hot loop that matters; in ordinary UI code it does not, so prefer weak for safety.

P0

What's the difference between a memory leak and abandoned memory?

A leak is memory the app can no longer reach — no pointer to it exists — typically a retain cycle. Instruments' Leaks instrument finds these.

Abandoned memory is still reachable but will never be used again: a cache that grows forever, an array of view controllers you keep "just in case", images held after a screen is dismissed, NotificationCenter observers accumulating. Leaks will not report it, because it's reachable. You find it with Allocations + Mark Generation: mark a generation, do a push/pop cycle several times, mark again — anything that grows monotonically across identical cycles is abandoned.

Saying "Leaks won't find abandoned memory, I use generation marking" is the answer that lands.

P1

autoreleasepool — when do you still need it in Swift?

Autorelease pools exist because Objective-C methods that return objects (+[NSString stringWithFormat:], UIImage(contentsOfFile:), most NSData/NSString bridged APIs) return autoreleased objects — they are released when the pool drains, which for the main thread is at the end of the run loop iteration.

Pure Swift code doesn't autorelease, but bridged Foundation APIs do. So a tight loop over thousands of files can spike memory hugely:

for path in tenThousandPaths {
    autoreleasepool {
        let image = UIImage(contentsOfFile: path)!
        let thumb = image.preparingThumbnail(of: CGSize(width: 100, height: 100))
        try? thumb?.pngData()?.write(to: outputURL(for: path))
    }   // pool drains each iteration — memory stays flat
}

Also relevant: threads you create yourself with Thread/pthread have no pool by default; DispatchQueue and OperationQueue wrap each work item in one.

P1

How do you debug a memory issue in a real app?

Give a process, not a tool list:

  1. Reproduce and measure. Xcode's Debug Navigator memory gauge while repeating the suspect flow ten times. Flat = fine; staircase = abandoned; sawtooth-but-rising = leak plus churn.
  2. Memory Graph Debugger (the ⌥⌘ purple icon). Filter to your module, look for objects that should be gone. Xcode draws cycles with a purple ! badge and shows the exact retaining chain.
  3. deinit breakpoints on the suspect classes.
  4. Instruments → Leaks for true cycles; Allocations with Mark Generation for abandoned memory; VM Tracker for dirty/compressed memory when images or Metal buffers are involved.
  5. malloc stack logging ("All Allocation and Free History") to get the allocation backtrace for a specific address.
  6. For the "app gets killed" case: MetricKit's MXAppExitMetric and the Jetsam event reports in Console tell you the memory footprint at kill time.

Common culprits I name: un-downsampled UIImage (a 4000×3000 JPEG is 48 MB decoded, not 2 MB), URLCache defaults, NSCache with no cost limits, unbounded [String: UIImage] dictionaries, retained AVPlayerItems, and Core Data contexts that never reset.

P1

Why is weak var delegate weak, and what breaks if it isn't?

protocol DownloaderDelegate: AnyObject { func didFinish() }

final class Downloader {
    weak var delegate: DownloaderDelegate?   // AnyObject constraint required for weak
}

The parent typically owns the child (vc.downloader = Downloader()), and the child points back at the parent as delegate. Strong both ways = cycle, neither deallocates. weak requires the protocol be class-bound (: AnyObject), because you can't weakly reference a value type.

Follow-up they ask: "When is a strong delegate correct?" When the delegate is genuinely owned by the object and nothing else holds it — e.g. UIViewPropertyAnimator's completion, or a data source object created solely for one table view. Be explicit about the ownership graph.

P1

Do Task and async let create retain cycles?

They can. An unstructured Task captures self strongly until it finishes:

final class Screen {
    private var task: Task<Void, Never>?

    func load() {
        task = Task {                       // captures self strongly
            let data = await fetch()
            self.apply(data)
        }
    }
    deinit { task?.cancel() }               // cancel, don't just drop it
}

This is not a permanent cycle if the task completes — it's a temporary extension of lifetime. It becomes a real problem with infinite loops (for await ... in stream), because the task never finishes. Fixes: [weak self] inside, cancel in deinit, or use .task {} in SwiftUI which is tied to view lifetime and auto-cancels.

Note: Task { [weak self] in ... } is legal and often correct; async let is structured and scoped to the function, so it can't outlive it.

P2

Image memory: UIImage size vs decoded size, and downsampling

A 12 MP photo is roughly 3 MB on disk as JPEG but 48 MB in memory once decoded (4032 × 3024 × 4 bytes). Loading 20 of them into a collection view is an instant jetsam kill.

func downsample(imageAt url: URL, to pointSize: CGSize, scale: CGFloat) -> UIImage? {
    let srcOptions = [kCGImageSourceShouldCache: false] as CFDictionary
    guard let src = CGImageSourceCreateWithURL(url as CFURL, srcOptions) else { return nil }
    let maxPixel = max(pointSize.width, pointSize.height) * scale
    let opts = [
        kCGImageSourceCreateThumbnailFromImageAlways: true,
        kCGImageSourceShouldCacheImmediately: true,        // decode now, off the main thread
        kCGImageSourceCreateThumbnailWithTransform: true,
        kCGImageSourceThumbnailMaxPixelSize: maxPixel
    ] as CFDictionary
    guard let cg = CGImageSourceCreateThumbnailAtIndex(src, 0, opts) else { return nil }
    return UIImage(cgImage: cg)
}

Modern alternative: UIImage.preparingThumbnail(of:) / byPreparingThumbnail(of:completionHandler:) and prepareForDisplay() which force-decodes off the main thread. Mentioning decode cost on the main thread causing scroll hitches is the detail interviewers want.

P2

NSCache vs Dictionary vs URLCache

NSCache is thread-safe, evicts under memory pressure automatically, holds strong references to values but doesn't prevent purging, and doesn't copy keys (unlike NSDictionary). It is the right default for an in-memory image cache.

final class ImageCache {
    private let cache = NSCache<NSString, UIImage>()
    init() {
        cache.countLimit = 200
        cache.totalCostLimit = 100 * 1024 * 1024    // 100 MB
        NotificationCenter.default.addObserver(
            forName: UIApplication.didReceiveMemoryWarningNotification,
            object: nil, queue: .main) { [cache] _ in cache.removeAllObjects() }
    }
    subscript(key: String) -> UIImage? {
        get { cache.object(forKey: key as NSString) }
        set {
            guard let newValue else { return cache.removeObject(forKey: key as NSString) }
            let cost = newValue.cgImage.map { $0.bytesPerRow * $0.height } ?? 0
            cache.setObject(newValue, forKey: key as NSString, cost: cost)
        }
    }
}

URLCache is the HTTP-layer cache and respects Cache-Control; it's what you configure on URLSessionConfiguration for free response caching. A Dictionary gives you no eviction and no thread safety — fine only for bounded, short-lived data.

P2

What is the memory footprint limit of an iOS app?

There is no single documented number — the limit is device- and state-dependent, enforced by Jetsam. Rough figures worth quoting: foreground apps on modern devices can reach 2–3 GB on high-RAM iPhones but far less on older ones; app extensions are far tighter (a share extension is typically capped around 120 MB, a notification service extension around 24 MB). That extension limit is a classic gotcha question — it's why you downsample images in a share extension rather than loading them.

os_proc_available_memory() gives you remaining headroom at runtime.

P2

Explain deinit ordering and what you must not do in it

deinit runs when the last strong reference goes, on whatever thread released it — which is not guaranteed to be the main thread. Rules:

  • Don't touch UIKit in deinit unless you're sure of the thread (and you usually aren't).
  • Subclass deinit runs before superclass deinit.
  • You can't resurrect self — storing self somewhere in deinit is undefined.
  • Structs and enums have no deinit (except ~Copyable structs, which do).
  • deinit on a @MainActor class is nonisolated — you cannot access main-actor state from it without hopping, which is a Swift 6 error people hit while migrating.

Good use: invalidating timers, removing observers (unnecessary since iOS 9 for the selector-based API, still necessary for block-based observers), closing file handles, cancelling tasks.

P3

Retain count in practice — why CFGetRetainCount lies

Interviewers occasionally ask "how do you check the retain count?". The correct answer is: you don't. CFGetRetainCount exists but is meaningless in Swift — the compiler elides retains/releases, uses guaranteed calling conventions, and the value includes transient +1s from the caller. Use the Memory Graph Debugger's "retain chain" instead, which shows who holds the object, which is the question you actually have.

P3

Tagged pointers and small-string optimisation

Small NSNumbers, short NSStrings, and NSDates can be encoded directly in the pointer value (tagged pointers) — no allocation, no refcounting. Swift's native String has a similar trick: strings up to 15 UTF-8 bytes are stored inline in the struct with no heap buffer. Relevant when someone asks why String is 16 bytes, or why creating millions of short strings isn't as slow as expected.

3

Concurrency (GCD, async/await, Actors, Swift 6)

26 questions
P0

GCD fundamentals: queues, sync vs async, serial vs concurrent, QoS

// Serial — one task at a time, FIFO
let serial = DispatchQueue(label: "com.app.serial")

// Concurrent — many tasks in parallel
let concurrent = DispatchQueue(label: "com.app.concurrent", attributes: .concurrent)

// Global concurrent queues by quality of service
DispatchQueue.global(qos: .userInitiated).async { ... }

// Main queue — serial, bound to the main thread
DispatchQueue.main.async { self.label.text = "done" }

sync vs async describes whether the calling thread waits. serial vs concurrent describes whether the queue runs one or many items at once. They're orthogonal — a very common confusion.

QoS levels, highest to lowest: .userInteractive (animation, main-thread-ish work), .userInitiated (user is waiting, < a few seconds), .default, .utility (progress bar, seconds to minutes), .background (user isn't aware — sync, prefetch), .unspecified. Choosing too high wastes battery and can cause thermal throttling; too low and the system deprioritises you. GCD also does priority inversion avoidance by temporarily raising a low-QoS queue's priority when a high-QoS item waits on it.

P0

What causes a deadlock with DispatchQueue.main.sync?

// On the main thread:
DispatchQueue.main.sync { print("never runs") }   // 💥 deadlock

sync blocks the current thread until the block finishes. The block is scheduled on the main queue, but the main thread is blocked waiting — so the block can never start. Instant deadlock.

Same applies to any serial queue calling sync on itself:

let q = DispatchQueue(label: "s")
q.async { q.sync { } }   // 💥 deadlock

The safe pattern when you need "run on main, now if already there":

func onMain(_ work: @escaping () -> Void) {
    if Thread.isMainThread { work() } else { DispatchQueue.main.async(execute: work) }
}

Note DispatchQueue.main.async from the main thread is fine — it just defers to the next run loop pass.

P0

Implement a thread-safe array (the barrier question)

final class ThreadSafeArray<Element> {
    private var storage: [Element] = []
    private let queue = DispatchQueue(label: "tsa", attributes: .concurrent)

    var count: Int { queue.sync { storage.count } }              // concurrent reads

    func append(_ e: Element) {
        queue.async(flags: .barrier) { self.storage.append(e) }  // exclusive write
    }
    subscript(i: Int) -> Element { queue.sync { storage[i] } }
}

This is the readers–writer pattern: reads run concurrently via sync; writes use a .barrier flag, which makes the queue wait for all in-flight items, run the barrier alone, then resume concurrency.

The Swift 6 answer — say this too, it shows you're current:

actor SafeArray<Element> {
    private var storage: [Element] = []
    var count: Int { storage.count }
    func append(_ e: Element) { storage.append(e) }
    func element(at i: Int) -> Element { storage[i] }
}

Other primitives to name: NSLock / NSRecursiveLock, os_unfair_lock (fastest, but must not be copied — wrap it in a class or use OSAllocatedUnfairLock), DispatchSemaphore, and Mutex from the Synchronization module (Swift 6, Mutex<[Element]> — Sendable and non-blocking-friendly).

import Synchronization
let counter = Mutex<Int>(0)
counter.withLock { $0 += 1 }
P0

DispatchGroup vs DispatchSemaphore — and what replaced them

// Wait for N parallel requests (pre-async/await)
let group = DispatchGroup()
var results = [String: Data]()
let lock = NSLock()

for url in urls {
    group.enter()
    URLSession.shared.dataTask(with: url) { data, _, _ in
        defer { group.leave() }
        guard let data else { return }
        lock.lock(); results[url.absoluteString] = data; lock.unlock()
    }.resume()
}
group.notify(queue: .main) { print("all done", results.count) }

DispatchSemaphore is a counting semaphore — use it to limit concurrency (DispatchSemaphore(value: 4) = max 4 in flight) or to bridge async → sync (which you should almost never do; it blocks a thread and can deadlock the cooperative pool).

The modern replacement is a task group:

func loadAll(_ urls: [URL]) async throws -> [URL: Data] {
    try await withThrowingTaskGroup(of: (URL, Data).self) { group in
        for url in urls {
            group.addTask { (url, try await URLSession.shared.data(from: url).0) }
        }
        var out = [URL: Data]()
        for try await (url, data) in group { out[url] = data }
        return out
    }
}

Never use DispatchSemaphore to wait on an async function. It blocks a thread from the cooperative pool, which has a fixed width equal to the core count — enough of them and you deadlock the entire concurrency runtime. This is a genuine interview trap.

P0

Explain async/await — what actually happens at a suspension point?

await marks a potential suspension point. When the function suspends, it does not block a thread: the current continuation is saved, the thread is returned to the cooperative pool to do other work, and when the awaited operation completes the continuation is resumed — possibly on a different thread.

Consequences you must state:

  1. No thread blocking — one thread can service many suspended async functions. This is why async/await scales better than GCD's "one blocked thread per wait".
  2. State can change across await. Anything you read before the suspension may be stale after it. Re-check invariants.
  3. The thread may change, so thread-local storage and Thread.isMainThread assumptions break. Actor isolation, not thread identity, is what guarantees correctness.
  4. Async functions are not automatically concurrent. Sequential awaits run one after another. To get parallelism you need async let or a task group.
// Sequential — 3 seconds if each takes 1
let a = await fetchA(); let b = await fetchB(); let c = await fetchC()

// Parallel — ~1 second
async let x = fetchA()
async let y = fetchB()
async let z = fetchC()
let (a2, b2, c2) = await (x, y, z)
P0

Structured vs unstructured concurrency

Structured: async let and task groups. Child tasks are scoped to the parent; the parent cannot return until they finish; cancellation propagates down automatically; errors propagate up; priority is inherited. The scope is visible in the code.

Unstructured: Task { } and Task.detached { }. They escape the scope, must be cancelled manually, and you hold a Task handle to await or cancel them.

// Structured — cancellation and errors handled for you
func thumbnails(for ids: [String]) async throws -> [UIImage] {
    try await withThrowingTaskGroup(of: UIImage.self) { group in
        for id in ids { group.addTask { try await self.thumbnail(id) } }
        return try await group.reduce(into: []) { $0.append($1) }
    }
}

// Unstructured — inherits actor + priority from context
let t = Task { await refresh() }
t.cancel()

// Detached — inherits NOTHING: no actor, no priority, no task-locals
Task.detached(priority: .background) { await warmCaches() }

When Task.detached is right: almost never in app code. Use it when you genuinely must escape the current actor's isolation and priority — e.g. fire-and-forget background cleanup from a @MainActor context where inheritance would wrongly pin it to main. Say that you prefer Task { } plus explicit @concurrent/nonisolated functions.

P0

Task cancellation — how do you do it correctly?

Cancellation in Swift is cooperative: calling cancel() sets a flag. Nothing stops unless the code checks.

func process(_ items: [Item]) async throws -> [Result] {
    var out = [Result]()
    for item in items {
        try Task.checkCancellation()          // throws CancellationError
        // or: if Task.isCancelled { return out }   — for partial results
        out.append(try await transform(item))
    }
    return out
}
  • Task.checkCancellation() throws; Task.isCancelled just reads the flag. Use the latter when you want to return partial work or clean up.
  • Most system async APIs (URLSession.data, Task.sleep) check cancellation and throw CancellationError/URLError.cancelled for you.
  • Cancellation propagates to child tasks automatically, not to unstructured ones.
  • withTaskCancellationHandler(operation:onCancel:) lets you cancel a non-async resource:
func download(_ url: URL) async throws -> Data {
    let task = URLSession.shared.dataTask(with: url)
    return try await withTaskCancellationHandler {
        try await withCheckedThrowingContinuation { cont in
            // ... set up completion, resume(cont)
            task.resume()
        }
    } onCancel: {
        task.cancel()          // note: this closure is @Sendable and runs immediately
    }
}

Interview trap: Task { } inside a SwiftUI button action is not cancelled when the view disappears. .task { } is.

P0

What is an actor, and what is actor reentrancy?

An actor protects its mutable state by serialising access: only one task executes on the actor at a time, and all cross-actor access is awaited.

actor ImageDownloader {
    private var cache: [URL: UIImage] = [:]

    func image(for url: URL) async throws -> UIImage {
        if let cached = cache[url] { return cached }
        let (data, _) = try await URLSession.shared.data(from: url)   // ⚠️ suspension
        guard let image = UIImage(data: data) else { throw Err.decode }
        cache[url] = image
        return image
    }
}

Reentrancy: while this actor is suspended at the await, other calls can enter. Two simultaneous calls for the same URL both miss the cache and both download. The actor prevented a data race, but not redundant work — actors are not locks held across suspension.

The standard fix — coalesce in-flight work:

actor ImageDownloader {
    private enum Entry { case inProgress(Task<UIImage, Error>), ready(UIImage) }
    private var cache: [URL: Entry] = [:]

    func image(for url: URL) async throws -> UIImage {
        if let entry = cache[url] {
            switch entry {
            case .ready(let img):   return img
            case .inProgress(let t): return try await t.value    // join the existing download
            }
        }
        let task = Task<UIImage, Error> {
            let (data, _) = try await URLSession.shared.data(from: url)
            guard let img = UIImage(data: data) else { throw Err.decode }
            return img
        }
        cache[url] = .inProgress(task)
        do {
            let img = try await task.value
            cache[url] = .ready(img)
            return img
        } catch {
            cache[url] = nil          // don't cache failures
            throw error
        }
    }
}

This exact question — "two callers, same URL, what happens?" — is asked constantly. Being able to write the inProgress cache is a differentiator.

P0

@MainActor — what does it actually do?

@MainActor is a global actor whose executor is the main dispatch queue. Applying it to a type, function, or property means all access is isolated to the main thread, enforced at compile time.

@MainActor
final class ProfileViewModel: ObservableObject {
    @Published private(set) var user: User?

    func load() async {                 // runs on MainActor
        let fetched = await api.user()  // api is nonisolated -> hops off and back
        user = fetched                  // safe: back on main
    }

    nonisolated func trackImpression() { analytics.log("profile") }  // opt out
}

Key points:

  • @MainActor on a class isolates all its members (with nonisolated as the opt-out).
  • MainActor.run { } hops imperatively; MainActor.assumeIsolated { } (iOS 17+) asserts you're already there without a hop — use it in delegate callbacks documented as main-thread.
  • @MainActor on a protocol requirement propagates to conformances.
  • deinit is always nonisolated.

Replacement for DispatchQueue.main.async: in async code, just call a @MainActor function. Don't sprinkle MainActor.run — it's a code smell that means your isolation boundaries are in the wrong place.

P0

Sendable — what is it and how do you satisfy it?

Sendable marks a type as safe to pass across concurrency domains (actor/task boundaries). The compiler checks it.

struct Point: Sendable { let x, y: Double }             // implicit for value types
                                                         // with Sendable members
final class Config: Sendable {                           // class: must be final +
    let baseURL: URL                                     // all lets + Sendable members
    init(baseURL: URL) { self.baseURL = baseURL }
}

final class LegacyCache: @unchecked Sendable {           // "trust me, I lock"
    private var storage = [String: Data]()
    private let lock = NSLock()
    func value(_ k: String) -> Data? { lock.withLock { storage[k] } }
}

enum Direction: Sendable { case up, down }               // implicit
@MainActor final class VM { }                            // global-actor-isolated types
                                                         // are implicitly Sendable

Rules to recite:

  • Structs/enums: implicitly Sendable if all members are, and the type isn't public (public types must declare it explicitly).
  • Classes: final with only immutable Sendable stored properties, or isolated to a global actor, or @unchecked Sendable with your own synchronisation.
  • Actors are always Sendable.
  • Closures: @Sendable closures may only capture Sendable values.
  • sending (Swift 6) is the better tool for "I hand this over and stop using it" — it transfers a non-Sendable value across an isolation boundary safely, backed by region-based isolation analysis.
func handOff(_ buffer: sending NonSendableBuffer) async { await store(buffer) }
P0

Swift 6 strict concurrency — what changed and how do you migrate?

In Swift 6 language mode, data-race safety errors are errors, not warnings. The migration story every team is living through:

  1. Stay in Swift 5 mode, turn on SWIFT_STRICT_CONCURRENCY = minimal → targeted → complete and fix warnings incrementally.
  2. Annotate your UI layer @MainActor (or set Default Actor Isolation = MainActor in Xcode 26 — see the next question).
  3. Make your models Sendable (usually free: they're structs).
  4. Push @unchecked Sendable only where you own the locking, and write a comment explaining the invariant.
  5. Wrap legacy delegate/callback APIs in continuations or AsyncStream.
  6. Flip the target to Swift 6 language mode module by module.

The three errors you will see most:

  • "Capture of 'self' with non-sendable type ... in a @Sendable closure" → isolate the type or capture only Sendable values.
  • "Main actor-isolated property cannot be referenced from a nonisolated context" → mark the caller @MainActor or await it.
  • "Type does not conform to the 'Sendable' protocol" on a third-party type → @preconcurrency import the module to downgrade it while you wait for the vendor.
@preconcurrency import SomeOldSDK     // suppresses Sendable errors from that module
P1

Swift 6.2 "Approachable Concurrency" — nonisolated(nonsending), @concurrent, default MainActor isolation

This is the newest high-value topic and a strong signal you're current.

The problem it solved: before 6.2, a nonisolated async function always hopped to the global concurrent executor, even when called from @MainActor. That meant innocuous-looking helper functions silently jumped threads and required their parameters to be Sendable.

nonisolated(nonsending) (SE-0461): with the Approachable Concurrency build setting on, nonisolated async functions now run on the caller's actor by default. No surprise hop, no Sendable requirement on arguments.

// With approachable concurrency ON:
nonisolated func parse(_ data: Data) async throws -> Model { ... }
// called from @MainActor -> runs on MainActor. Called from an actor -> runs there.

@concurrent                     // explicit opt-out: run off the caller's actor
func compress(_ data: Data) async -> Data { ... }

Default Actor Isolation = MainActor (Xcode 26 build setting): every declaration in the module is implicitly @MainActor unless marked otherwise. New app projects default to this. The mental model becomes: your app is single-threaded until you deliberately say @concurrent or nonisolated. That is a genuinely better default for UI apps — the compiler stops fighting you, and the places that need real concurrency become explicit and reviewable.

The build settings by name: SWIFT_APPROACHABLE_CONCURRENCY (Approachable Concurrency) and SWIFT_DEFAULT_ACTOR_ISOLATION (MainActor / nonisolated). Individual upcoming-feature flags: NonisolatedNonsendingByDefault, InferIsolatedConformances, InferSendableFromCaptures, GlobalActorIsolatedTypesUsability, DisableOutwardActorInference.

Trade-off to state: turning on default MainActor isolation for a library is usually wrong (you'd force main-thread isolation onto consumers); for an app target it's usually right.

P1

AsyncSequence and AsyncStream — bridging callbacks to async

// AsyncStream from a delegate / notification / callback API
func locationUpdates(_ manager: CLLocationManager) -> AsyncStream<CLLocation> {
    AsyncStream { continuation in
        let delegate = LocationDelegate { location in
            continuation.yield(location)
        }
        manager.delegate = delegate
        manager.startUpdatingLocation()
        continuation.onTermination = { @Sendable _ in
            manager.stopUpdatingLocation()
        }
    }
}

for await location in locationUpdates(manager) {
    updateMap(location)
}

Buffering policy matters and is a great detail to mention:

AsyncStream<Int>(bufferingPolicy: .bufferingNewest(1)) { c in ... }
// .unbounded (default, can grow forever), .bufferingOldest(n), .bufferingNewest(n)

Writing your own AsyncSequence:

struct Countdown: AsyncSequence {
    typealias Element = Int
    let start: Int
    struct Iterator: AsyncIteratorProtocol {
        var current: Int
        mutating func next() async -> Int? {
            guard current > 0 else { return nil }
            try? await Task.sleep(for: .seconds(1))
            defer { current -= 1 }
            return current
        }
    }
    func makeAsyncIterator() -> Iterator { Iterator(current: start) }
}

AsyncStream has no failure type; AsyncThrowingStream does. Note an AsyncStream supports only one consumer — for multicast you need AsyncChannel from swift-async-algorithms, or a broadcast wrapper.

P1

Continuations — wrapping legacy callback APIs

func legacyFetch(id: String, completion: @escaping (Result<User, Error>) -> Void) { }

func fetch(id: String) async throws -> User {
    try await withCheckedThrowingContinuation { continuation in
        legacyFetch(id: id) { result in
            continuation.resume(with: result)
        }
    }
}

The three rules you must state:

  1. Resume exactly once. Zero times = the task hangs forever. Twice = crash (SWIFT_TASK_CONTINUATION_MISUSE).
  2. withCheckedContinuation traps on misuse and is the right default — the "checked" overhead is trivial. withUnsafeContinuation skips the check; use only after profiling proves it matters.
  3. Continuations are not cancellation-aware — pair with withTaskCancellationHandler if the underlying work is cancellable.

The classic bug: a completion handler with multiple early-return paths where one path forgets to call the completion — under callbacks it was a silent hang in one screen; under continuations it's a permanently suspended task.

P1

Task priority, TaskLocal, and TaskPriority escalation

enum Trace {
    @TaskLocal static var requestID: String?
}

await Trace.$requestID.withValue(UUID().uuidString) {
    await performRequest()      // and every child task sees Trace.requestID
}

@TaskLocal values propagate to child tasks (structured and Task { }, but not Task.detached). This is how distributed tracing and logging context work.

Priority escalation: if a high-priority task awaits a low-priority one, the runtime raises the low one's priority to avoid inversion.

P1

How do you run something on a background thread and update the UI?

Give all three eras — interviewers often ask "and how would you do it the old way?"

// 1. GCD
DispatchQueue.global(qos: .userInitiated).async {
    let result = expensive()
    DispatchQueue.main.async { self.label.text = result }
}

// 2. OperationQueue
let q = OperationQueue(); q.qualityOfService = .userInitiated
q.addOperation { let r = expensive(); OperationQueue.main.addOperation { self.label.text = r } }

// 3. Swift concurrency (preferred)
@MainActor func update() async {
    let result = await compute()      // @concurrent / nonisolated -> off main
    label.text = result               // back on main automatically
}
@concurrent func compute() async -> String { expensive() }

The key line: "with structured concurrency I don't hop manually — I put isolation on the types and let the compiler place the hops."

P1

OperationQueue / Operation — when is it still the right tool?

Operation gives you things GCD doesn't: dependencies, cancellation, maxConcurrentOperationCount, KVO-observable state, and priority per operation.

final class DownloadOperation: Operation, @unchecked Sendable {
    private var task: URLSessionDataTask?
    private var _executing = false, _finished = false
    override var isAsynchronous: Bool { true }
    override var isExecuting: Bool { _executing }
    override var isFinished: Bool { _finished }

    override func start() {
        guard !isCancelled else { return finish() }
        willChangeValue(forKey: "isExecuting"); _executing = true
        didChangeValue(forKey: "isExecuting")
        task = URLSession.shared.dataTask(with: url) { [weak self] _,_,_ in self?.finish() }
        task?.resume()
    }
    override func cancel() { super.cancel(); task?.cancel() }
    private func finish() { /* KVO transitions to finished */ }
}

let queue = OperationQueue()
queue.maxConcurrentOperationCount = 3
let a = DownloadOperation(), b = ProcessOperation()
b.addDependency(a)
queue.addOperations([a, b], waitUntilFinished: false)

Honest modern answer: task groups + withTaskGroup concurrency limiting cover most cases now, but Operation is still the cleanest fit for a dependency graph of long-running work, and you'll meet it in every legacy codebase.

Limiting concurrency with a task group (the modern equivalent of maxConcurrentOperationCount):

func loadLimited(_ urls: [URL], limit: Int = 4) async throws -> [Data] {
    try await withThrowingTaskGroup(of: Data.self) { group in
        var iterator = urls.makeIterator()
        var results = [Data]()
        for _ in 0..<min(limit, urls.count) {
            if let u = iterator.next() { group.addTask { try await fetch(u) } }
        }
        while let d = try await group.next() {
            results.append(d)
            if let u = iterator.next() { group.addTask { try await fetch(u) } }
        }
        return results
    }
}
P1

Combine vs async/await — when would you still use Combine?

Combine is for streams of values over time with rich operators; async/await is for a single value, eventually.

// Debounced search — Combine's sweet spot
$searchText
    .debounce(for: .milliseconds(300), scheduler: DispatchQueue.main)
    .removeDuplicates()
    .flatMap { [api] query in
        api.search(query).catch { _ in Just([]) }    // flatMapLatest via .switchToLatest
    }
    .receive(on: DispatchQueue.main)
    .assign(to: &$results)
// Same thing with async/await — cancel the previous task
@Observable final class SearchModel {
    var query = "" { didSet { scheduleSearch() } }
    private(set) var results: [Item] = []
    private var searchTask: Task<Void, Never>?

    private func scheduleSearch() {
        searchTask?.cancel()
        searchTask = Task { [query] in
            try? await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(300))   // debounce
            guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
            results = (try? await api.search(query)) ?? []
        }
    }
}

Say this: Apple has clearly moved investment to Swift Concurrency; @Observable replaced ObservableObject+@Published in new SwiftUI code. I keep Combine where it earns its place — debounce/throttle, combineLatest of several UI inputs, and existing codebases — and use AsyncAlgorithms (.debounce, .throttle, .combineLatest) when I want the same operators in async land.

P2

@Observable and concurrency: why ObservableObject + @Published was replaced

ObservableObject sends objectWillChange for any property change, so every observing view re-renders even if it reads none of the changed properties. @Observable (the Observation framework) tracks which key paths a view actually read during body evaluation, and only invalidates views that read a changed property.

@Observable
final class Cart {
    var items: [Item] = []
    var promoCode: String = ""      // a view showing only `items` will NOT re-render
}                                   // when promoCode changes

Also: @Observable works with plain let in a view for non-owned instances, needs @State for ownership, has no @Published boilerplate, and supports nested observation through class properties. Concurrency angle: @Observable classes are usually @MainActor-isolated in practice, and Observations / withObservationTracking gives you an async stream of changes.

P2

What is a data race vs a race condition?

A data race is two threads accessing the same memory with at least one write and no synchronisation — undefined behaviour at the memory model level. Swift 6's whole point is eliminating these at compile time.

A race condition is a logic bug where behaviour depends on timing — e.g. two awaits where the second result arrives first and overwrites the newer data. Actors eliminate data races but not race conditions; that's the reentrancy problem again.

// Race condition despite full actor safety:
func search(_ q: String) async {
    let results = await api.search(q)    // slow request for "a" finishes AFTER "ab"
    self.results = results               // stale results win
}
// Fix: version token
private var generation = 0
func search(_ q: String) async {
    generation += 1
    let g = generation
    let results = await api.search(q)
    guard g == generation else { return }
    self.results = results
}
P2

Thread Sanitizer, Main Thread Checker, and how you catch concurrency bugs

  • Thread Sanitizer (TSan): Scheme → Diagnostics → Thread Sanitizer. Detects real data races at runtime with ~5–15× slowdown. Not compatible with Address Sanitizer simultaneously. Run your UI test suite under it in CI nightly.
  • Main Thread Checker: on by default in debug; catches UIKit/AppKit calls off the main thread.
  • dispatchPrecondition(condition: .onQueue(.main)) and MainActor.assertIsolated() for explicit assertions.
  • Swift 6 strict concurrency shifts most of this to compile time, which is the real win.
P2

What is the cooperative thread pool and why does thread explosion matter?

Swift Concurrency uses a fixed-width cooperative pool — one thread per CPU core. Tasks never block threads (they suspend), so you can have thousands of tasks on four threads.

GCD, by contrast, spawns a new thread when all current threads are blocked — hence thread explosion: 60+ threads, each with an 512 KB stack, massive context-switch overhead, and eventual deadlock at GCD's 64-thread limit per queue.

// Thread explosion — DON'T
for i in 0..<100 {
    DispatchQueue.global().async { semaphore.wait(); work(i); semaphore.signal() }
}

Corollary you should state: never block inside a Swift concurrency task (sema.wait(), queue.sync, Thread.sleep, or synchronous file I/O in a hot path). Blocking starves the pool. Use await Task.sleep, and push genuinely blocking C APIs onto a dedicated DispatchQueue behind a continuation.

P2

Global actors — writing your own

@globalActor
actor DatabaseActor {
    static let shared = DatabaseActor()
}

@DatabaseActor
final class Store {
    private var connection: SQLiteConnection!
    func write(_ row: Row) { connection.insert(row) }
}

@DatabaseActor func migrate() { }
