A global actor gives you a process-wide serial execution context you can annotate anything with — useful for "all database work happens on one serial context" without threading a single instance everywhere. Custom executors (SerialExecutor, unownedExecutor) let you back an actor with a specific DispatchQueue, which is the bridge for legacy code that must run on a known queue.

P3

isolated parameters and #isolation

func mutate(on actor: isolated SomeActor) { actor.count += 1 }   // no await needed inside

func generic(isolation: isolated (any Actor)? = #isolation) async { }

isolated parameters let a free function run inside an actor's isolation domain. #isolation (Swift 6) captures the caller's isolation, which is how library authors write helpers that inherit isolation properly — you'll see it in AsyncStream.makeStream and in swift-async-algorithms.

P3

Atomic and the Synchronization module

import Synchronization

let counter = Atomic<Int>(0)
counter.wrappingAdd(1, ordering: .relaxed)
let value = counter.load(ordering: .acquiring)

let mutex = Mutex<[String: Int]>([:])
mutex.withLock { $0["key", default: 0] += 1 }

Atomic and Mutex (Swift 6) are Sendable, non-copyable (~Copyable), and don't require a class box the way os_unfair_lock did. Mutex.withLock is non-async — it must not be used to guard an await. The right answer to "how do I share a counter across tasks without an actor" in 2026 is Atomic or Mutex.

P3

Actor custom executors and assumeIsolated

final class QueueExecutor: SerialExecutor {
    let queue = DispatchQueue(label: "legacy")
    func enqueue(_ job: consuming ExecutorJob) {
        let job = UnownedJob(job)
        queue.async { job.runSynchronously(on: self.asUnownedSerialExecutor()) }
    }
    func asUnownedSerialExecutor() -> UnownedSerialExecutor { UnownedSerialExecutor(ordinary: self) }
}

actor LegacyBridge {
    private let executor = QueueExecutor()
    nonisolated var unownedExecutor: UnownedSerialExecutor { executor.asUnownedSerialExecutor() }
}

Use case: an actor that must run on the same DispatchQueue as an existing C/Obj-C library. Rarely needed, excellent to know.

// assumeIsolated: no hop, asserts you're already on the actor
func delegateCallbackKnownOnMain() {
    MainActor.assumeIsolated { self.label.text = "updated" }
}
4

UIKit

22 questions
P0

The full UIViewController lifecycle, in order, with what belongs where

init(coder:) / init(nibName:bundle:)
  ↓
loadView()                 — create self.view (only if not using storyboard/nib)
  ↓
viewDidLoad()              — ONCE. Outlets are connected. One-time setup.
  ↓
viewWillAppear(_:)         — EVERY time. Update data that may have changed.
  ↓
viewIsAppearing(_:)        — iOS 17+. View added to hierarchy, trait collection &
                             geometry are final. Best place for scroll-position work.
  ↓
viewWillLayoutSubviews()   — bounds are about to change
  ↓
    [ Auto Layout engine resolves constraints ]
  ↓
viewDidLayoutSubviews()    — frames are final. Layer-based work (corner radius on a
                             dynamic frame, gradient frames, masks) goes here.
  ↓
viewDidAppear(_:)          — on screen, transition finished. Start animations,
                             analytics "screen viewed", begin video playback.
  ↓
viewWillDisappear(_:) → viewDidDisappear(_:)
  ↓
deinit

What goes where — the part they're actually testing:

  • viewDidLoad: register cells, configure delegates, add subviews, set up constraints, one-time styling. Never assume final frame sizes here — bounds are still the nib/storyboard values.
  • viewWillAppear: refresh data that could have changed while you were away, deselect the table row, update the nav bar. Called on every appearance including returning from a push.
  • viewDidLayoutSubviews: anything frame-dependent — layer.cornerRadius is fine anywhere, but CAGradientLayer.frame, UIBezierPath masks, and layer.shadowPath must be set here because they don't auto-resize. It can be called many times, so make it idempotent and cheap.
  • viewDidAppear: start expensive/animated work; present another VC (presenting in viewWillAppear is a classic bug).

Trap: viewWillAppear is not called when the app returns from background — that's sceneDidBecomeActive / UIApplication.willEnterForegroundNotification. Candidates get this wrong constantly.

P0

frame vs bounds vs center

  • frame: the view's rectangle in its superview's coordinate system. Changing it moves/resizes the view.
  • bounds: the view's rectangle in its own coordinate system. origin is usually .zero — except in UIScrollView, where scrolling changes bounds.origin, which is literally how scrolling works.
  • center: the frame's midpoint in the superview's coordinates.

The question they follow with: "What happens to frame when you rotate a view?" Rotating via transform keeps bounds unchanged but makes frame the bounding box of the rotated view — so frame becomes meaningless (and the docs say it's undefined when transform is not identity). Use bounds + center when a transform is applied.

view.transform = CGAffineTransform(rotationAngle: .pi / 4)
// bounds: 100x100 unchanged; frame: ~141x141 bounding box
P0

Explain the layout cycle: setNeedsLayout, layoutIfNeeded, layoutSubviews

UIKit batches layout. Marking a view dirty doesn't lay it out immediately; the run loop does it in the "update cycle" before the next frame is drawn.

CallMeaning
setNeedsLayout()Mark dirty; layout happens later in the cycle. Cheap, can call repeatedly.
layoutIfNeeded()Force layout now, synchronously, if dirty.
layoutSubviews()The override point — never call directly.
setNeedsUpdateConstraints() / updateConstraints()Same pattern for constraint creation. Rarely needed.
setNeedsDisplay() / draw(_:)The drawing equivalent.

Where this matters — animating a constraint change:

heightConstraint.constant = 200
UIView.animate(withDuration: 0.3) {
    self.view.layoutIfNeeded()     // without this, the change snaps instead of animating
}

Because the constraint change only marks the view dirty, the actual frame change happens during the next layout pass. Calling layoutIfNeeded() inside the animation block makes that pass happen inside the animation, so UIKit animates the frames.

Trap: calling layoutIfNeeded() in a loop, or inside layoutSubviews, causes redundant passes and can infinite-loop. Also call layoutIfNeeded() before the animation block if you want to start from a settled state.

P0

Auto Layout: constraints, priorities, hugging, compression resistance

A constraint is a linear equation: item1.attribute1 = multiplier × item2.attribute2 + constant.

NSLayoutConstraint.activate([
    label.leadingAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.layoutMarginsGuide.leadingAnchor),
    label.topAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.safeAreaLayoutGuide.topAnchor, constant: 16),
    label.trailingAnchor.constraint(lessThanOrEqualTo: view.trailingAnchor, constant: -16)
])
label.translatesAutoresizingMaskIntoConstraints = false   // the #1 forgotten line

Priorities run 1–1000. .required = 1000, .defaultHigh = 750, .defaultLow = 250. A constraint below 1000 is a preference the engine breaks if needed.

Content hugging (default 250) = "resist growing larger than my intrinsic size". Compression resistance (default 750) = "resist being squeezed smaller than my intrinsic size". The mnemonic: hugging pulls in, compression resistance pushes out.

Classic scenario they'll give you: two labels side by side, which one truncates? The one with lower compression resistance. Which one stretches? The one with lower hugging priority.

titleLabel.setContentCompressionResistancePriority(.required, for: .horizontal)
subtitleLabel.setContentHuggingPriority(.defaultLow, for: .horizontal)

intrinsicContentSize is how a view tells Auto Layout its natural size — UILabel, UIButton, UIImageView have one; a plain UIView doesn't. Override it plus call invalidateIntrinsicContentSize() when the content changes.

Debugging: "Unable to simultaneously satisfy constraints" in the console → run the printed VFL through the constraint browser, or set a symbolic breakpoint on UIViewAlertForUnsatisfiableConstraints. Also: view.hasAmbiguousLayout, view.exerciseAmbiguityInLayout(), and po view.value(forKey: "_autolayoutTrace") in lldb.

P0

UITableView cell reuse — explain it and the async image bug

func tableView(_ tv: UITableView, cellForRowAt ip: IndexPath) -> UITableViewCell {
    let cell = tv.dequeueReusableCell(withIdentifier: "Cell", for: ip) as! PostCell
    cell.configure(with: posts[ip.row])
    return cell
}

dequeueReusableCell returns a cell from the reuse pool (or creates one if registered). Only ~(visible rows + 2) cells ever exist, no matter how long the list is. prepareForReuse() is where you reset mutable state — image, text, cancel in-flight work, reset accessory.

The bug every interviewer asks about:

// WRONG — cell is reused before the image arrives; wrong image appears
func configure(with post: Post) {
    titleLabel.text = post.title
    ImageLoader.shared.load(post.imageURL) { [weak self] image in
        self?.imageView.image = image      // this cell may now show a different post!
    }
}

Fix — tag the request with the identity it was made for, and cancel on reuse:

final class PostCell: UITableViewCell {
    private var currentURL: URL?
    private var task: Task<Void, Never>?

    func configure(with post: Post) {
        titleLabel.text = post.title
        imageView.image = placeholder
        currentURL = post.imageURL
        task?.cancel()
        task = Task { [weak self] in
            guard let image = try? await ImageLoader.shared.image(for: post.imageURL) else { return }
            guard let self, self.currentURL == post.imageURL else { return }   // identity check
            self.imageView.image = image
        }
    }

    override func prepareForReuse() {
        super.prepareForReuse()
        task?.cancel(); task = nil
        currentURL = nil
        imageView.image = nil
        titleLabel.text = nil
    }
}

Both guards matter: cancellation stops wasted work, the identity check prevents a race where an older request lands after reuse.

P0

Diffable data source and compositional layout

enum Section: Hashable { case pinned, all }

private lazy var dataSource = UICollectionViewDiffableDataSource<Section, Post.ID>(
    collectionView: collectionView
) { [weak self] cv, indexPath, id in
    cv.dequeueConfiguredReusableCell(using: self!.cellRegistration, for: indexPath, item: id)
}

private let cellRegistration = UICollectionView.CellRegistration<UICollectionViewListCell, Post.ID> {
    cell, _, id in
    var content = cell.defaultContentConfiguration()
    content.text = Store.shared.post(id)?.title
    cell.contentConfiguration = content
}

func apply(_ posts: [Post], animated: Bool = true) {
    var snapshot = NSDiffableDataSourceSnapshot<Section, Post.ID>()
    snapshot.appendSections([.pinned, .all])
    snapshot.appendItems(posts.filter(\.isPinned).map(\.id), toSection: .pinned)
    snapshot.appendItems(posts.filter { !$0.isPinned }.map(\.id), toSection: .all)
    dataSource.apply(snapshot, animatingDifferences: animated)
}

Why it's better than reloadData: it computes the diff and animates inserts/deletes/moves correctly, and it eliminates the NSInternalInconsistencyException: invalid number of rows crash caused by the data source and the batch updates disagreeing.

Two details that separate answers:

  1. Use IDs as items, not model structs. If you use the whole struct as the item and a field changes, the diff sees a different item and animates a delete+insert instead of a reload. With IDs plus snapshot.reconfigureItems([id]) (iOS 15+) you get an in-place update with no animation flicker. reconfigureItems reuses the existing cell; reloadItems dequeues a fresh one.
  2. apply() is safe to call from a background thread as long as you're consistent, but calling it from mixed threads causes crashes — pick main and stay there.

Compositional layout:

let layout = UICollectionViewCompositionalLayout { section, env in
    let item = NSCollectionLayoutItem(layoutSize: .init(
        widthDimension: .fractionalWidth(1/3), heightDimension: .fractionalHeight(1)))
    item.contentInsets = .init(top: 4, leading: 4, bottom: 4, trailing: 4)
    let group = NSCollectionLayoutGroup.horizontal(layoutSize: .init(
        widthDimension: .fractionalWidth(1), heightDimension: .absolute(120)),
        subitems: [item])
    let section = NSCollectionLayoutSection(group: group)
    section.orthogonalScrollingBehavior = .continuous     // horizontal carousel in a vertical list
    section.boundarySupplementaryItems = [
        .init(layoutSize: .init(widthDimension: .fractionalWidth(1),
                                heightDimension: .estimated(44)),
              elementKind: UICollectionView.elementKindSectionHeader, alignment: .top)
    ]
    return section
}

Dimensions: .absolute, .fractionalWidth/Height (of the container), .estimated (self-sizing). orthogonalScrollingBehavior is the feature people name when asked "how would you build the App Store home screen".

P0

UITableView vs UICollectionView — which and why?

Modern answer: UICollectionView for almost everything new. Since iOS 14, UICollectionLayoutListConfiguration gives you table-view appearance (including swipe actions, separators, headers) with all the compositional-layout power, so there's rarely a reason to start with UITableView.

var config = UICollectionLayoutListConfiguration(appearance: .insetGrouped)
config.trailingSwipeActionsConfigurationProvider = { indexPath in
    UISwipeActionsConfiguration(actions: [ .init(style: .destructive, title: "Delete") { _,_,done in
        // delete
        done(true)
    }])
}
let layout = UICollectionViewCompositionalLayout.list(using: config)

UITableView is still fine for simple vertical lists and is what you'll find in existing code.

P1

Self-sizing cells — how do you make them work?

tableView.rowHeight = UITableView.automaticDimension
tableView.estimatedRowHeight = 88     // must be non-zero

The requirement: the cell's contentView must have an unbroken chain of constraints from top to bottom so Auto Layout can resolve a height. A missing bottom constraint is the number-one cause of "self-sizing doesn't work".

NSLayoutConstraint.activate([
    title.topAnchor.constraint(equalTo: contentView.topAnchor, constant: 12),
    body.topAnchor.constraint(equalTo: title.bottomAnchor, constant: 8),
    body.bottomAnchor.constraint(equalTo: contentView.bottomAnchor, constant: -12)  // ← the chain
])
body.numberOfLines = 0

Gotchas: a multi-line label needs preferredMaxLayoutWidth set (or a proper trailing constraint, which is the modern way); estimated heights cause scroll-indicator jumpiness, which you fix by caching real heights in willDisplay and returning them from estimatedHeightForRowAt.

For collection views, use .estimated dimensions plus preferredLayoutAttributesFitting if you self-size manually.

P1

Explain the responder chain

Touches and events travel up the chain until something handles them: UIView → superview → … → UIViewController → its view's window → UIWindow → UIApplication → AppDelegate.

override var canBecomeFirstResponder: Bool { true }
override func motionEnded(_ motion: UIEvent.EventSubtype, with event: UIEvent?) { }

// Sending an action to "whoever handles it" — how UIMenuController / Edit menus work
UIApplication.shared.sendAction(#selector(MyProtocol.doThing), to: nil, from: self, for: nil)

Hit testing is the other half and is asked more often:

override func hitTest(_ point: CGPoint, with event: UIEvent?) -> UIView? {
    // Expand the tappable area of a small button
    let expanded = bounds.insetBy(dx: -20, dy: -20)
    return expanded.contains(point) ? self : nil
}

hitTest(_:with:) walks the hierarchy front to back, depth first calling point(inside:with:), and returns the deepest view that contains the point. A view is skipped if isHidden, alpha <= 0.01, or isUserInteractionEnabled == false.

The classic puzzle: "A button is outside its superview's bounds — why doesn't it respond?" Because hitTest on the superview returns nil before it ever asks the button, since the point is outside the superview. Fix by overriding point(inside:) on the superview or using clipsToBounds-independent hit test expansion.

P1

UIView.animate vs UIViewPropertyAnimator vs Core Animation

// 1. Block-based — simplest
UIView.animate(withDuration: 0.3, delay: 0, options: [.curveEaseInOut]) {
    self.box.alpha = 0
} completion: { _ in self.box.removeFromSuperview() }

// 2. Spring
UIView.animate(withDuration: 0.5, delay: 0, usingSpringWithDamping: 0.7,
               initialSpringVelocity: 0.5) { self.box.center.y += 100 }

// 3. UIViewPropertyAnimator — interactive, interruptible, scrubbable
let animator = UIViewPropertyAnimator(duration: 0.4, dampingRatio: 0.8) {
    self.card.transform = .init(translationX: 0, y: -300)
}
animator.addCompletion { position in print(position) }   // .end / .start / .current
animator.startAnimation()
animator.pauseAnimation()
animator.fractionComplete = 0.5          // scrub with a pan gesture
animator.isReversed = true

// 4. Core Animation — layer-level, runs on the render server
let anim = CABasicAnimation(keyPath: "transform.rotation.z")
anim.fromValue = 0; anim.toValue = CGFloat.pi * 2
anim.duration = 1; anim.repeatCount = .infinity
spinner.layer.add(anim, forKey: "spin")

The critical conceptual point: UIView.animate animates model values — the property really changes. CABasicAnimation animates the presentation layer only; the model layer keeps its original value, so the view snaps back at the end unless you set fillMode = .forwards; isRemovedOnCompletion = false (which lies about state) or set the model value yourself. Layer animations also run on the render server process, so they keep going smoothly even if your main thread stutters — which is why CAAnimation-driven loaders are the right choice for spinners.

Which properties are animatable on CALayer: frame(via bounds/position), bounds, position, transform, opacity, backgroundColor, cornerRadius, borderWidth/Color, shadowOpacity/Offset/Radius/Path, path on CAShapeLayer.

P1

CALayer vs UIView, and implicit animations

Every UIView is backed by a CALayer (view.layer) that does the actual drawing and compositing. UIView adds touch handling, responder chain, and Auto Layout on top.

Implicit animations: changing a standalone layer's animatable property animates over 0.25 s automatically. A layer backing a view has its actions disabled by UIView's delegate, so it doesn't. That asymmetry is a favourite question.

let l = CALayer()                  // standalone
l.opacity = 0                      // implicitly animates!
CATransaction.begin()
CATransaction.setDisableActions(true)
l.opacity = 0                      // no animation
CATransaction.commit()

Layer subclasses worth naming: CAShapeLayer (vector paths, animatable path/strokeEnd — how progress rings are built), CAGradientLayer, CATextLayer, CAReplicatorLayer, CAEmitterLayer (particles), CATransformLayer, AVPlayerLayer, CAMetalLayer.

// Progress ring — a classic live-coding ask
let ring = CAShapeLayer()
ring.path = UIBezierPath(arcCenter: center, radius: 40, startAngle: -.pi/2,
                         endAngle: 1.5 * .pi, clockwise: true).cgPath
ring.strokeColor = UIColor.systemBlue.cgColor
ring.fillColor = UIColor.clear.cgColor
ring.lineWidth = 8
ring.lineCap = .round
ring.strokeEnd = 0
let a = CABasicAnimation(keyPath: "strokeEnd")
a.fromValue = 0; a.toValue = 1; a.duration = 2
ring.add(a, forKey: nil)
ring.strokeEnd = 1     // set the model value so it stays
P1

What causes offscreen rendering and how do you fix it?

Offscreen rendering forces the GPU to render into a separate buffer, then composite — expensive, and the main cause of scroll jank in list-heavy apps.

Triggers: layer.masksToBounds/clipsToBounds combined with cornerRadius on a view with content; layer.shadow* without a shadowPath; layer.mask; shouldRasterize; group opacity; UIVisualEffectView; text with certain rendering paths.

// Shadow — always set shadowPath
layer.shadowColor = UIColor.black.cgColor
layer.shadowOpacity = 0.2
layer.shadowRadius = 8
layer.shadowOffset = CGSize(width: 0, height: 4)
layer.shadowPath = UIBezierPath(roundedRect: bounds, cornerRadius: 12).cgPath  // ← key

// Rounded corners without masking the whole subtree
imageView.layer.cornerRadius = 12
imageView.layer.cornerCurve = .continuous     // iOS 13+, the "squircle"
imageView.clipsToBounds = true                // acceptable for a plain image
// For complex content, pre-render a rounded image instead, or use
// layer.cornerRadius on a solid-colour layer only.

Diagnosis: Simulator → Debug → Color Offscreen-Rendered Yellow, Color Blended Layers (red = expensive blending — fix with opaque backgrounds), Color Misaligned Images (purple = non-integral frames causing resampling). Instruments' Animation Hitches / Core Animation template gives you the frame timings.

P1

UIScrollView with Auto Layout — how do you set it up?

The thing candidates get wrong. A scroll view needs two things: constraints that define the content's size (the scrollable area), and constraints that define its position.

scrollView.translatesAutoresizingMaskIntoConstraints = false
contentView.translatesAutoresizingMaskIntoConstraints = false
scrollView.addSubview(contentView)

NSLayoutConstraint.activate([
    // scroll view fills the screen
    scrollView.topAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.safeAreaLayoutGuide.topAnchor),
    scrollView.leadingAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.leadingAnchor),
    scrollView.trailingAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.trailingAnchor),
    scrollView.bottomAnchor.constraint(equalTo: view.bottomAnchor),

    // content pinned to contentLayoutGuide -> defines contentSize
    contentView.topAnchor.constraint(equalTo: scrollView.contentLayoutGuide.topAnchor),
    contentView.leadingAnchor.constraint(equalTo: scrollView.contentLayoutGuide.leadingAnchor),
    contentView.trailingAnchor.constraint(equalTo: scrollView.contentLayoutGuide.trailingAnchor),
    contentView.bottomAnchor.constraint(equalTo: scrollView.contentLayoutGuide.bottomAnchor),

    // width pinned to frameLayoutGuide -> vertical-only scrolling
    contentView.widthAnchor.constraint(equalTo: scrollView.frameLayoutGuide.widthAnchor)
])

contentLayoutGuide = the scrollable content. frameLayoutGuide = the visible window. Pinning width to the frame guide is what makes it scroll vertically only.

Also know: contentInsetAdjustmentBehavior, adjustedContentInset (safe area + your inset), keyboardLayoutGuide (iOS 15+, replaces all the keyboard-notification maths), and contentOffset vs contentInset (offset is where you are; inset is padding around content).

P1

Navigation patterns: push, present, and the Coordinator alternative

navigationController?.pushViewController(detail, animated: true)
present(modal, animated: true)               // modalPresentationStyle: .automatic/.fullScreen/.pageSheet
navigationController?.setViewControllers([root, a, b], animated: true)
navigationController?.popToRootViewController(animated: true)

Details worth knowing: since iOS 13 the default presentation is a sheet (.pageSheet), which is dismissible by swipe — so viewWillDisappear may fire without your dismiss code running. Use isModalInPresentation = true to prevent it, and UIAdaptivePresentationControllerDelegate.presentationControllerDidDismiss to be told.

UISheetPresentationController (iOS 15+) gives you detents:

if let sheet = modal.sheetPresentationController {
    sheet.detents = [.medium(), .large(), .custom { _ in 200 }]
    sheet.prefersGrabberVisible = true
    sheet.largestUndimmedDetentIdentifier = .medium
}

Why Coordinators: view controllers shouldn't know what comes next. See Chapter 6 for the full pattern.

P2

Custom view controller transitions

final class FadeTransition: NSObject, UIViewControllerAnimatedTransitioning {
    func transitionDuration(using ctx: UIViewControllerContextTransitioning?) -> TimeInterval { 0.3 }

    func animateTransition(using ctx: UIViewControllerContextTransitioning) {
        guard let to = ctx.view(forKey: .to) else { return ctx.completeTransition(false) }
        ctx.containerView.addSubview(to)
        to.alpha = 0
        UIView.animate(withDuration: 0.3) { to.alpha = 1 } completion: { _ in
            ctx.completeTransition(!ctx.transitionWasCancelled)   // ← must call this
        }
    }
}

extension MyVC: UIViewControllerTransitioningDelegate {
    func animationController(forPresented p: UIViewController, presenting: UIViewController,
                             source: UIViewController) -> UIViewControllerAnimatedTransitioning? {
        FadeTransition()
    }
}

For interactive (gesture-driven) transitions, add a UIPercentDrivenInteractiveTransition and return it from interactionControllerForPresentation. The must-say detail: always call completeTransition(!transitionWasCancelled), or the app locks up in a half-transitioned state.

P2

UIStackView — what it buys you and its limits

let stack = UIStackView(arrangedSubviews: [icon, label, spacer])
stack.axis = .horizontal
stack.spacing = 8
stack.alignment = .center          // perpendicular axis
stack.distribution = .fill         // along the axis
stack.isLayoutMarginsRelativeArrangement = true
stack.setCustomSpacing(16, after: icon)   // iOS 11+

distribution: .fill (respects hugging/compression), .fillEqually, .fillProportionally (by intrinsic size), .equalSpacing, .equalCentering. alignment: .fill, .leading/.top, .center, .trailing/.bottom, .firstBaseline, .lastBaseline.

Key behaviours: setting isHidden = true on an arranged subview removes it from the layout entirely — that's the main reason to use stack views. arrangedSubviews ≠ subviews; removeArrangedSubview doesn't remove from the hierarchy, you need removeFromSuperview() too (a real bug source).

Limit: deeply nested stack views are slow — Auto Layout solves a bigger system. For a cell that appears 1000 times, hand-rolled constraints or a manual layoutSubviews is faster.

P2

Storyboards vs XIBs vs programmatic UI

The question is really about team scale. Say:

  • Storyboards: fast for prototypes and for seeing flow; terrible merge conflicts, slow to open in big files, hard to reuse, runtime-only errors for bad segues/identifiers.
  • XIBs: one view per file — much better merge story, reusable, still visual.
  • Programmatic: fully reviewable in diffs, compile-time safe, easy to parameterise and test, no as! casts on dequeue. Downside: slower to iterate visually and you must be disciplined about layout code.

My answer: programmatic for shared components and anything in a design system, XIBs for one-off complex layouts a designer iterates on, storyboards only for legacy. And whatever you choose, be consistent — the worst outcome is three styles in one codebase.

P2

How do you support Dynamic Type, Dark Mode, and accessibility?

// Dynamic Type
label.font = UIFont.preferredFont(forTextStyle: .body)
label.adjustsFontForContentSizeCategory = true
// Custom font scaled:
let base = UIFont(name: "Inter-Regular", size: 17)!
label.font = UIFontMetrics(forTextStyle: .body).scaledFont(for: base)

// React to size category change
override func traitCollectionDidChange(_ previous: UITraitCollection?) {
    super.traitCollectionDidChange(previous)
    if traitCollection.preferredContentSizeCategory != previous?.preferredContentSizeCategory {
        rebuildLayout()
    }
}
// iOS 17+ preferred API:
registerForTraitChanges([UITraitPreferredContentSizeCategory.self]) { (self: Self, _) in
    self.rebuildLayout()
}

// Dark Mode — use semantic / dynamic colours, never hardcoded
view.backgroundColor = .systemBackground
let dynamic = UIColor { $0.userInterfaceStyle == .dark ? .white : .black }
// CGColor is NOT dynamic — resolve it on trait change:
layer.borderColor = UIColor.separator.resolvedColor(with: traitCollection).cgColor

// Accessibility
button.accessibilityLabel = "Add to cart"
button.accessibilityHint = "Adds this item to your shopping cart"
button.accessibilityTraits = [.button]
container.isAccessibilityElement = true      // group a composite cell into one element
UIAccessibility.post(notification: .screenChanged, argument: newFocusView)

Naming accessibilityLabel vs accessibilityValue vs accessibilityHint, and "I group composite cells into one accessibility element", is the level of detail that impresses.

P2

UIApplication / SceneDelegate — the multi-window lifecycle

Since iOS 13, UISceneDelegate owns UI lifecycle and UIApplicationDelegate owns process lifecycle.

func scene(_ scene: UIScene, willConnectTo session: UISceneSession,
           options connectionOptions: UIScene.ConnectionOptions) {
    guard let windowScene = scene as? UIWindowScene else { return }
    window = UIWindow(windowScene: windowScene)
    window?.rootViewController = RootViewController()
    window?.makeKeyAndVisible()
    // deep links arrive here on cold start:
    if let url = connectionOptions.urlContexts.first?.url { handle(url) }
}
func sceneDidBecomeActive(_ scene: UIScene) { }   // resume timers, refresh
func sceneWillResignActive(_ scene: UIScene) { }  // pause, e.g. incoming call
func sceneDidEnterBackground(_ scene: UIScene) { } // save state, blur snapshot

Trap: a URL opened while the app is already running goes to scene(_:openURLContexts:), not willConnectTo. You need both paths or deep links break in one of the two states — a very common production bug and a great interview story.

P3

UIBezierPath, CGContext, and custom draw(_:)

override func draw(_ rect: CGRect) {
    guard let ctx = UIGraphicsGetCurrentContext() else { return }
    ctx.setFillColor(UIColor.systemBlue.cgColor)
    let path = UIBezierPath(roundedRect: rect.insetBy(dx: 4, dy: 4), cornerRadius: 12)
    ctx.addPath(path.cgPath)
    ctx.fillPath()
}

Say clearly: overriding draw(_:) allocates a backing store the size of the view and rasterises on the CPU, so it's much more expensive than composing layers. Use it only for genuinely custom drawing (charts, signatures), and prefer CAShapeLayer when the shape is a path. Modern image rendering uses UIGraphicsImageRenderer, not the deprecated UIGraphicsBeginImageContext:

let image = UIGraphicsImageRenderer(size: size).image { ctx in
    UIColor.red.setFill(); ctx.fill(CGRect(origin: .zero, size: size))
}
P3

Gesture recognizers: conflicts, require(toFail:), delegates

let tap = UITapGestureRecognizer(target: self, action: #selector(handleTap))
let doubleTap = UITapGestureRecognizer(target: self, action: #selector(handleDouble))
doubleTap.numberOfTapsRequired = 2
tap.require(toFail: doubleTap)          // single tap waits for double to fail
view.addGestureRecognizer(tap); view.addGestureRecognizer(doubleTap)

extension VC: UIGestureRecognizerDelegate {
    func gestureRecognizer(_ g: UIGestureRecognizer,
        shouldRecognizeSimultaneouslyWith other: UIGestureRecognizer) -> Bool { true }
    func gestureRecognizerShouldBegin(_ g: UIGestureRecognizer) -> Bool {
        // e.g. only start a pan if it's mostly horizontal
        guard let pan = g as? UIPanGestureRecognizer else { return true }
        let v = pan.velocity(in: view)
        return abs(v.x) > abs(v.y)
    }
}

The classic scenario: a pan inside a scroll view. Answer with shouldRecognizeSimultaneouslyWith, gestureRecognizerShouldBegin, and scrollView.panGestureRecognizer interplay.

P3

UIKit + SwiftUI interop

// SwiftUI inside UIKit
let host = UIHostingController(rootView: ProfileView(user: user))
addChild(host)
host.view.translatesAutoresizingMaskIntoConstraints = false
view.addSubview(host.view)
NSLayoutConstraint.activate([ /* pin */ ])
host.didMove(toParent: self)
host.sizingOptions = .intrinsicContentSize     // iOS 16+, self-sizing in a stack/cell

// UIKit inside SwiftUI
struct MapViewRepresentable: UIViewRepresentable {
    @Binding var region: MKCoordinateRegion

    func makeUIView(context: Context) -> MKMapView {
        let map = MKMapView()
        map.delegate = context.coordinator
        return map
    }
    func updateUIView(_ map: MKMapView, context: Context) { map.setRegion(region, animated: true) }
    func makeCoordinator() -> Coordinator { Coordinator(self) }

    final class Coordinator: NSObject, MKMapViewDelegate {
        let parent: MapViewRepresentable
        init(_ parent: MapViewRepresentable) { self.parent = parent }
        func mapView(_ m: MKMapView, regionDidChangeAnimated a: Bool) {
            parent.region = m.region     // careful: can cause update loops
        }
    }
}

The gotcha to name: updateUIView is called on every SwiftUI update, so guard against redundant work and against feedback loops where the coordinator writes a binding that triggers another update.

5

SwiftUI

21 questions
P0

How does SwiftUI actually work? (view identity, the dependency graph, diffing)

A SwiftUI View is not a view — it is a lightweight, immutable description of what should be on screen. SwiftUI builds a tree of these value types, compares it to the previous tree, and updates a separate, persistent render tree of actual drawing primitives.

The loop is: state changes → SwiftUI invalidates the views that depend on that state → body is re-evaluated → the new description is diffed against the old → only the changed render nodes are updated.

Two concepts you must be able to explain:

Identity. SwiftUI tracks views by identity, which is either structural (position in the view tree) or explicit (.id(_:) or the id in a ForEach). Identity determines whether a view is "the same view updated" or "a different view replacing it" — and therefore whether @State is preserved or reset, and whether a transition animates.

// Structural identity change — @State inside DetailView is RESET, and it's a new view
if isEditing { DetailView() } else { DetailView() }

// Same identity, just a modifier change — @State PRESERVED
DetailView().opacity(isEditing ? 1 : 0.5)

// Explicit identity change — deliberately force a fresh view
DetailView().id(selectedUserID)

Lifetime. @State storage lives as long as the identity does, not as long as the struct does. The struct is recreated on every update; the state box is not.

Dependencies. During body evaluation, SwiftUI records which properties you read. With @Observable, that tracking is per-key-path, so a view that reads only model.title is not invalidated when model.subtitle changes. With the older ObservableObject, any @Published change fires objectWillChange and invalidates every observer.

P0

All the property wrappers and when to use each

WrapperOwns storage?Use it for
@StateYesValue-type state owned by this view (and, with @Observable, owned reference types)
@BindingNoA two-way reference to state owned by an ancestor
@Observable + @StateYesModern observable reference model owned by this view
@Observable + plain letNoObservable model passed in and owned elsewhere
@BindableNoGetting $ bindings from an @Observable object you don't own
@StateObjectYesLegacy. ObservableObject owned by this view — created once
@ObservedObjectNoLegacy. ObservableObject owned elsewhere — recreated on every update
@EnvironmentObjectNoLegacy. ObservableObject injected through the environment
@EnvironmentNoSystem values (\.colorScheme, \.dismiss) and injected @Observable objects
@AppStorage / @SceneStorageYesUserDefaults / state restoration
@FocusStateYesKeyboard/field focus
@NamespaceYesmatchedGeometryEffect / zoom transitions
@GestureStateYesTransient gesture state that auto-resets

The single most-asked SwiftUI question: @StateObject vs @ObservedObject.

struct Parent: View {
    @State private var counter = 0
    var body: some View {
        VStack {
            Button("Tick \(counter)") { counter += 1 }
            Child()          // every tick re-creates Child's struct
        }
    }
}

struct Child: View {
    @StateObject  private var vmA = ViewModel()   // created ONCE, survives re-renders
    @ObservedObject private var vmB = ViewModel() // ❌ re-created on EVERY parent update
    var body: some View { Text("\(vmA.value)") }
}

@StateObject initialises its object once per view identity and keeps it alive. @ObservedObject does not own the object — writing @ObservedObject private var vm = ViewModel() means a brand-new view model, with all its state lost, on every parent redraw. That's the bug. @ObservedObject is only correct when the object is passed in from a parent that owns it.

Modern equivalent: use @State private var model = MyObservableModel() for ownership and a plain let model: MyObservableModel (or @Bindable) for injection.

P0

@Observable — the modern model layer, end to end

import Observation

@Observable
final class CartModel {
    var items: [CartItem] = []
    var promoCode: String = ""
    private(set) var isLoading = false

    @ObservationIgnored private let api: CartAPI     // not observed, no invalidation
    init(api: CartAPI) { self.api = api }

    var total: Decimal { items.reduce(0) { $0 + $1.price } }   // computed — tracked via items

    @MainActor
    func refresh() async {
        isLoading = true
        defer { isLoading = false }
        items = (try? await api.fetchCart()) ?? []
    }
}

struct CartScreen: View {
    @State private var model = CartModel(api: .live)   // owned here

    var body: some View {
        List(model.items) { ItemRow(item: $0) }
            .overlay { if model.isLoading { ProgressView() } }
            .task { await model.refresh() }
    }
}

struct PromoField: View {
    @Bindable var model: CartModel                     // injected, needs $ bindings
    var body: some View { TextField("Promo", text: $model.promoCode) }
}

What the macro does: it conforms the class to Observable, wraps every stored property into a computed property whose getter calls access(keyPath:) and whose setter calls withMutation(keyPath:), and adds an ObservationRegistrar. @ObservationIgnored opts a property out.

Wins to name: per-property tracking (fewer re-renders), works with computed properties and nested objects, no @Published boilerplate, works outside SwiftUI via withObservationTracking, and no objectWillChange timing weirdness.

Gotcha: an @Observable class is not automatically Sendable and not automatically @MainActor — annotate it. And putting @Observable on a class you then mark @ObservedObject is a mistake; don't mix the systems.

P0

@Environment vs @EnvironmentObject and custom environment values

// Modern: put an @Observable object in the environment
@main struct App: App {
    @State private var session = SessionModel()
    var body: some Scene { WindowGroup { RootView().environment(session) } }
}
struct RootView: View {
    @Environment(SessionModel.self) private var session
    var body: some View { Text(session.username) }
}

// Custom environment value (iOS 17+ macro style)
extension EnvironmentValues {
    @Entry var theme: Theme = .default
}
SomeView().environment(\.theme, .dark)

// Pre-macro style, still worth knowing
private struct ThemeKey: EnvironmentKey { static let defaultValue = Theme.default }
extension EnvironmentValues {
    var theme: Theme { get { self[ThemeKey.self] } set { self[ThemeKey.self] = newValue } }
}

The trap: @EnvironmentObject (and @Environment(Type.self)) crashes at runtime if nothing injected the object. It's a deliberate fatal error, not an optional. In previews you must inject it explicitly. @Environment(Type.self) private var x: SessionModel? (optional form) is the non-crashing variant.

P0

Why is my SwiftUI view re-rendering too much, and how do you fix it?

Give a diagnosis process, then the fixes.

Diagnose:

var body: some View {
    let _ = Self._printChanges()        // logs which property caused this update
    ...
}

Plus Instruments' SwiftUI template (View Body / View Properties / Core Animation Commits), and the _printChanges output which literally names the changed dependency or says @self / @identity.

Fixes, roughly in order of impact:

  1. Move state down. If only a small subview needs the state, put it there. State at the top of the tree invalidates everything below.
  2. Split big bodys into small views. SwiftUI's invalidation granularity is the view, so smaller views = smaller invalidations.
  3. Use @Observable so only views that read a changed property re-render.
  4. Don't compute in body. Sorting, filtering, and date formatting in body runs on every update. Hoist into the model or cache it.
  5. EquatableView / .equatable() when you can cheaply prove nothing changed.
struct Row: View, Equatable {
    let item: Item
    static func == (l: Row, r: Row) -> Bool { l.item.id == r.item.id && l.item.version == r.item.version }
    var body: some View { ... }
}
Row(item: item).equatable()
  1. Avoid AnyView. It erases structural identity, so SwiftUI can't diff and rebuilds the subtree. Use @ViewBuilder or generics instead.
// ❌
func makeView(_ kind: Kind) -> AnyView {
    kind == .a ? AnyView(A()) : AnyView(B())
}
// ✅
@ViewBuilder func makeView(_ kind: Kind) -> some View {
    switch kind { case .a: A(); case .b: B() }
}
  1. ForEach with stable IDs. ForEach(items.indices, id: \.self) breaks identity on insertion — every row after the insertion point is treated as changed.
  2. LazyVStack/List over VStack in a ScrollView, so offscreen rows aren't built.
  3. .drawingGroup() to flatten an expensive subtree into a single Metal-rendered layer (good for many shapes, bad for anything that needs to stay interactive or crisp text).
P0

SwiftUI layout system — how does a view get its size?

Three steps, in a strict order:

  1. Parent proposes a size to the child (each dimension may be a concrete value, nil = "unspecified/ideal", 0 = "minimum", or .infinity = "maximum").
  2. The child chooses its own size. SwiftUI never forces a size on a child — this is the fundamental difference from Auto Layout.
  3. The parent places the child in its own coordinate space.
Text("Hello")
    .frame(width: 50)          // frame proposes 50 to Text; Text may still overflow/truncate
    .background(.red)          // background gets the size Text chose

Modifier order therefore matters enormously:

Text("Hi").padding().background(.blue)   // blue includes the padding
Text("Hi").background(.blue).padding()   // blue hugs the text, padding outside

frame is a view, not a property. .frame(width:height:) wraps your view in a new container view of that size and centres the child inside. .frame(maxWidth: .infinity) means "propose infinity to me and I'll take all of it", which is how you make things fill.

Layout priority: in an HStack/VStack, the stack offers space to the least flexible child first. .layoutPriority(1) makes a child get offered space earlier.

Custom layout (iOS 16+):

struct FlowLayout: Layout {
    var spacing: CGFloat = 8

    func sizeThatFits(proposal: ProposedViewSize, subviews: Subviews,
                      cache: inout ()) -> CGSize {
        let maxWidth = proposal.width ?? .infinity
        var x: CGFloat = 0, y: CGFloat = 0, rowHeight: CGFloat = 0
        for v in subviews {
            let size = v.sizeThatFits(.unspecified)
            if x + size.width > maxWidth { x = 0; y += rowHeight + spacing; rowHeight = 0 }
            x += size.width + spacing
            rowHeight = max(rowHeight, size.height)
        }
        return CGSize(width: maxWidth, height: y + rowHeight)
    }

    func placeSubviews(in bounds: CGRect, proposal: ProposedViewSize,
                       subviews: Subviews, cache: inout ()) {
        var x = bounds.minX, y = bounds.minY, rowHeight: CGFloat = 0
        for v in subviews {
            let size = v.sizeThatFits(.unspecified)
            if x + size.width > bounds.maxX { x = bounds.minX; y += rowHeight + spacing; rowHeight = 0 }
            v.place(at: CGPoint(x: x, y: y), proposal: ProposedViewSize(size))
            x += size.width + spacing
            rowHeight = max(rowHeight, size.height)
        }
    }
}

A tag-cloud / flow layout is a very common SwiftUI live-coding task, and Layout is the right answer (not GeometryReader hacks).

P1

GeometryReader — why is it a trap and what replaced it?

GeometryReader accepts the full proposed size and makes its content top-leading aligned, which breaks layouts in surprising ways. Nesting them costs an extra layout pass each.

// ❌ GeometryReader takes all available space, collapsing siblings
VStack {
    GeometryReader { _ in Text("Hi") }   // expands to fill
    Text("Below")                        // squeezed
}

Modern replacements:

// Reading a size without affecting layout (iOS 17+)
someView.onGeometryChange(for: CGSize.self) { $0.size } action: { size = $0 }

// Container-relative sizing
someView.containerRelativeFrame(.horizontal) { width, _ in width * 0.8 }

// Visual effects driven by position (iOS 17+) — no GeometryReader needed
card.visualEffect { content, proxy in
    content.scaleEffect(min(1, proxy.frame(in: .scrollView).minY / 300))
}

// Scroll position and phase (iOS 17/18)
ScrollView { ... }
    .scrollPosition(id: $scrolledID)
    .onScrollGeometryChange(for: CGFloat.self) { $0.contentOffset.y } action: { old, new in }

Legitimate remaining uses of GeometryReader: as a .background/.overlay (where it takes the size of the thing it decorates, not the layout), and for genuinely proportional custom drawing.

P1

PreferenceKey — child-to-parent communication

Environment flows down; preferences flow up.

struct HeightKey: PreferenceKey {
    static var defaultValue: CGFloat = 0
    static func reduce(value: inout CGFloat, nextValue: () -> CGFloat) {
        value = max(value, nextValue())
    }
}

struct Child: View {
    var body: some View {
        Text("dynamic")
            .background(GeometryReader { p in
                Color.clear.preference(key: HeightKey.self, value: p.size.height)
            })
    }
}

struct Parent: View {
    @State private var tallest: CGFloat = 0
    var body: some View {
        HStack { Child(); Child() }
            .onPreferenceChange(HeightKey.self) { tallest = $0 }
            .frame(height: tallest)
    }
}

reduce combines values from siblings. This is how navigationTitle, toolbar, and matchedGeometryEffect are implemented internally. Trap: writing a preference and reading it into @State that feeds back into the same layout causes an update loop — "Bound preference ... tried to update multiple times per frame".

P1

Navigation: NavigationStack, value-based navigation, deep links

enum Route: Hashable { case profile(User.ID), settings, post(Post.ID) }

struct RootView: View {
    @State private var path = NavigationPath()          // or [Route] for full control

    var body: some View {
        NavigationStack(path: $path) {
            HomeView()
                .navigationDestination(for: Route.self) { route in
                    switch route {
                    case .profile(let id): ProfileView(id: id)
                    case .settings:        SettingsView()
                    case .post(let id):    PostView(id: id)
                    }
                }
        }
        .onOpenURL { url in
            path = NavigationPath()
            if let route = Route(url: url) { path.append(route) }
        }
    }
}
// Navigate from anywhere by mutating the path — fully testable, no view references

Why value-based navigation matters: the path is plain data, so deep links, state restoration, and "pop to root then push three screens" all become array manipulations. NavigationLink(value:) + navigationDestination(for:) replaced NavigationLink(destination:isActive:), which was deprecated because it eagerly built destinations and couldn't express arbitrary stacks.

Use a typed array ([Route]) rather than NavigationPath when you need to inspect or mutate the stack; NavigationPath is type-erased and supports heterogeneous types.

NavigationSplitView is the iPad/Mac two- or three-column container.

P1

.task vs .onAppear vs .onChange vs .refreshable

List(model.items) { ... }
    .task { await model.load() }                  // async, auto-cancelled on disappear
    .task(id: userID) { await model.load(userID) } // re-runs when id changes
    .onAppear { analytics.log("screen") }          // sync, every appearance
    .onChange(of: query) { old, new in model.filter(new) }   // iOS 17 two-param form
    .refreshable { await model.reload() }           // pull-to-refresh, async
    .onDisappear { player.pause() }

Key points: .task creates a Task tied to view lifetime and cancels it on disappear — that's the main reason to prefer it over onAppear { Task { } }, which leaks work. .task(id:) cancels and restarts when the id changes. .onAppear can fire more than once (e.g. in a TabView or when returning from a push) so it must be idempotent.

P1

Animations: implicit, explicit, transitions, matchedGeometryEffect

// Implicit — animates any change to values this view depends on
Circle().scaleEffect(scale).animation(.spring(duration: 0.4), value: scale)

// Explicit — animates every change caused by this state mutation
withAnimation(.snappy) { isExpanded.toggle() }

// Transitions — how a view enters/leaves the hierarchy
if isShown {
    Card().transition(.asymmetric(insertion: .scale.combined(with: .opacity),
                                  removal: .move(edge: .bottom)))
}

// Matched geometry — hero animation between two views
@Namespace private var ns
if isDetail {
    Image("hero").matchedGeometryEffect(id: "hero", in: ns)
} else {
    Image("hero").frame(width: 60, height: 60).matchedGeometryEffect(id: "hero", in: ns)
}

// Phase and keyframe animations (iOS 17+)
Image(systemName: "bell")
    .phaseAnimator([0, -20, 20, 0], trigger: notificationCount) { view, angle in
        view.rotationEffect(.degrees(angle))
    }

Critical detail: the deprecated .animation(_:) without a value: animated everything in the subtree including unrelated changes — always use the value: form. And a transition only runs when the view's identity appears/disappears inside a withAnimation block or an animated container.

Custom animatable value:

struct CounterText: View, Animatable {
    var value: Double
    var animatableData: Double { get { value } set { value = newValue } }
    var body: some View { Text("\(Int(value))") }
}
P1

Lists: performance, LazyVStack, sections, swipe actions

List {
    Section("Pinned") {
        ForEach(model.pinned) { PostRow(post: $0) }
    }
    Section("All") {
        ForEach(model.all) { post in
            PostRow(post: post)
                .swipeActions(edge: .trailing) {
                    Button(role: .destructive) { model.delete(post) } label: { Label("Delete", systemImage: "trash") }
                }
                .onAppear { if post == model.all.last { Task { await model.loadNextPage() } } }
        }
    }
}
.listStyle(.insetGrouped)
.searchable(text: $query)

Performance rules for lists:

  • Stable Identifiable IDs, never array indices.
  • Keep row body cheap — no date formatters, no sorting, no image decoding.
  • .id() on rows only when you want to destroy state.
  • ScrollView { LazyVStack { … } } when you need full visual control; List when you want system behaviours (separators, swipe, selection, keyboard nav) and cell reuse.
  • List on iOS is backed by UICollectionView, so it reuses rows; LazyVStack only lazily creates views, it does not reuse or discard them once created, so a very long LazyVStack grows memory. That distinction is a great answer.
  • Pagination: trigger on .onAppear of a row near the end, guard against re-entry with a loading flag.
P1

How do you test SwiftUI views and view models?

Test the model, not the pixels, plus a thin layer of view smoke tests.

import Testing

@Suite @MainActor
struct CartModelTests {
    @Test func totalSumsItems() {
        let model = CartModel(api: MockCartAPI(items: [.init(price: 10), .init(price: 5)]))
        model.items = [.init(price: 10), .init(price: 5)]
        #expect(model.total == 15)
    }

    @Test func refreshPopulatesItems() async {
        let model = CartModel(api: MockCartAPI(items: [.sample]))
        await model.refresh()
        #expect(model.items.count == 1)
        #expect(model.isLoading == false)
    }
}

For the view layer: ViewInspector (third-party) to assert structure, snapshot testing (swift-snapshot-testing) for visual regressions, and XCUITest for flows. Since Xcode 26 there is also better support for evaluating view bodies in tests. The key interview point: an @Observable view model is a plain class — it's trivially unit-testable, which is exactly why you keep logic out of body.

P2

Liquid Glass (iOS 26) — the new design system in SwiftUI

Worth knowing in 2026 because every interviewer has just shipped an iOS 26 redesign.

// Basic glass material
Text("Hello")
    .padding()
    .glassEffect()                                  // .regular, capsule by default
    .glassEffect(.clear.tint(.blue).interactive(), in: .rect(cornerRadius: 16))

// Group elements so they morph together
GlassEffectContainer(spacing: 24) {
    HStack {
        Button { } label: { Image(systemName: "pencil") }.glassEffect()
        Button { } label: { Image(systemName: "eraser") }.glassEffect()
    }
}

// Morphing between states
@Namespace private var ns
Button("Toggle") { }.glassEffect().glassEffectID("toggle", in: ns)

// Buttons
Button("Cancel") { }.buttonStyle(.glass)
Button("Save")   { }.buttonStyle(.glassProminent)

// Tab bar behaviours
TabView {
    Tab("Home", systemImage: "house") { HomeView() }
    Tab("Search", systemImage: "magnifyingglass", role: .search) { SearchView() }
}
.tabBarMinimizeBehavior(.onScrollDown)
.tabViewBottomAccessory { NowPlayingBar() }

// Toolbar
.toolbar {
    ToolbarItem(placement: .confirmationAction) { Button("Done") { } }
    ToolbarSpacer(.flexible)
}

The answer that shows judgement: Liquid Glass is a system material — the right adoption strategy is to let standard controls (toolbars, tab bars, sheets) pick it up for free by building against the iOS 26 SDK, and apply .glassEffect() sparingly to floating custom controls. Over-applying it to content surfaces hurts legibility. Always check accessibilityReduceTransparency and respect it; the system handles most adaptation, but custom glass on custom backgrounds needs manual contrast checks.

UIKit equivalent: UIGlassEffect(glass: .regular, isInteractive: true) in a UIVisualEffectView.

P2

SwiftUI + UIKit interop, both directions

// UIViewControllerRepresentable — e.g. a photo picker or a legacy screen
struct PickerView: UIViewControllerRepresentable {
    @Binding var image: UIImage?
    @Environment(\.dismiss) private var dismiss

    func makeUIViewController(context: Context) -> PHPickerViewController {
        var config = PHPickerConfiguration(); config.filter = .images
        let vc = PHPickerViewController(configuration: config)
        vc.delegate = context.coordinator
        return vc
    }
    func updateUIViewController(_ vc: PHPickerViewController, context: Context) { }
    func makeCoordinator() -> Coordinator { Coordinator(self) }

    final class Coordinator: NSObject, PHPickerViewControllerDelegate {
        let parent: PickerView
        init(_ p: PickerView) { parent = p }
        func picker(_ picker: PHPickerViewController, didFinishPicking results: [PHPickerResult]) {
            defer { parent.dismiss() }
            guard let provider = results.first?.itemProvider,
                  provider.canLoadObject(ofClass: UIImage.self) else { return }
            provider.loadObject(ofClass: UIImage.self) { obj, _ in
                Task { @MainActor in self.parent.image = obj as? UIImage }
            }
        }
    }
}

Lifecycle mapping to state clearly: makeUIView(Controller) is called once per identity; updateUIView(Controller) on every SwiftUI update; makeCoordinator once, and the coordinator is the right place for delegates and stored state; dismantleUIView(Controller:coordinator:) (static) for teardown.

For SwiftUI inside UIKit, UIHostingController with sizingOptions = .intrinsicContentSize, and UIHostingConfiguration for cells:

cell.contentConfiguration = UIHostingConfiguration {
    HStack { Image(systemName: "star"); Text(item.title) }
}
P2

ViewBuilder, ViewModifier, and building a design system

struct CardStyle: ViewModifier {
    func body(content: Content) -> some View {
        content
            .padding(16)
            .background(.regularMaterial, in: .rect(cornerRadius: 16))
            .shadow(color: .black.opacity(0.08), radius: 12, y: 4)
    }
}
extension View { func card() -> some View { modifier(CardStyle()) } }

// Custom container using @ViewBuilder
struct LabeledCard<Content: View>: View {
    let title: String
    @ViewBuilder let content: Content
    var body: some View {
        VStack(alignment: .leading, spacing: 8) {
            Text(title).font(.headline)
            content
        }.card()
    }
}

// Custom button style — the idiomatic way to theme controls
struct PrimaryButtonStyle: ButtonStyle {
    func makeBody(configuration: Configuration) -> some View {
        configuration.label
            .frame(maxWidth: .infinity).padding(.vertical, 14)
            .background(configuration.isPressed ? Color.accentColor.opacity(0.8) : .accentColor,
                        in: .capsule)
            .foregroundStyle(.white)
            .scaleEffect(configuration.isPressed ? 0.98 : 1)
            .animation(.snappy(duration: 0.15), value: configuration.isPressed)
    }
}

Why ButtonStyle/LabelStyle/ToggleStyle over a custom view: styles compose through the environment, so you can set one at the top of a subtree and everything picks it up, and you keep the semantic Button (accessibility, hit testing, keyboard) for free.

P2

AsyncImage — and why you'd write your own

AsyncImage(url: url, transaction: .init(animation: .easeIn)) { phase in
    switch phase {
    case .empty:            ProgressView()
    case .success(let img): img.resizable().scaledToFill()
    case .failure:          Image(systemName: "photo")
    @unknown default:       EmptyView()
    }
}

Limitations to name: no persistent disk cache (only URLCache), no downsampling, no in-flight request coalescing, reloads when the view is recycled in a List, and no priority/cancellation control. For a production feed I'd use an actor-backed loader (see Chapter 3's ImageDownloader) or Nuke/Kingfisher, wrapped in a small SwiftUI view. Saying exactly why is the differentiator.

P2

SwiftData in SwiftUI

@Model
final class Trip {
    @Attribute(.unique) var id: UUID
    var name: String
    var date: Date
    @Relationship(deleteRule: .cascade, inverse: \Stop.trip) var stops: [Stop] = []
    init(id: UUID = .init(), name: String, date: Date) { self.id = id; self.name = name; self.date = date }
}

@main struct App: App {
    var body: some Scene {
        WindowGroup { TripList() }
            .modelContainer(for: Trip.self)
    }
}

struct TripList: View {
    @Environment(\.modelContext) private var context
    @Query(filter: #Predicate<Trip> { $0.date > Date.now },
           sort: [SortDescriptor(\Trip.date)]) private var upcoming: [Trip]

    var body: some View {
        List(upcoming) { Text($0.name) }
            .toolbar { Button("Add") { context.insert(Trip(name: "New", date: .now)) } }
    }
}

@Query re-runs automatically on changes. @Model is a macro that makes the class observable and persistent-backed. Chapter 8 covers SwiftData vs Core Data trade-offs, which is the question they actually ask.

P3

Canvas, TimelineView, and high-performance drawing

TimelineView(.animation) { timeline in
    Canvas { context, size in
        let t = timeline.date.timeIntervalSinceReferenceDate
        for i in 0..<200 {
            let x = size.width  * (0.5 + 0.4 * cos(t + Double(i)))
            let y = size.height * (0.5 + 0.4 * sin(t * 1.3 + Double(i)))
            context.fill(Path(ellipseIn: CGRect(x: x, y: y, width: 4, height: 4)),
                         with: .color(.blue))
        }
    }
}

Canvas draws immediate-mode into one layer — hugely faster than 200 Circle() views because there's no view tree. TimelineView(.animation) redraws every frame; .periodic(from:by:) on a schedule. Mention .drawingGroup() as the Metal-flattening alternative for existing view trees.
