Transferable, drag & drop, and share sheets

struct Trip: Transferable {
    static var transferRepresentation: some TransferRepresentation {
        CodableRepresentation(contentType: .trip)
        ProxyRepresentation(exporting: \.name)
    }
}
Text(trip.name).draggable(trip)
List { }.dropDestination(for: Trip.self) { items, location in true }
ShareLink(item: trip, preview: .init(trip.name))
P3

State restoration and @SceneStorage

struct ContentView: View {
    @SceneStorage("selectedTab") private var selectedTab: String = "home"
    @SceneStorage("draft") private var draft: String = ""
}

@SceneStorage is per-scene and restored by the system on relaunch; @AppStorage is UserDefaults and global. Neither is for large data — say that, since people try to store models in @AppStorage.

6

Architecture, Patterns & Design Principles

15 questions
P0

MVC, MVVM, MVP, VIPER, Clean, TCA — compare them and tell me what you use

Answer with the problem each one solves, not the box diagram.

MVC (Apple's version). Model, View, Controller — but on iOS the view controller owns the view, so it collapses into "Massive View Controller". It's fine for simple screens and it's what UIKit's APIs assume. Its real failure mode is testability: business logic ends up entangled with UIViewController lifecycle, which you can't instantiate in a unit test without a window.

MVVM. Introduce a ViewModel that owns presentation logic and exposes UI-ready state. The view binds to it. The view model has no UIKit/SwiftUI import, so it's a plain object you can unit test. This is the default for both UIKit (with Combine or closures for binding) and SwiftUI (with @Observable).

MVP. Presenter holds a weak reference to a View protocol and pushes updates into it. More explicit than MVVM, easier in ObjC-era code, but more boilerplate and the view protocol tends to grow one method per UI element.

VIPER. View, Interactor, Presenter, Entity, Router. Extremely explicit separation; every screen becomes 5+ files. Pays off in very large teams with strict module boundaries; drowns small teams in boilerplate. Be honest about that.

Clean Architecture. Layers with a dependency rule pointing inward: Presentation → Domain (use cases + entities) → Data (repositories). The domain layer imports nothing. Good for large apps where business rules outlive UI frameworks.

TCA (The Composable Architecture). Unidirectional: State, Action, Reducer, Effect, Store. Everything is a value; side effects are described as data and executed by the runtime. Huge testability win (you assert on exact state transitions), strong composition, good navigation story — at the cost of a steep learning curve, compile-time cost, and a third-party dependency.

What I'd say for myself at 3 years: "MVVM with a repository/service layer and coordinators for navigation, because it gives me testable presentation logic without VIPER's file explosion. I'd reach for Clean's use-case layer when the business rules get complex enough that view models start duplicating logic, and I'd only bring in TCA if the team already knows it."

P0

Write an MVVM screen properly (SwiftUI and UIKit)

SwiftUI version:

// Domain
struct Product: Identifiable, Equatable, Sendable { let id: String; let name: String; let price: Decimal }

protocol ProductRepository: Sendable {
    func products(page: Int) async throws -> [Product]
}

// ViewModel — no SwiftUI import
@Observable
@MainActor
final class ProductListViewModel {
    enum State: Equatable { case idle, loading, loaded([Product]), failed(String) }

    private(set) var state: State = .idle
    private let repository: ProductRepository
    private var page = 0

    init(repository: ProductRepository) { self.repository = repository }

    func load() async {
        guard state != .loading else { return }
        state = .loading
        do {
            let items = try await repository.products(page: 0)
            page = 0
            state = .loaded(items)
        } catch {
            state = .failed(error.localizedDescription)
        }
    }

    func loadMoreIfNeeded(currentItem: Product) async {
        guard case .loaded(let items) = state,
              items.suffix(3).contains(currentItem) else { return }
        page += 1
        if let more = try? await repository.products(page: page) {
            state = .loaded(items + more)
        }
    }
}

// View
struct ProductListView: View {
    @State private var viewModel: ProductListViewModel
    init(repository: ProductRepository) {
        _viewModel = State(initialValue: ProductListViewModel(repository: repository))
    }

    var body: some View {
        Group {
            switch viewModel.state {
            case .idle, .loading:      ProgressView()
            case .loaded(let items):
                List(items) { product in
                    ProductRow(product: product)
                        .task { await viewModel.loadMoreIfNeeded(currentItem: product) }
                }
            case .failed(let message):
                ContentUnavailableView("Something went wrong", systemImage: "wifi.slash",
                                       description: Text(message))
            }
        }
        .task { await viewModel.load() }
    }
}

Why an enum State instead of isLoading/items/error booleans: it makes illegal states unrepresentable. You can't be loading and failed. Interviewers love this — say "I model state as a sum type so impossible combinations can't compile."

UIKit version of the binding:

final class ProductListViewController: UIViewController {
    private let viewModel: ProductListViewModel
    private var cancellables = Set<AnyCancellable>()

    override func viewDidLoad() {
        super.viewDidLoad()
        viewModel.$state                         // if using ObservableObject
            .receive(on: DispatchQueue.main)
            .sink { [weak self] in self?.render($0) }
            .store(in: &cancellables)
        Task { await viewModel.load() }
    }

    private func render(_ state: ProductListViewModel.State) {
        switch state { /* update UI */ }
    }
}

With @Observable in UIKit, use withObservationTracking or expose an AsyncStream; with ObservableObject, Combine as above; with neither, a plain var onStateChange: ((State) -> Void)? closure — which is the simplest and most testable of all.

P0

Dependency injection in Swift — show me three ways

// 1. Initializer injection — the default, always prefer it
final class CheckoutService {
    private let api: PaymentAPI
    private let analytics: AnalyticsTracking
    init(api: PaymentAPI, analytics: AnalyticsTracking) { self.api = api; self.analytics = analytics }
}

// 2. Property injection — for UIKit VCs created from storyboards
final class CheckoutViewController: UIViewController {
    var service: CheckoutService!      // set by the coordinator right after init
}

// 3. Environment / container injection
struct AppDependencies {
    var api: PaymentAPI = .live
    var analytics: AnalyticsTracking = FirebaseAnalytics()
}
extension EnvironmentValues { @Entry var deps = AppDependencies() }

The swift-dependencies / TCA style (worth knowing, increasingly common):

private enum PaymentAPIKey: DependencyKey {
    static let liveValue: PaymentAPI = LivePaymentAPI()
    static let testValue: PaymentAPI = MockPaymentAPI()
}
extension DependencyValues {
    var paymentAPI: PaymentAPI { get { self[PaymentAPIKey.self] } set { self[PaymentAPIKey.self] = newValue } }
}
final class Model { @Dependency(\.paymentAPI) var api }

What they're testing: can you make a type testable? The answer is always "depend on a protocol (or a struct of closures), inject it, and substitute a mock in tests." Name the downside of a global service locator / singleton container: hidden dependencies, implicit ordering requirements, and tests that leak state into each other.

Protocol vs struct-of-closures is a good nuance to raise:

struct PaymentAPI: Sendable {
    var charge: @Sendable (Money) async throws -> Receipt
    static let live = PaymentAPI { money in try await realCharge(money) }
    static let failing = PaymentAPI { _ in throw TestError.unimplemented }
}

Structs of closures avoid a protocol + mock class per dependency and let you override one method per test. That's the modern Point-Free style.

P0

Singletons — when are they acceptable?

Acceptable: genuinely process-wide, stateless-or-system-owned things — URLSession.shared, FileManager.default, UserDefaults.standard, NotificationCenter.default.

Problems: hidden dependencies (you can't see them in the initializer), impossible to substitute in tests, shared mutable state across tests, initialisation-order bugs, and they make concurrency reasoning harder (in Swift 6 a mutable static var is a compile error unless isolated).

// Swift 6: this no longer compiles
final class Legacy { static var shared = Legacy() }   // ❌ non-Sendable mutable global

// Fix options:
final class Service: Sendable { static let shared = Service() }        // immutable + Sendable
@MainActor final class UIService { static let shared = UIService() }   // actor-isolated
actor Store { static let shared = Store() }

The answer that scores: "I keep the singleton as an implementation detail — AnalyticsService.shared exists, but every consumer takes an AnalyticsTracking protocol in its initializer and production code passes .shared. Tests pass a spy."

P0

SOLID applied to iOS — with real examples

S — Single Responsibility. A view controller that does networking, parsing, formatting and layout has four reasons to change. Split into VC (layout/lifecycle) + ViewModel (presentation) + Repository (data) + Formatter.

O — Open/Closed. Adding a new analytics provider shouldn't mean editing AnalyticsService. Make it accept [AnalyticsProvider] and add a new conformer.

protocol AnalyticsProvider { func track(_ event: Event) }
final class Analytics { private let providers: [AnalyticsProvider]
    init(providers: [AnalyticsProvider]) { self.providers = providers }
    func track(_ e: Event) { providers.forEach { $0.track(e) } } }

L — Liskov Substitution. A subclass must be usable wherever the base is. The classic iOS violation: subclassing UITableViewCell and overriding prepareForReuse without calling super. Also Square: Rectangle where setting width changes height.

I — Interface Segregation. Don't force conformers to implement methods they don't need. UIKit's own UITableViewDelegate is a mild violation (40 optional methods) — in your own code, split Repository into Reading and Writing protocols so a read-only consumer isn't handed delete.

D — Dependency Inversion. High-level modules depend on abstractions. ProductListViewModel depends on ProductRepository (protocol), not on URLSessionProductRepository. The concrete type is chosen at the composition root (AppDelegate/App).

P1

Coordinator pattern — implement it

protocol Coordinator: AnyObject {
    var childCoordinators: [Coordinator] { get set }
    var navigationController: UINavigationController { get }
    func start()
}

final class AppCoordinator: Coordinator {
    var childCoordinators: [Coordinator] = []
    let navigationController: UINavigationController
    private let deps: AppDependencies

    init(navigationController: UINavigationController, deps: AppDependencies) {
        self.navigationController = navigationController
        self.deps = deps
    }

    func start() {
        let vc = HomeViewController(viewModel: .init(repo: deps.productRepo))
        vc.onSelectProduct = { [weak self] id in self?.showDetail(id) }
        navigationController.setViewControllers([vc], animated: false)
    }

    private func showDetail(_ id: String) {
        let child = ProductCoordinator(navigationController: navigationController,
                                       productID: id, deps: deps)
        child.onFinish = { [weak self, weak child] in
            self?.childCoordinators.removeAll { $0 === child }   // ← the leak everyone forgets
        }
        childCoordinators.append(child)
        child.start()
    }
}

The two things interviewers check: (1) the view controller exposes closures/delegates and never pushes anything itself — that's what makes it reusable and testable; (2) you remove finished children from childCoordinators, or you leak every screen the user ever visited.

SwiftUI equivalent: a Router/NavigationPath object in the environment, as shown in Chapter 5. Same idea — navigation is data, owned outside the view.

P1

Repository pattern and a clean data layer

protocol ProductRepository: Sendable {
    func products(page: Int) async throws -> [Product]
    func product(id: String) async throws -> Product
}

actor DefaultProductRepository: ProductRepository {
    private let remote: ProductRemoteDataSource
    private let local: ProductLocalDataSource
    private var memoryCache: [String: (Product, Date)] = [:]

    func products(page: Int) async throws -> [Product] {
        do {
            let fresh = try await remote.products(page: page)
            try await local.save(fresh)                 // write-through
            return fresh
        } catch let error as URLError where error.code == .notConnectedToInternet {
            return try await local.products(page: page) // offline fallback
        }
    }
}

The interview point: the repository hides where data comes from. The view model doesn't know about URLSession, Core Data, or cache policy — it asks for products. That's what makes offline support, caching strategy changes, and test doubles cheap.

Name the strategies: cache-first, network-first with cache fallback, stale-while-revalidate (return cache immediately, then push an update), and say which you'd pick for a feed (SWR) versus a payment confirmation (network-only).

P1

The Gang-of-Four patterns that actually appear in iOS

Delegate — one-to-one callbacks (UITableViewDelegate). Use a weak class-bound protocol. Observer — NotificationCenter, KVO, Combine, @Observable. Factory — UIColor.systemBlue, URLSession(configuration:), and your own static func make.... Builder — URLComponents, NSAttributedString composition, SwiftUI's modifier chain. Adapter — UIViewRepresentable, a wrapper turning a third-party SDK into your protocol. Facade — a PaymentService hiding Stripe + your backend + Keychain behind one API. Strategy — ButtonStyle, sorting comparators, retry policies injected as closures. Decorator — a CachingRepository wrapping another Repository; middleware around a network client. Composite — the view hierarchy itself. Command — Operation, undo managers, TCA actions. Singleton — discussed above. Chain of Responsibility — the UIKit responder chain.

// Decorator — logging and caching layered onto a repository without touching it
struct LoggingRepository<Base: ProductRepository>: ProductRepository {
    let base: Base
    let log: (String) -> Void
    func products(page: Int) async throws -> [Product] {
        log("fetch page \(page)")
        defer { log("done page \(page)") }
        return try await base.products(page: page)
    }
}
P1

How do you structure a large iOS app into modules?

App target (thin: composition root, AppDelegate/App, DI wiring)
├── Features/            (Feature modules — Checkout, Profile, Feed)
│     each depends on Domain + DesignSystem, NEVER on another feature
├── Domain/              (Entities, use cases, repository PROTOCOLS — no UIKit)
├── Data/                (Repository implementations, networking, persistence)
├── DesignSystem/        (Colors, typography, components)
└── Core/                (Extensions, Logger, small utilities)

Rules I state: dependencies point inward and downward only; feature modules never import each other (route through the app layer or a shared routing protocol); Domain imports nothing but Foundation; interfaces live in the module that uses them, not the one that implements them (dependency inversion).

Why modularise: faster incremental builds (only the changed module recompiles), enforced boundaries the compiler checks, parallel work without merge hell, feature-level previews and tests that don't build the whole app, and the ability to ship a feature into an App Clip or extension.

Costs to be honest about: more Xcode project/SPM ceremony, cross-module dispatch can't always be inlined (mitigate with @inlinable/package access), and over-modularising a 3-person app is a net loss.

// Package.swift for a feature module
let package = Package(
    name: "Checkout",
    platforms: [.iOS(.v17)],
    products: [.library(name: "Checkout", targets: ["Checkout"])],
    dependencies: [.package(path: "../Domain"), .package(path: "../DesignSystem")],
    targets: [
        .target(name: "Checkout", dependencies: ["Domain", "DesignSystem"],
                swiftSettings: [.swiftLanguageMode(.v6)]),
        .testTarget(name: "CheckoutTests", dependencies: ["Checkout"])
    ]
)
P2

Unidirectional data flow / Redux / TCA — explain and implement a mini version

// Minimal unidirectional store — enough to whiteboard
@MainActor
final class Store<State, Action>: ObservableObject {
    @Published private(set) var state: State
    private let reduce: (inout State, Action) -> Effect<Action>?

    init(initial: State, reduce: @escaping (inout State, Action) -> Effect<Action>?) {
        self.state = initial; self.reduce = reduce
    }

    func send(_ action: Action) {
        guard let effect = reduce(&state, action) else { return }
        Task { for await next in effect.actions { self.send(next) } }
    }
}

struct Effect<Action> { let actions: AsyncStream<Action> }

// Usage
enum CounterAction { case increment, loadRemote, remoteLoaded(Int) }
struct CounterState { var count = 0; var isLoading = false }

let reducer: (inout CounterState, CounterAction) -> Effect<CounterAction>? = { state, action in
    switch action {
    case .increment: state.count += 1; return nil
    case .loadRemote:
        state.isLoading = true
        return Effect(actions: AsyncStream { c in
            Task { c.yield(.remoteLoaded(try! await api.count())); c.finish() }
        })
    case .remoteLoaded(let n): state.count = n; state.isLoading = false; return nil
    }
}

Why teams adopt it: every state change is a pure function you can test with no mocks (reduce(&state, .increment); XCTAssertEqual(state.count, 1)), time-travel debugging, and composition of features. Why teams regret it: learning curve, verbosity for simple screens, compile times, and a large dependency.

P2

How would you refactor a 3000-line view controller?

Give a sequence, because that's what they're testing — can you do this safely?

  1. Add characterisation tests first — snapshot tests or UI tests that pin current behaviour so you can detect regressions.
  2. Extract pure functions: formatting, validation, calculations → free functions or a Formatter type. Zero risk, immediate test coverage.
  3. Extract the data source / delegate into its own object.
  4. Extract networking into a repository behind a protocol.
  5. Introduce a ViewModel and move presentation state into it, one property at a time, keeping the VC compiling at each step.
  6. Extract navigation into a coordinator or closures.
  7. Split the view into subviews/child view controllers.
  8. Only then delete the dead code.

State the principle: never a big-bang rewrite; small, shippable, reviewable steps behind existing tests. And mention feature flags so a half-migrated screen can ship.

P2

How do you handle app-wide state (auth/session)?

@Observable @MainActor
final class SessionModel {
    enum State { case unknown, signedOut, signedIn(User) }
    private(set) var state: State = .unknown

    private let keychain: KeychainStore
    private let api: AuthAPI

    func restore() async {
        guard let token = try? keychain.token() else { return state = .signedOut }
        state = (try? await api.me(token: token)).map(State.signedIn) ?? .signedOut
    }
    func signOut() { try? keychain.clear(); state = .signedOut }
}

// Root switches on it — this is the whole "auth flow" problem
struct RootView: View {
    @Environment(SessionModel.self) private var session
    var body: some View {
        switch session.state {
        case .unknown:  SplashView()
        case .signedOut: SignInView()
        case .signedIn(let user): MainTabView(user: user).id(user.id)  // reset on user change
        }
    }
}

The .id(user.id) detail — resetting all downstream state when the user changes — is a nice touch that shows you've shipped a multi-account app.

P2

How do you do feature flags / A/B tests / remote config?

protocol FeatureFlagProvider: Sendable {
    func isEnabled(_ flag: Flag) -> Bool
    func variant<T: Decodable>(_ key: String, as: T.Type) -> T?
}

enum Flag: String, CaseIterable { case newCheckout, glassRedesign }

@Observable final class FeatureFlags {
    private let remote: FeatureFlagProvider
    private let overrides: [Flag: Bool]      // debug menu overrides, local-first
    func isEnabled(_ f: Flag) -> Bool { overrides[f] ?? remote.isEnabled(f) }
}

Things to mention: flags must have a safe default for when config hasn't loaded; you need a debug menu to force variants (QA will demand it); flags must be removable — track their creation date and delete them, or you accumulate combinatorial untested paths; and remote-config-driven UI changes must not violate App Review guidelines (no shipping hidden features that change the app's purpose).

P3

Clean Architecture use cases — is the extra layer worth it?

protocol UseCase { associatedtype Input; associatedtype Output
    func execute(_ input: Input) async throws -> Output }

struct PlaceOrderUseCase: UseCase {
    let cart: CartRepository
    let payments: PaymentGateway
    let inventory: InventoryService

    func execute(_ input: Cart) async throws -> Order {
        try inventory.validate(input)
        let receipt = try await payments.charge(input.total)
        let order = try await cart.placeOrder(input, receipt: receipt)
        return order
    }
}

Argue both sides honestly: the use case is where business rules live, independent of any view model, so two screens that place an order share it and it's tested once. But for a screen whose "business rule" is "fetch and show", the use case is a pass-through class that adds a file and no value. My rule: introduce the use-case layer when logic is shared across features or has more than about three branches.

P3

How do you design an SDK/framework others consume?

Points that land: a minimal public surface (public only what's needed; internal by default; open only where subclassing is intended); semantic versioning and a documented deprecation policy; @available(*, deprecated, renamed:) rather than deleting; no forced singletons — let consumers inject configuration; no swizzling; avoid @frozen/@inlinable unless you accept the ABI commitment; ship as an XCFramework and SPM; don't leak your dependencies into the public API (wrap them); and make the SDK work with strict concurrency (Sendable public types) because consumers on Swift 6 can't adopt you otherwise.

7

Networking

13 questions
P0

Build a production network layer from scratch

This is the single most common "write some code" task at 3 years. Practise writing this in under 20 minutes.

// MARK: - Endpoint

struct Endpoint<Response: Decodable> {
    var path: String
    var method: HTTPMethod = .get
    var query: [URLQueryItem] = []
    var headers: [String: String] = [:]
    var body: Data? = nil
}

enum HTTPMethod: String { case get = "GET", post = "POST", put = "PUT", delete = "DELETE", patch = "PATCH" }

// MARK: - Errors

enum NetworkError: Error, LocalizedError {
    case invalidURL
    case transport(URLError)
    case unacceptableStatus(code: Int, data: Data)
    case decoding(DecodingError)
    case unauthorized
    case unknown(Error)

    var errorDescription: String? {
        switch self {
        case .invalidURL:                 "Could not build the request."
        case .transport(let e) where e.code == .notConnectedToInternet: "You appear to be offline."
        case .transport:                  "Network problem. Please try again."
        case .unacceptableStatus(let c,_): "Server returned \(c)."
        case .decoding:                   "Unexpected response from the server."
        case .unauthorized:               "Please sign in again."
        case .unknown:                    "Something went wrong."
        }
    }
}

// MARK: - Client

protocol HTTPClient: Sendable {
    func send<Response: Decodable>(_ endpoint: Endpoint<Response>) async throws -> Response
}

actor APIClient: HTTPClient {
    private let baseURL: URL
    private let session: URLSession
    private let decoder: JSONDecoder
    private let tokenProvider: TokenProviding

    init(baseURL: URL,
         session: URLSession = .shared,
         tokenProvider: TokenProviding) {
        self.baseURL = baseURL
        self.session = session
        self.tokenProvider = tokenProvider
        let d = JSONDecoder()
        d.keyDecodingStrategy = .convertFromSnakeCase
        d.dateDecodingStrategy = .iso8601
        self.decoder = d
    }

    func send<Response: Decodable>(_ endpoint: Endpoint<Response>) async throws -> Response {
        var request = try makeRequest(endpoint)
        request.setValue("Bearer \(await tokenProvider.accessToken())",
                         forHTTPHeaderField: "Authorization")

        let (data, response) = try await perform(request)

        guard let http = response as? HTTPURLResponse else { throw NetworkError.unknown(URLError(.badServerResponse)) }

        switch http.statusCode {
        case 200..<300:
            if Response.self == EmptyResponse.self { return EmptyResponse() as! Response }
            do { return try decoder.decode(Response.self, from: data) }
            catch let e as DecodingError { throw NetworkError.decoding(e) }
        case 401:
            try await tokenProvider.refresh()            // single-flight refresh, see below
            return try await send(endpoint)              // retry once
        default:
            throw NetworkError.unacceptableStatus(code: http.statusCode, data: data)
        }
    }

    private func perform(_ request: URLRequest) async throws -> (Data, URLResponse) {
        do { return try await session.data(for: request) }
        catch let e as URLError { throw NetworkError.transport(e) }
    }

    private func makeRequest<R>(_ e: Endpoint<R>) throws -> URLRequest {
        guard var comps = URLComponents(url: baseURL.appendingPathComponent(e.path),
                                        resolvingAgainstBaseURL: false) else { throw NetworkError.invalidURL }
        if !e.query.isEmpty { comps.queryItems = e.query }
        guard let url = comps.url else { throw NetworkError.invalidURL }

        var request = URLRequest(url: url)
        request.httpMethod = e.method.rawValue
        request.httpBody = e.body
        request.setValue("application/json", forHTTPHeaderField: "Content-Type")
        e.headers.forEach { request.setValue($0.value, forHTTPHeaderField: $0.key) }
        return request
    }
}

struct EmptyResponse: Decodable {}

// MARK: - Usage

extension Endpoint {
    static func products(page: Int) -> Endpoint<[Product]> {
        .init(path: "products", query: [.init(name: "page", value: "\(page)")])
    }
}

let products = try await client.send(.products(page: 1))

What makes this a good answer: generic over the response type so there are no casts at the call site; errors are a typed enum with user-facing messages; auth is injected not hardcoded; the client is a protocol so it's mockable; and the endpoint definitions live next to the feature that uses them.

P0

Retry with exponential backoff and jitter

func withRetry<T: Sendable>(
    maxAttempts: Int = 3,
    baseDelay: Duration = .milliseconds(300),
    isRetryable: (Error) -> Bool = defaultRetryPolicy,
    operation: () async throws -> T
) async throws -> T {
    var attempt = 0
    while true {
        do { return try await operation() }
        catch {
            attempt += 1
            guard attempt < maxAttempts, isRetryable(error) else { throw error }
            // exponential backoff with full jitter
            let exponential = baseDelay * Int(pow(2.0, Double(attempt - 1)))
            let jittered = Duration.milliseconds(
                Int.random(in: 0...Int(exponential.components.seconds * 1000 +
                                       exponential.components.attoseconds / 1_000_000_000_000_000)))
            try await Task.sleep(for: jittered)
            try Task.checkCancellation()
        }
    }
}

func defaultRetryPolicy(_ error: Error) -> Bool {
    switch error {
    case let e as URLError:
        return [.timedOut, .networkConnectionLost, .cannotConnectToHost,
                .notConnectedToInternet, .dnsLookupFailed].contains(e.code)
    case NetworkError.unacceptableStatus(let code, _):
        return code == 429 || (500...599).contains(code)
    default: return false
    }
}

Points to make out loud: never retry non-idempotent requests (a POST that charges a card) unless the server supports an idempotency key; honour Retry-After on 429/503; add jitter so thousands of clients don't retry in lockstep and stampede the server; and always respect cancellation inside the backoff sleep.

P0

Token refresh without a thundering herd (single-flight)

A favourite deep-dive: "Ten requests all get 401 at once. What happens?"

actor TokenProvider: TokenProviding {
    private var token: Token?
    private var refreshTask: Task<Token, Error>?

    func accessToken() async throws -> String {
        if let token, !token.isExpired { return token.value }
        return try await refresh().value
    }

    @discardableResult
    func refresh() async throws -> Token {
        if let refreshTask { return try await refreshTask.value }   // join the in-flight refresh

        let task = Task<Token, Error> { [refreshToken = token?.refresh] in
            guard let refreshToken else { throw NetworkError.unauthorized }
            return try await authAPI.refresh(refreshToken)
        }
        refreshTask = task
        defer { refreshTask = nil }

        do {
            let new = try await task.value
            token = new
            try keychain.save(new)
            return new
        } catch {
            token = nil
            try? keychain.clear()
            await MainActor.run { session.signOut() }
            throw NetworkError.unauthorized
        }
    }
}

Without the refreshTask coalescing, ten 401s trigger ten refresh calls, nine of which invalidate the refresh token (most backends rotate it) and log the user out. This is a real production bug and a great story to have.

P0

URLSession — configurations, tasks, delegates

let config = URLSessionConfiguration.default
config.timeoutIntervalForRequest = 30       // per-request inactivity
config.timeoutIntervalForResource = 300     // total lifetime of the resource
config.waitsForConnectivity = true          // wait instead of failing immediately
config.requestCachePolicy = .useProtocolCachePolicy
config.httpMaximumConnectionsPerHost = 6
config.urlCache = URLCache(memoryCapacity: 20*1024*1024, diskCapacity: 200*1024*1024)
config.httpAdditionalHeaders = ["Accept": "application/json"]
let session = URLSession(configuration: config, delegate: self, delegateQueue: nil)

Configurations: .default (disk cache + cookies + credentials), .ephemeral (nothing persisted — "private browsing"), .background(withIdentifier:) (transfers continue when the app is suspended or killed; only upload/download tasks, no completion-handler data tasks).

Task types: dataTask (in memory), uploadTask, downloadTask (to a file, supports background + resume data), streamTask, webSocketTask.

Async APIs: data(for:), upload(for:from:), download(for:), and bytes(for:) which gives you an AsyncSequence of bytes — the right tool for streaming NDJSON or server-sent events.

let (bytes, _) = try await URLSession.shared.bytes(for: request)
for try await line in bytes.lines {
    let event = try JSONDecoder().decode(StreamEvent.self, from: Data(line.utf8))
    await handle(event)
}

Key delegate methods to name: urlSession(_:task:didSendBodyData:...) for upload progress, urlSession(_:downloadTask:didWriteData:...) for download progress, urlSession(_:didReceive challenge:) for auth and certificate pinning, urlSession(_:task:willPerformHTTPRedirection:) to control redirects, and urlSessionDidFinishEvents(forBackgroundURLSession:) to call the app delegate's stored completion handler.

Retain warning: URLSession(configuration:delegate:delegateQueue:) holds a strong reference to the delegate until you call finishTasksAndInvalidate() or invalidateAndCancel(). Forgetting this leaks the delegate — a classic real bug.

P1

HTTP fundamentals an iOS dev must know

Status codes: 200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content, 301/302 redirect, 304 Not Modified (conditional GET), 400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized (not authenticated), 403 Forbidden (authenticated but not allowed), 404, 409 Conflict, 422 Unprocessable, 429 Too Many Requests, 500, 502, 503, 504.

Idempotency: GET, PUT, DELETE, HEAD are idempotent; POST and PATCH are not. This drives retry policy.

Caching headers: Cache-Control: max-age=60, no-store, no-cache, ETag + If-None-Match, Last-Modified + If-Modified-Since. URLCache handles these automatically when your policy is .useProtocolCachePolicy.

HTTP/2 and HTTP/3: multiplexing over one connection removes head-of-line blocking at the HTTP layer; URLSession negotiates it automatically. That's why httpMaximumConnectionsPerHost matters less than it used to.

REST vs GraphQL: REST gives you cacheable URLs and simple HTTP semantics; GraphQL gives you exactly the fields you need in one round trip (great for mobile bandwidth) at the cost of HTTP caching and a heavier client. If asked which you'd pick for a mobile app: GraphQL when the screen needs data from many resources and you control the backend; REST when caching and simplicity matter.

P1

Codable decoding failures — how do you debug and harden them?

do { _ = try decoder.decode(Product.self, from: data) }
catch let DecodingError.keyNotFound(key, ctx) {
    print("Missing key '\(key.stringValue)' at \(ctx.codingPath.map(\.stringValue).joined(separator: "."))")
} catch let DecodingError.typeMismatch(type, ctx) {
    print("Expected \(type) at \(ctx.codingPath.map(\.stringValue).joined(separator: "."))")
} catch let DecodingError.valueNotFound(type, ctx) {
    print("Null for non-optional \(type) at \(ctx.debugDescription)")
} catch let DecodingError.dataCorrupted(ctx) {
    print("Corrupt: \(ctx.debugDescription)")
}

Hardening strategies: make every field the server may omit Optional or give it a default via decodeIfPresent; use a lossy array wrapper so one bad element doesn't kill the page; log the raw payload (redacted) with the coding path on failure; and version your DTOs separately from your domain models so a backend change doesn't ripple through the app.

Always separate DTO from domain model:

struct ProductDTO: Decodable { let id: String; let name: String?; let price_cents: Int }
extension ProductDTO {
    func toDomain() throws -> Product {
        guard let name else { throw MappingError.missingName(id: id) }
        return Product(id: id, name: name, price: Decimal(price_cents) / 100)
    }
}

This is a strong senior signal — it means a backend rename doesn't touch your views, and your domain model can have non-optional invariants.

P1

Certificate pinning — implement it and argue about it

final class PinningDelegate: NSObject, URLSessionDelegate {
    private let pinnedKeyHashes: Set<String>   // base64 SHA-256 of SPKI

    func urlSession(_ session: URLSession,
                    didReceive challenge: URLAuthenticationChallenge) async
    -> (URLSession.AuthChallengeDisposition, URLCredential?) {

        guard challenge.protectionSpace.authenticationMethod == NSURLAuthenticationMethodServerTrust,
              let trust = challenge.protectionSpace.serverTrust else {
            return (.performDefaultHandling, nil)
        }

        // 1. Let the system validate the chain first — never skip this.
        var error: CFError?
        guard SecTrustEvaluateWithError(trust, &error) else { return (.cancelAuthenticationChallenge, nil) }

        // 2. Then check our pin against the leaf's public key.
        guard let chain = SecTrustCopyCertificateChain(trust) as? [SecCertificate],
              let leaf = chain.first,
              let key = SecCertificateCopyKey(leaf),
              let data = SecKeyCopyExternalRepresentation(key, nil) as Data?
        else { return (.cancelAuthenticationChallenge, nil) }

        let hash = Data(SHA256.hash(data: subjectPublicKeyInfoHeader + data)).base64EncodedString()
        return pinnedKeyHashes.contains(hash)
            ? (.useCredential, URLCredential(trust: trust))
            : (.cancelAuthenticationChallenge, nil)
    }
}

The nuance that earns points: pin the public key (SPKI), not the certificate — certificates rotate yearly, keys usually don't, so key pinning survives renewal. Always ship at least two pins (current + backup) and a kill switch, or a rotation bricks every installed app. And note the modern alternative: NSPinnedDomains in Info.plist, which does pinning declaratively with no code.

Argue the trade-off: pinning defends against a compromised CA and against users installing a proxy CA to inspect traffic; it does not protect a jailbroken device (the pin can be patched out), and it creates an operational risk. For a banking app, yes. For a content app, usually not worth it.

P1

Alamofire vs raw URLSession — what would you pick?

Honest answer: with async/await, most of what Alamofire provided (chaining, response serialisation, validation) is now ~50 lines of your own code, and you avoid a dependency in your most security-sensitive layer. I'd use raw URLSession for a new app.

Where Alamofire still earns its place: complex multipart uploads with progress, RequestInterceptor/RequestRetrier if you want it off the shelf, Session event monitors for logging, and reachability. Also: if the team already uses it everywhere, consistency beats purity.

The thing not to say: "Alamofire is easier." Say what specific capability you'd be buying.

P2

WebSockets and real-time

actor ChatSocket {
    private var task: URLSessionWebSocketTask?
    private var continuation: AsyncStream<ChatEvent>.Continuation?

    func connect(url: URL) -> AsyncStream<ChatEvent> {
        let (stream, continuation) = AsyncStream<ChatEvent>.makeStream()
        self.continuation = continuation
        let task = URLSession.shared.webSocketTask(with: url)
        self.task = task
        task.resume()
        Task { await receiveLoop() }
        Task { await pingLoop() }
        return stream
    }

    private func receiveLoop() async {
        guard let task else { return }
        do {
            while true {
                switch try await task.receive() {
                case .data(let d):   continuation?.yield(try decode(d))
                case .string(let s): continuation?.yield(try decode(Data(s.utf8)))
                @unknown default: break
                }
            }
        } catch {
            continuation?.finish()
            await reconnectWithBackoff()
        }
    }

    private func pingLoop() async {
        while !Task.isCancelled {
            try? await Task.sleep(for: .seconds(20))
            task?.sendPing { _ in }       // keep NAT/proxy from dropping the connection
        }
    }

    func send(_ message: ChatMessage) async throws {
        try await task?.send(.data(JSONEncoder().encode(message)))
    }
}

Points to raise: receive() must be called in a loop — it delivers one message per call; you need heartbeat pings; reconnect with backoff and resume from a last-known message ID so you don't lose or duplicate messages; and sockets die on backgrounding, so you need push notifications as the background delivery channel.

P2

Background downloads / uploads

let config = URLSessionConfiguration.background(withIdentifier: "com.app.bg")
config.isDiscretionary = false          // true lets the system pick a good time (Wi-Fi, charging)
config.sessionSendsLaunchEvents = true  // relaunch the app when transfers finish
let session = URLSession(configuration: config, delegate: self, delegateQueue: nil)
session.downloadTask(with: url).resume()

// AppDelegate
func application(_ app: UIApplication, handleEventsForBackgroundURLSession id: String,
                 completionHandler: @escaping () -> Void) {
    backgroundCompletionHandler = completionHandler       // store it
}

// URLSessionDelegate
func urlSessionDidFinishEvents(forBackgroundURLSession session: URLSession) {
    DispatchQueue.main.async {
        self.backgroundCompletionHandler?()               // must call it or you get killed
        self.backgroundCompletionHandler = nil
    }
}

func urlSession(_ s: URLSession, downloadTask: URLSessionDownloadTask,
                didFinishDownloadingTo location: URL) {
    // location is a TEMP file deleted when this method returns — move it synchronously
    try? FileManager.default.moveItem(at: location, to: finalURL)
}

Constraints to name: no completion handlers (delegate only), data tasks not supported, the app may be relaunched into the background purely to handle the event, and the downloaded file's temp URL is valid only inside the delegate call.

Resume data:

task.cancel(byProducingResumeData: { data in self.resumeData = data })
let resumed = session.downloadTask(withResumeData: resumeData)
P2

How do you test networking code?

final class MockURLProtocol: URLProtocol {
    nonisolated(unsafe) static var handler: ((URLRequest) throws -> (HTTPURLResponse, Data))?

    override class func canInit(with request: URLRequest) -> Bool { true }
    override class func canonicalRequest(for r: URLRequest) -> URLRequest { r }
    override func startLoading() {
        guard let handler = Self.handler else { return }
        do {
            let (response, data) = try handler(request)
            client?.urlProtocol(self, didReceive: response, cacheStoragePolicy: .notAllowed)
            client?.urlProtocol(self, didLoad: data)
            client?.urlProtocolDidFinishLoading(self)
        } catch {
            client?.urlProtocol(self, didFailWithError: error)
        }
    }
    override func stopLoading() {}
}

// In the test
let config = URLSessionConfiguration.ephemeral
config.protocolClasses = [MockURLProtocol.self]
let session = URLSession(configuration: config)

MockURLProtocol.handler = { request in
    #expect(request.url?.path == "/products")
    let response = HTTPURLResponse(url: request.url!, statusCode: 200, httpVersion: nil, headerFields: nil)!
    return (response, productsJSON)
}
let client = APIClient(baseURL: .init(string: "https://x.test")!, session: session, tokenProvider: .stub)
let result = try await client.send(.products(page: 1))
#expect(result.count == 2)

URLProtocol stubbing tests the real URLSession code path including headers, encoding, and status handling — much higher fidelity than mocking your own client protocol. Mention the alternatives: injecting a HTTPClient protocol mock (fast, less fidelity) and contract tests against a recorded fixture set.

P3

Multipart upload with progress

func multipartBody(boundary: String, fields: [String: String], fileURL: URL, fieldName: String) throws -> Data {
    var body = Data()
    func append(_ s: String) { body.append(Data(s.utf8)) }

    for (k, v) in fields {
        append("--\(boundary)\r\n")
        append("Content-Disposition: form-data; name=\"\(k)\"\r\n\r\n")
        append("\(v)\r\n")
    }
    append("--\(boundary)\r\n")
    append("Content-Disposition: form-data; name=\"\(fieldName)\"; filename=\"\(fileURL.lastPathComponent)\"\r\n")
    append("Content-Type: application/octet-stream\r\n\r\n")
    body.append(try Data(contentsOf: fileURL))
    append("\r\n--\(boundary)--\r\n")
    return body
}

// Progress via the modern API
let task = session.uploadTask(with: request, fromFile: fileURL)
let observation = task.progress.observe(\.fractionCompleted) { p, _ in
    Task { @MainActor in self.progress = p.fractionCompleted }
}
task.resume()

For large files, use uploadTask(with:fromFile:) rather than building the whole body in memory — otherwise a 500 MB video is 500 MB of RAM.

P3

Reachability — and why you probably shouldn't gate on it

import Network
let monitor = NWPathMonitor()
monitor.pathUpdateHandler = { path in
    let online = path.status == .satisfied
    let expensive = path.isExpensive        // cellular / personal hotspot
    let constrained = path.isConstrained    // Low Data Mode
}
monitor.start(queue: .global(qos: .background))

The senior answer: don't pre-flight with reachability — "connected to Wi-Fi" doesn't mean your server is reachable (captive portals, DNS failures, VPN). Just make the request and handle URLError.notConnectedToInternet. Use NWPathMonitor for UX decisions — showing an offline banner, deferring a big video upload when isExpensive/isConstrained is true, and to know when to auto-retry. Also set allowsExpensiveNetworkAccess / allowsConstrainedNetworkAccess on the request to respect Low Data Mode.

Persistence & Data

13 questions
P0

What are all the persistence options on iOS and when do you use each?

OptionUse forDon't use for
UserDefaultsSmall user preferences, flags, last-selected tab. Plist-backed, loaded into memory at launch.Anything secret, anything large (it's read at launch — a 5 MB defaults file costs launch time), structured/queried data
KeychainTokens, passwords, small secrets. Encrypted, survives app deletion unless you clear it.Bulk data (it's slow and size-limited in practice)
File system (Documents, Library/Caches, tmp)Images, downloads, JSON blobs, DBsAnything needing queries or relationships
Codable + JSON/plist fileSmall structured data, an offline snapshotLarge datasets, concurrent writes, partial updates
SQLite (direct or GRDB)Large relational datasets, full control, fast queriesWhen you want object graph management for free
Core DataObject graph with relationships, faulting, undo, iCloud sync (NSPersistentCloudKitContainer)Simple key-value; tiny apps
SwiftDataSame as Core Data with modern Swift API, best in SwiftUIComplex migrations, heavy background work, apps supporting older iOS
CloudKitCross-device sync using the user's iCloudCross-platform (Apple-only), server-side business logic
Realm / FirebaseCross-platform syncWhen you want zero third-party risk

Directory rules they check: Documents/ is backed up to iCloud and user-visible if you enable file sharing — put user-created content there. Library/Application Support/ for app-generated data you want backed up but hidden. Library/Caches/ is not backed up and can be purged by the system — put regenerable downloads there. tmp/ can be purged any time. Mark files isExcludedFromBackup if they're large and regenerable but must live in Documents.

var url = cacheFileURL
var values = URLResourceValues(); values.isExcludedFromBackup = true
try url.setResourceValues(values)

App Review rejects apps that store large regenerable downloads in Documents — a nice detail to mention.

P0

Core Data: the stack, contexts, and threading rules

final class CoreDataStack {
    static let shared = CoreDataStack()

    lazy var container: NSPersistentContainer = {
        let c = NSPersistentContainer(name: "Model")
        c.persistentStoreDescriptions.first?.setOption(true as NSNumber,
            forKey: NSPersistentHistoryTrackingKey)
        c.loadPersistentStores { _, error in
            if let error { fatalError("Store failed: \(error)") }
        }
        c.viewContext.automaticallyMergesChangesFromParent = true
        c.viewContext.mergePolicy = NSMergeByPropertyObjectTrumpMergePolicy
        return c
    }()

    var viewContext: NSManagedObjectContext { container.viewContext }

    func performBackgroundTask<T>(_ block: @escaping (NSManagedObjectContext) throws -> T) async throws -> T {
        try await withCheckedThrowingContinuation { cont in
            container.performBackgroundTask { ctx in
                ctx.mergePolicy = NSMergeByPropertyObjectTrumpMergePolicy
                do { let r = try block(ctx); if ctx.hasChanges { try ctx.save() }; cont.resume(returning: r) }
                catch { cont.resume(throwing: error) }
            }
        }
    }
}

The threading rule — say this precisely: NSManagedObject and NSManagedObjectContext are not thread-safe. A context is bound to the queue it was created with. Always access a context inside perform/performAndWait (or the async perform in iOS 15+). Never pass a managed object between contexts — pass its NSManagedObjectID and re-fetch with context.object(with:).

let objectID = product.objectID
try await stack.performBackgroundTask { ctx in
    let bgProduct = ctx.object(with: objectID) as! ProductMO
    bgProduct.isFavourite = true
}

The pieces of the stack: NSManagedObjectModel (the schema), NSPersistentStoreCoordinator (mediates between contexts and stores), NSPersistentStore (SQLite file), NSManagedObjectContext (scratchpad of objects), NSPersistentContainer (wires all of the above up for you).

Concurrency types: .mainQueueConcurrencyType (the view context, for UI) and .privateQueueConcurrencyType (background). Child contexts push changes to their parent on save, not to disk.

P0

Core Data performance — what do you actually tune?

let request = ProductMO.fetchRequest()
request.predicate = NSPredicate(format: "category == %@ AND price < %@", category, NSNumber(value: 100))
request.sortDescriptors = [NSSortDescriptor(keyPath: \ProductMO.name, ascending: true)]
request.fetchBatchSize = 50               // page results instead of loading everything
request.fetchLimit = 200
request.relationshipKeyPathsForPrefetching = ["category"]   // avoid N+1 faults
request.returnsObjectsAsFaults = false    // fully materialise if you'll touch all fields
request.propertiesToFetch = ["name", "price"]               // with resultType = .dictionaryResultType

The big ones, in order of typical impact:

  1. fetchBatchSize — turns one giant fetch into lazy pages. For a table view, set it to about 2× the visible rows.
  2. Prefetch relationships — without it, iterating 200 products and reading product.category.name fires 200 separate faults (the Core Data N+1).
  3. Batch operations — NSBatchInsertRequest, NSBatchUpdateRequest, NSBatchDeleteRequest run in SQL without loading objects into memory. Note they bypass the context, so you must merge the changes back:
let delete = NSBatchDeleteRequest(fetchRequest: request)
delete.resultType = .resultTypeObjectIDs
let result = try ctx.execute(delete) as! NSBatchDeleteResult
let changes = [NSDeletedObjectsKey: result.result as! [NSManagedObjectID]]
NSManagedObjectContext.mergeChanges(fromRemoteContextSave: changes, into: [viewContext])
  1. Indexes on any attribute you filter or sort by (set in the model editor, or NSFetchIndexDescription).
  2. NSFetchedResultsController for table/collection views — it does batched fetching, section grouping, and change tracking with animated diffs for free.
  3. Reset the context periodically in long-running imports (ctx.reset()) so the row cache doesn't grow without bound.
  4. Store binary data externally — tick "Allows External Storage" so large blobs live as files, not in the SQLite row.

Debug flag to mention: -com.apple.CoreData.SQLDebug 1 in the scheme arguments prints the actual SQL, which is how you find the N+1.

P0

Core Data migrations

Lightweight migration handles most changes automatically — adding/removing attributes and entities, renaming (via the "Renaming ID" in the model inspector), and changing optionality with a default.

let description = container.persistentStoreDescriptions.first!
description.shouldMigrateStoreAutomatically = true
description.shouldInferMappingModelAutomatically = true

Heavyweight migration is needed when you must transform data (splitting one entity into two, changing a type, deriving a value). You create a mapping model and optionally an NSEntityMigrationPolicy subclass.

Staged migrations (iOS 17+) are the modern answer and worth naming:

let stages = NSStagedMigrationManager(stages: [
    NSCustomMigrationStage(migratingFrom: v1, to: v2),
    NSLightweightMigrationStage([v3Checksum])
])
description.setOption(stages, forKey: NSPersistentStoreStagedMigrationManagerOptionKey)

Rules to state: always version the model (never edit a shipped version in place); test migration from every shipped version, not just the previous one; migration happens on loadPersistentStores, so it can block launch — do it with a progress UI for big stores; and always have a fallback (delete and re-sync) so a failed migration doesn't brick the app.

P0

SwiftData vs Core Data — which would you choose in 2026?

@Model
final class Trip {
    #Unique<Trip>([\.id])
    #Index<Trip>([\.startDate])
    var id: UUID
    var name: String
    var startDate: Date
    @Relationship(deleteRule: .cascade, inverse: \Stop.trip) var stops: [Stop] = []
    @Transient var isSelected = false
    init(id: UUID = .init(), name: String, startDate: Date) { ... }
}

// Background work
let container = try ModelContainer(for: Trip.self)
@ModelActor actor TripImporter {
    func importTrips(_ dtos: [TripDTO]) throws {
        for dto in dtos { modelContext.insert(Trip(name: dto.name, startDate: dto.date)) }
        try modelContext.save()
    }
}

What SwiftData gives you: declarative schema in Swift with no .xcdatamodeld editor, @Model types that are observable out of the box, @Query in SwiftUI with automatic updates, type-safe #Predicate checked at compile time, @ModelActor for background work, and free CloudKit sync.

Why teams still pick Core Data in 2026:

  • Migration control. SwiftData's SchemaMigrationPlan is much less flexible than mapping models + migration policies for complex transformations.
  • NSFetchedResultsController has no exact SwiftData equivalent with the same performance characteristics for very large tables.
  • Deployment target — SwiftData needs iOS 17+, and many apps still support 16.
  • Debuggability. Core Data has SQLDebug, a mature instrument, and 15 years of Stack Overflow. SwiftData failures are often opaque.
  • #Predicate limitations — not every predicate expressible in NSPredicate compiles, particularly across relationships and with string operations.
  • Both use the same SQLite store under the hood, and they interoperate, so you can migrate incrementally.

My answer: SwiftData for a new SwiftUI-only app targeting iOS 17+ with a simple model; Core Data for anything with complex migrations, heavy import pipelines, or an existing store. And for a lot of apps the honest answer is neither — GRDB (a thin, fast SQLite wrapper with excellent concurrency and observation) is a better fit when you want SQL and predictable performance.

P1

Keychain — save, read, update, delete

enum Keychain {
    static func save(_ data: Data, service: String, account: String) throws {
        let query: [String: Any] = [
            kSecClass as String: kSecClassGenericPassword,
            kSecAttrService as String: service,
            kSecAttrAccount as String: account,
            kSecValueData as String: data,
            kSecAttrAccessible as String: kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly
        ]
        SecItemDelete(query as CFDictionary)              // simplest "upsert"
        let status = SecItemAdd(query as CFDictionary, nil)
        guard status == errSecSuccess else { throw KeychainError.status(status) }
    }

    static func read(service: String, account: String) throws -> Data? {
        let query: [String: Any] = [
            kSecClass as String: kSecClassGenericPassword,
            kSecAttrService as String: service,
            kSecAttrAccount as String: account,
            kSecReturnData as String: true,
            kSecMatchLimit as String: kSecMatchLimitOne
        ]
        var item: CFTypeRef?
        let status = SecItemCopyMatching(query as CFDictionary, &item)
        if status == errSecItemNotFound { return nil }
        guard status == errSecSuccess else { throw KeychainError.status(status) }
        return item as? Data
    }
}

Accessibility constants — know these: kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlocked (default, needs device unlocked), ...AfterFirstUnlock (available to background tasks after first unlock post-boot — what you want for background refresh), ...WhenPasscodeSetThisDeviceOnly (strictest; item is deleted if the passcode is removed), and the ThisDeviceOnly suffix which prevents the item from being restored to a different device from a backup.

The gotcha everyone hits: Keychain items survive app deletion (they're tied to the keychain access group, not the app bundle). So a reinstalled app can find a stale token from a previous install. Fix: on first launch after install (detect with a UserDefaults flag, which is wiped), clear your keychain items.

Keychain access groups let an app and its extensions/App Clip share items — configured via entitlements.

P1

UserDefaults — what can go wrong?

  • It's a plist read into memory at launch — large values slow down launch and stay resident.
  • Not encrypted. Anyone with a backup or a jailbroken device reads it. Never store tokens.
  • Writes are asynchronous to disk; synchronize() is deprecated and unnecessary.
  • register(defaults:) sets defaults without writing them to disk — the right way to provide fallbacks.
  • Suites (UserDefaults(suiteName:)) with an App Group are how you share with extensions and widgets.
  • KVO / NSUserDefaultsDidChangeNotification for observation; @AppStorage in SwiftUI.
UserDefaults.standard.register(defaults: ["theme": "system", "pageSize": 20])
let shared = UserDefaults(suiteName: "group.com.app.shared")!
P1

How would you implement offline-first sync?

Structure the answer as: local store is the source of truth for the UI, the network is a background reconciler.

  1. Every entity has a server ID, a local ID, an updatedAt, and a sync state (synced, pendingCreate, pendingUpdate, pendingDelete).
  2. Writes go to the local store immediately and enqueue an operation in an outbox table. The UI updates instantly (optimistic).
  3. A sync engine drains the outbox with retry/backoff, and pulls changes since a server cursor/updatedAt watermark.
  4. Conflict resolution — pick and justify: last-write-wins by server timestamp (simple, loses data), server-wins, client-wins, field-level merge, or CRDTs (correct but heavy). For most apps: last-write-wins with a server-authoritative clock, plus a conflict flag on entities where the user must decide.
  5. Idempotency — every outbox operation carries a client-generated UUID so a retried create doesn't duplicate.
  6. Tombstones for deletes, so a delete on device A propagates instead of being resurrected by device B's stale copy.
struct OutboxItem: Codable, Identifiable {
    let id: UUID                    // idempotency key
    let entity: String
    let entityID: String
    let operation: Operation        // .create/.update/.delete
    let payload: Data
    var attempts: Int
    var nextAttemptAt: Date
}

Mention the platform mechanics: BGAppRefreshTask / BGProcessingTask to sync in the background, silent push to trigger a pull, and NSPersistentHistoryTracking if you're on Core Data so extensions and the app agree on what changed.

P2

NSFetchedResultsController with diffable data source

lazy var frc: NSFetchedResultsController<ProductMO> = {
    let request = ProductMO.fetchRequest()
    request.sortDescriptors = [NSSortDescriptor(keyPath: \ProductMO.category, ascending: true),
                               NSSortDescriptor(keyPath: \ProductMO.name, ascending: true)]
    request.fetchBatchSize = 40
    let c = NSFetchedResultsController(fetchRequest: request,
                                       managedObjectContext: viewContext,
                                       sectionNameKeyPath: #keyPath(ProductMO.category),
                                       cacheName: nil)
    c.delegate = self
    return c
}()

extension VC: NSFetchedResultsControllerDelegate {
    func controller(_ c: NSFetchedResultsController<NSFetchRequestResult>,
                    didChangeContentWith snapshot: NSDiffableDataSourceSnapshotReference) {
        dataSource.apply(snapshot as NSDiffableDataSourceSnapshot<String, NSManagedObjectID>,
                         animatingDifferences: true)
    }
}

Note sectionNameKeyPath must match the first sort descriptor's key, or you get a runtime exception — a favourite gotcha. And when using the snapshot delegate, items are NSManagedObjectIDs, so your cell provider re-fetches the object.

P2

File I/O: FileManager, atomic writes, and coordination

let docs = FileManager.default.urls(for: .documentDirectory, in: .userDomainMask)[0]
let url = docs.appendingPathComponent("cache.json")

try data.write(to: url, options: [.atomic, .completeFileProtection])
let read = try Data(contentsOf: url, options: .mappedIfSafe)   // mmap for large files

try FileManager.default.createDirectory(at: dir, withIntermediateDirectories: true)
let exists = FileManager.default.fileExists(atPath: url.path(percentEncoded: false))
try FileManager.default.removeItem(at: url)

Points: .atomic writes to a temp file then renames, so a crash mid-write can't corrupt the file. .mappedIfSafe memory-maps instead of loading — essential for large files. Data protection classes (.completeFileProtection, .completeUnlessOpen, .completeUntilFirstUserAuthentication, .none) control encryption at rest; a background task reading a .complete file while the device is locked will fail — that's a real bug and a great thing to mention.

NSFileCoordinator/NSFilePresenter for files shared with other processes (App Groups, Files app, iCloud Documents).

P2

Designing a disk cache with an LRU policy

actor DiskCache {
    private let directory: URL
    private let maxBytes: Int
    private let fm = FileManager.default

    func value(for key: String) throws -> Data? {
        let url = url(for: key)
        guard fm.fileExists(atPath: url.path) else { return nil }
        try? fm.setAttributes([.modificationDate: Date()], ofItemAtPath: url.path)  // touch = LRU
        return try Data(contentsOf: url, options: .mappedIfSafe)
    }

    func store(_ data: Data, for key: String) throws {
        try data.write(to: url(for: key), options: .atomic)
        try trimIfNeeded()
    }

    private func trimIfNeeded() throws {
        let keys: [URLResourceKey] = [.contentModificationDateKey, .fileSizeKey]
        var files = try fm.contentsOfDirectory(at: directory, includingPropertiesForKeys: keys)
            .compactMap { url -> (URL, Date, Int)? in
                let v = try? url.resourceValues(forKeys: Set(keys))
                guard let d = v?.contentModificationDate, let s = v?.fileSize else { return nil }
                return (url, d, s)
            }
        var total = files.reduce(0) { $0 + $1.2 }
        guard total > maxBytes else { return }
        files.sort { $0.1 < $1.1 }                 // oldest first
        for (url, _, size) in files where total > maxBytes {
            try? fm.removeItem(at: url); total -= size
        }
    }

    private func url(for key: String) -> URL {
        directory.appendingPathComponent(SHA256.hash(data: Data(key.utf8)).hexString)
    }
}

Mention: hash the key so arbitrary URLs become safe filenames; put it in Library/Caches; and pair it with an in-memory NSCache for a two-tier cache.

P3

NSPersistentCloudKitContainer and CloudKit sync

let container = NSPersistentCloudKitContainer(name: "Model")
let desc = container.persistentStoreDescriptions.first!
desc.setOption(true as NSNumber, forKey: NSPersistentHistoryTrackingKey)
desc.setOption(true as NSNumber, forKey: NSPersistentStoreRemoteChangeNotificationPostOptionKey)
desc.cloudKitContainerOptions = .init(containerIdentifier: "iCloud.com.app")

Constraints worth naming: every relationship must have an inverse; attributes can't be non-optional without a default (CloudKit has no NOT NULL); no @Attribute(.unique) constraints; entities can't use NSMerge... uniqueness — you deduplicate manually; and initial sync can be slow with no progress API. It's magic when it works and painful when it doesn't.

P3

Encryption at rest beyond file protection

import CryptoKit
let key = SymmetricKey(size: .bits256)                        // store in Keychain
let sealed = try AES.GCM.seal(plaintext, using: key)
let ciphertext = sealed.combined!
let decrypted = try AES.GCM.open(.init(combined: ciphertext), using: key)

For an encrypted database, SQLCipher or a Realm encryption key. Say that iOS file protection already encrypts at rest when the device has a passcode, so app-level encryption is about protecting against backup extraction and jailbroken devices, and the key must live in the Keychain with ThisDeviceOnly accessibility — otherwise you've just moved the problem.
