Testing

14 questions
P0

Swift Testing vs XCTest — know both

Swift Testing is the modern framework (shipped with Xcode 16, standard by 2026). XCTest is still required for UI tests and performance tests, so real projects run both side by side in the same target.

import Testing
@testable import MyApp

@Suite("Cart")
struct CartTests {

    @Test func emptyCartTotalsZero() {
        let cart = Cart()
        #expect(cart.total == 0)
    }

    @Test("Totals sum line items",
          arguments: [([10, 5], 15), ([1, 1, 1], 3), ([], 0)])
    func totals(prices: [Decimal], expected: Decimal) {
        let cart = Cart(items: prices.map(Item.init(price:)))
        #expect(cart.total == expected)
    }

    @Test func removingMissingItemThrows() {
        let cart = Cart()
        #expect(throws: CartError.notFound) { try cart.remove(id: "nope") }
    }

    @Test func requiredValueUnwraps() throws {
        let item = try #require(Cart(items: [.sample]).items.first)   // stops the test if nil
        #expect(item.price > 0)
    }

    @Test(.disabled("Flaky on CI — FB12345"))
    func flaky() {}

    @Test(.timeLimit(.minutes(1)))
    func slow() async throws { }

    @Test(.tags(.critical)) func checkout() {}
}

Mapping from XCTest:

XCTestSwift Testing
XCTestCase subclassstruct (or final class/actor) with @Suite
func testX()@Test func x()
XCTAssertEqual(a, b)#expect(a == b)
XCTAssertTrue/False/Nil#expect(...) — one macro for all
XCTUnwraptry #require(...)
XCTAssertThrowsError#expect(throws: Error.self) { }
setUp/tearDowninit() / deinit
XCTestExpectationconfirmation { }
XCTSkipwithKnownIssue / .disabled
serial by defaultparallel by default

#expect vs #require: #expect records the failure and keeps going; #require throws and aborts the test. Use #require when everything after depends on it — this is the guard-let of testing. The macros capture the expression, so a failure prints Expectation failed: (cart.total → 15) == (expected → 20), not just "XCTAssertEqual failed" — a genuine ergonomic win.

Things that catch people out: Swift Testing runs tests in parallel and in random order by default, so shared mutable state across tests breaks. Use .serialized on a suite when you truly need order. A new struct suite instance is created per test, which is why init replaces setUp and state can't leak.

@Suite(.serialized) struct DatabaseTests { }

Async testing:

@Test func loadsProducts() async throws {
    let sut = ProductViewModel(repo: MockRepo(products: [.sample]))
    await sut.load()
    #expect(sut.state == .loaded([.sample]))
}

@Test func emitsThreeEvents() async {
    await confirmation(expectedCount: 3) { confirmed in
        let stream = service.events()
        for await _ in stream.prefix(3) { confirmed() }
    }
}
P0

How do you make code testable? Show a mock, a stub, a spy, and a fake

protocol AnalyticsTracking: Sendable { func track(_ event: String, properties: [String: String]) }
protocol ProductRepository: Sendable { func products() async throws -> [Product] }

// STUB — returns canned data, no assertions
struct StubRepository: ProductRepository {
    var result: Result<[Product], Error> = .success([])
    func products() async throws -> [Product] { try result.get() }
}

// SPY — records what happened so the test can assert on it
final class AnalyticsSpy: AnalyticsTracking, @unchecked Sendable {
    private(set) var events: [(String, [String: String])] = []
    private let lock = NSLock()
    func track(_ event: String, properties: [String: String]) {
        lock.withLock { events.append((event, properties)) }
    }
}

// FAKE — a real, simplified implementation
actor InMemoryRepository: ProductRepository {
    private var storage: [Product]
    init(_ p: [Product]) { storage = p }
    func products() async throws -> [Product] { storage }
    func add(_ p: Product) { storage.append(p) }
}

// MOCK — pre-programmed expectations that fail the test if unmet
final class RepositoryMock: ProductRepository {
    var expectedCallCount = 1
    private(set) var callCount = 0
    func products() async throws -> [Product] { callCount += 1; return [] }
    func verify(sourceLocation: SourceLocation = #_sourceLocation) {
        #expect(callCount == expectedCallCount, sourceLocation: sourceLocation)
    }
}

The definitions interviewers want: a stub provides answers; a spy records calls; a mock has built-in expectations and verifies them; a fake is a working lightweight implementation; a dummy is a placeholder that's never used. Knowing the difference and using the right word is a strong signal.

The struct-of-closures alternative — less boilerplate, override only what a test needs:

struct ProductRepo: Sendable {
    var products: @Sendable () async throws -> [Product] = { [] }
}
let sut = ViewModel(repo: ProductRepo(products: { throw URLError(.timedOut) }))
P0

How do you test async code and code with actors?

// 1. Plain await — the usual case
@Test func fetchesAndMaps() async throws {
    let sut = Loader(client: StubClient(json: fixture))
    let items = try await sut.load()
    #expect(items.count == 3)
}

// 2. Testing an actor — just await into it
@Test func cacheCoalescesConcurrentRequests() async throws {
    let counter = CallCounter()
    let sut = ImageDownloader(fetch: { url in await counter.increment(); return .sample })

    async let a = sut.image(for: url)
    async let b = sut.image(for: url)
    _ = try await (a, b)

    #expect(await counter.value == 1)    // one network call for two callers
}

// 3. Testing MainActor code
@Suite @MainActor struct ViewModelTests {
    @Test func setsLoading() async {
        let sut = ViewModel(repo: SlowRepo())
        let task = Task { await sut.load() }
        #expect(sut.state == .loading)
        await task.value
    }
}

// 4. Deterministic time — inject the clock, never sleep in a test
protocol ClockProtocol: Sendable { func sleep(for: Duration) async throws }
struct ImmediateClock: ClockProtocol { func sleep(for: Duration) async throws {} }
// production passes ContinuousClock(); tests pass ImmediateClock()

Rules to state: never use Thread.sleep or arbitrary Task.sleep waits to "let it settle" — inject a clock or use confirmation. Never assert on timing. And for anything that polls, expose a completion signal rather than testing the side effect with a delay. Flaky tests are worse than no tests; be vocal about that.

P0

What is the testing pyramid, and what do you actually test in an iOS app?

Unit tests (the base, ~70%): pure logic — view models, mappers, formatters, validation, reducers, caching policy, date maths. Fast (milliseconds), no UIKit, no network, no disk.

Integration tests (~20%): the network layer against URLProtocol stubs, the persistence layer against an in-memory Core Data store, the repository combining both.

func makeInMemoryContainer() -> NSPersistentContainer {
    let c = NSPersistentContainer(name: "Model")
    let d = NSPersistentStoreDescription()
    d.type = NSInMemoryStoreType             // or url = /dev/null for SQLite semantics
    c.persistentStoreDescriptions = [d]
    c.loadPersistentStores { _, e in precondition(e == nil) }
    return c
}

UI tests (the tip, ~10%): only the critical user journeys — sign in, add to cart, checkout. They're slow and flaky, so keep them few and make them count.

What I don't test: trivial getters, SwiftUI body structure (brittle), third-party code, and generated code.

P1

Write a UI test, and make it not flaky

final class CheckoutUITests: XCTestCase {
    var app: XCUIApplication!

    override func setUpWithError() throws {
        continueAfterFailure = false
        app = XCUIApplication()
        app.launchArguments = ["-UITests", "-AppleLanguages", "(en)"]
        app.launchEnvironment = ["STUB_API": "checkout_happy_path"]   // deterministic backend
        app.launch()
    }

    func testAddToCartAndCheckout() throws {
        app.buttons["product_cell_0"].tap()
        let addButton = app.buttons["add_to_cart"]
        XCTAssertTrue(addButton.waitForExistence(timeout: 5))
        addButton.tap()

        app.tabBars.buttons["Cart"].tap()
        XCTAssertEqual(app.staticTexts["cart_total"].label, "$19.99")

        app.buttons["checkout"].tap()
        let confirmation = app.staticTexts["order_confirmed"]
        XCTAssertTrue(confirmation.waitForExistence(timeout: 10))
    }
}

Anti-flake rules — say all of these:

  1. Use accessibility identifiers, never localized labels or index paths.
  2. Always waitForExistence(timeout:); never sleep().
  3. Stub the network — launch with an argument that makes the app use a local stub server or URLProtocol fixtures. A UI test hitting a real backend is a test of your backend's uptime.
  4. Reset state at launch (-UITests wipes the database, signs out, disables animations).
  5. UIView.setAnimationsEnabled(false) in the app under -UITests.
  6. Use XCTExpectation + XCTNSPredicateExpectation for anything not covered by waitForExistence.
  7. Run them on a fixed simulator/OS in CI, and quarantine flaky tests rather than retrying blindly.

XCUITest extras worth naming: app.swipeUp(), element.press(forDuration:thenDragTo:), XCUIDevice.shared.orientation, addUIInterruptionMonitor for system alerts (permissions), app.launchArguments for injection, and XCTAttachment screenshots on failure.

P1

Snapshot testing — what it's good for and what it isn't

import SnapshotTesting

func testProductRowAppearance() {
    let view = ProductRow(product: .sample)
    assertSnapshot(of: UIHostingController(rootView: view),
                   as: .image(on: .iPhone13, traits: .init(userInterfaceStyle: .dark)))
    assertSnapshot(of: UIHostingController(rootView: view),
                   as: .image(on: .iPhone13, traits: .init(preferredContentSizeCategory: .accessibilityLarge)))
}

Good for: design-system components, catching unintended visual regressions across Dark Mode / Dynamic Type / RTL, and documenting what a component looks like.

Bad at: logic, and anything that changes legitimately every sprint. Costs: binary files in the repo, failures on new OS versions/simulators when rendering shifts a pixel, and a temptation to "just re-record" which silently accepts regressions.

Practical advice: pin the simulator and OS in CI, record on one device model only, and review re-recorded snapshots in the PR like any other diff.

P1

What is code coverage good for, and what's a good number?

Coverage tells you what was executed, not what was verified — a test with no assertions gives 100% coverage. So the honest answer is: I use coverage to find untested areas, not as a target.

Practical targets: 70–85% on domain/view-model/mapping code; low coverage on view code is fine and expected. I'd rather have 60% coverage with meaningful assertions than 90% of exercised-but-unasserted lines.

xcodebuild test -scheme App -destination 'platform=iOS Simulator,name=iPhone 16' \
  -enableCodeCoverage YES -resultBundlePath Result.xcresult
xcrun xccov view --report --json Result.xcresult > coverage.json

Say what you'd enforce: coverage must not decrease in a PR (a ratchet), rather than an absolute floor that people game.

P1

TDD — do you use it, and how do you answer this honestly?

Don't claim you TDD everything — interviewers don't believe it. A credible answer:

"I use TDD where it genuinely pays: pure logic with clear inputs and outputs — pricing rules, date calculations, parsers, state reducers, retry policies. Writing the test first forces me to design the interface from the caller's side and I end up with smaller, injectable types. For UI work and exploratory integration I write the code first and add tests once the shape settles, then I make sure the regression case is covered. What I always do is write a failing test for every bug before fixing it — that's non-negotiable, because it proves the fix and prevents the regression."

Red → Green → Refactor, and the three laws, are worth being able to recite.

P2

Testing legacy code that has no seams

The techniques, named:

  1. Extract and override — pull the untestable bit into a method, subclass in the test and override it.
  2. Parameterise the constructor — add an initializer with defaults so production code is unchanged but tests can inject.
  3. Extract a protocol around a concrete dependency (URLSession → HTTPClient).
  4. Wrap a singleton behind a protocol and pass .shared in production.
  5. Characterisation tests — write tests that capture current behaviour (even if it's wrong) so you can refactor safely, then fix.
  6. Seam via #if DEBUG as a last resort — exposing internals for tests. @testable import gives you internal access without this.
final class LegacyService {
    private let session: URLSessionProtocol
    init(session: URLSessionProtocol = URLSession.shared) { self.session = session }  // seam added, callers unchanged
}
P2

How do you test @Observable / SwiftUI view models?

@Observable classes are plain classes, so they test like any other object. The only subtlety is observing changes:

@Test @MainActor func observationFires() async {
    let model = CartModel(api: .stub)
    var changed = false
    withObservationTracking {
        _ = model.items                       // register a read
    } onChange: {
        changed = true
    }
    model.items = [.sample]
    #expect(changed)
}

withObservationTracking's onChange fires once, before the value actually changes (it's willSet semantics) — a detail that trips people up. For repeated observation you re-register, or use the Observations async sequence in newer SDKs.

P2

Testing push notifications, deep links, and background tasks

// Deep links — test the parser, not the OS
@Test func parsesProductDeepLink() throws {
    let route = try #require(Route(url: URL(string: "myapp://product/123?ref=push")!))
    #expect(route == .product(id: "123"))
}

// Simulator push
// $ xcrun simctl push booted com.app.bundle payload.apns

// Background task — you cannot wait for the system; force it from the debugger:
// (lldb) e -l objc -- (void)[[BGTaskScheduler sharedScheduler]
//        _simulateLaunchForTaskWithIdentifier:@"com.app.refresh"]

The interview point: extract all the logic (URL parsing, payload decoding, what work to do) into pure functions you can unit test, and leave the OS integration as a thin, manually-verified shell.

P2

CI setup for tests — what does a good pipeline run?

# .github/workflows/ci.yml (sketch)
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: macos-15
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - name: Select Xcode
        run: sudo xcode-select -s /Applications/Xcode_26.app
      - name: Build & test
        run: |
          set -o pipefail
          xcodebuild test \
            -scheme App \
            -destination 'platform=iOS Simulator,name=iPhone 16,OS=26.0' \
            -resultBundlePath Result.xcresult \
            -enableCodeCoverage YES \
            -parallel-testing-enabled YES \
            | xcbeautify
      - name: Lint
        run: swiftlint --strict

What to say runs on every PR: build, unit + integration tests, SwiftLint/SwiftFormat, and coverage ratchet. What runs nightly: UI tests, snapshot tests, Thread Sanitizer, a build with strict concurrency, and a release-configuration build. What runs on tag: archive, sign, upload to TestFlight via fastlane.

P3

Property-based and fuzz testing

@Test(arguments: (0..<1000).map { _ in Int.random(in: -10_000...10_000) })
func absIsNonNegative(n: Int) {
    #expect(abs(n) >= 0)
}

// Round-trip property — very high value for Codable
@Test(arguments: Product.randomSamples(200))
func codableRoundTrips(product: Product) throws {
    let data = try JSONEncoder().encode(product)
    #expect(try JSONDecoder().decode(Product.self, from: data) == product)
}

Round-trip properties (encode→decode, parse→format, compress→decompress) are the highest-value property tests in an iOS codebase and cost almost nothing.

P3

Performance tests and preventing regressions

func testParsePerformance() {
    let data = largeFixture
    measure(metrics: [XCTClockMetric(), XCTMemoryMetric(), XCTCPUMetric()]) {
        _ = try! JSONDecoder().decode([Product].self, from: data)
    }
}

func testLaunchPerformance() {
    measure(metrics: [XCTApplicationLaunchMetric()]) {
        XCUIApplication().launch()
    }
}

measure records a baseline you accept in Xcode; subsequent runs fail if they regress beyond the threshold. Say honestly that these are noisy on shared CI machines — pin them to a dedicated runner or treat them as trend data rather than gates, and use MetricKit and Xcode Organizer for real-world launch/hang/memory data from production, which is more trustworthy than any simulator benchmark.

10

Performance, Debugging & Instruments

14 questions
P0

The app scrolls with jank. Walk me through how you fix it.

Structure this as measure → find → fix → verify. Never start with "I'd cache things".

1. Reproduce and measure. Run on the oldest supported physical device in Release configuration — the simulator has your Mac's CPU and GPU and will hide the problem entirely.

2. Identify whether it's CPU or GPU bound.

  • Instruments → Animation Hitches (or the Core Animation template) gives you hitch time per frame and tells you whether it's a commit (CPU) or render (GPU) problem.
  • Simulator/device debug options: Color Blended Layers (red = expensive blending), Color Offscreen-Rendered Yellow, Color Misaligned Images.

3. Common causes, roughly in frequency order:

SymptomCauseFix
Spikes when new cells appearImage decode on the main threadDecode/downsample off-main; prepareForDisplay()
Steady low frame rateToo many blended/offscreen layersOpaque backgrounds, shadowPath, avoid masksToBounds + cornerRadius on complex content
Spikes on data reloadreloadData() on every changeDiffable data source; reconfigureItems
Random stallsSynchronous disk/network on mainMove off main; os_signpost to confirm
Slow cell configAuto Layout solving a large system per cellFlatten the hierarchy, fewer nested stack views, cache heights
Slow first appearanceDate/number formatters created per rowCreate once; formatters are expensive (~ms each)
Jank while typingWork on every keystrokeDebounce

4. Verify with numbers. "Hitch ratio went from 42 ms/s to 3 ms/s on an iPhone SE" is what makes the answer credible.

The one-liner that impresses: "60 fps means 16.67 ms per frame, and the main thread has to do layout, Core Animation commit, and my code in that budget — so anything synchronous and unbounded on the main thread is the suspect list." (120 Hz ProMotion: 8.3 ms.)

P0

App launch time — what happens, and how do you improve it?

The phases:

  1. Pre-main: the dynamic linker (dyld) maps the executable, loads and links dynamic frameworks, performs rebasing/binding, runs ObjC +load methods and C++ static initialisers.
  2. main() → didFinishLaunching: your app delegate work.
  3. First frame: root view controller construction, first layout, first render.

Target: under 400 ms to first frame is Apple's guidance; the watchdog kills you around 20 s.

How to measure:

  • DYLD_PRINT_STATISTICS=1 (or DYLD_PRINT_STATISTICS_DETAILS=1) in the scheme environment prints pre-main timings.
  • Instruments → App Launch template gives you the full breakdown with signposts.
  • Xcode Organizer → Launch Time gives you real-world p50/p90 from actual users. This is the number that matters.
  • MetricKit's MXAppLaunchMetric.

Fixes, ordered by typical payoff:

  1. Fewer dynamic frameworks. Each dylib costs measurable load time. Link statically (SPM static libraries, mergeable libraries in Xcode 15+) — going from 40 dylibs to 5 is often the single biggest win.
  2. Defer everything non-essential out of didFinishLaunching — analytics SDKs, remote config, crash reporters that don't need to be first, prefetching. Move them to after the first frame or to a background task.
  3. Don't do disk or network I/O synchronously at launch. A big UserDefaults plist or a Core Data migration blocks you.
  4. Lazy-load the object graph. Don't construct every service in the composition root; use lazy or factories.
  5. Avoid +load and static initialisers; use +initialize or lazy statics.
  6. Reduce the first screen's complexity — render a cheap skeleton, then fill it.
  7. Order file linking / use profile-guided optimisation for very large apps.
P0

Which Instruments do you use, and for what?

  • Time Profiler — where CPU time goes. Sample-based. Turn on "Hide System Libraries" + "Invert Call Tree" to find your hot leaves. The single most useful instrument.
  • Allocations — object counts and sizes over time; Mark Generation for abandoned memory.
  • Leaks — retain cycles only.
  • Animation Hitches / Core Animation — frame timing, commit vs render.
  • Time Profiler + Points of Interest (os_signpost) — instrument your own phases:
import OSLog
let signposter = OSSignposter(subsystem: "com.app", category: "Feed")

func loadFeed() async {
    let state = signposter.beginInterval("loadFeed")
    defer { signposter.endInterval("loadFeed", state) }
    ...
}
  • Network — request waterfall, payload sizes, connection reuse.
  • Energy Log / CPU Profiler — battery drain, wakeups.
  • System Trace / Thread State — for blocking, priority inversion, thread explosion.
  • SwiftUI template — View Body count, View Properties, Core Animation commits. The fastest way to find "this view rebuilds 400 times".
  • File Activity — synchronous I/O on main.
  • Thread Sanitizer / Address Sanitizer / UBSan — correctness, via Scheme → Diagnostics.
P0

The app is using too much memory. What do you do?

Chapter 2 covers the tooling. The performance-specific checklist:

  1. Images — downsample to display size; a full-res photo in a 60 pt thumbnail is ~800× the pixels needed. Use preparingThumbnail(of:) / CGImageSourceCreateThumbnailAtIndex.
  2. Caches — NSCache with totalCostLimit, not a Dictionary. Clear on memory warning.
  3. Core Data — fetchBatchSize, ctx.reset() in import loops, batch operations instead of loading objects.
  4. Video/audio — release AVPlayerItems; don't hold decoded frames.
  5. Retained view controllers — see Chapter 2.
  6. Large Data — use .mappedIfSafe so the OS can page it out instead of holding it in dirty memory.
  7. Know the terms: dirty memory (written by your app, counts fully against you), clean memory (can be evicted and reloaded — e.g. mapped files), compressed memory (iOS compresses inactive dirty pages instead of swapping). Footprint = dirty + compressed, and that's what Jetsam looks at.
P1

Build times are terrible. What do you do?

Measure first:

# Per-file timings
defaults write com.apple.dt.Xcode ShowBuildOperationDuration -bool YES
# Slow function bodies and type-checks
OTHER_SWIFT_FLAGS = -Xfrontend -warn-long-function-bodies=100 \
                    -Xfrontend -warn-long-expression-type-checking=100
# Full timeline
xcodebuild -scheme App -destination ... build \
  -resultBundlePath Build.xcresult

Fixes:

  1. Break up complex expressions. A single long chain of + on mixed numeric types can take seconds to type-check. Add explicit types and split the expression. This is often the single biggest Swift-specific win.
  2. Add explicit types to properties and closure returns, especially in large literal arrays/dictionaries.
  3. Modularise so incremental builds recompile one module, not everything.
  4. Reduce @inlinable/public surface and use internal/package, which lets the compiler skip cross-module work.
  5. final on classes that don't need subclassing — cheaper dispatch and less vtable work.
  6. Whole Module Optimisation for Release, Incremental for Debug. SWIFT_COMPILATION_MODE = singlefile (incremental) in Debug.
  7. Check build phases — a "Run Script" phase without inputFiles/outputFiles declared runs on every build and invalidates caching. This is a very common finding.
  8. Prefer SPM over CocoaPods for dependencies where possible; prebuilt binary XCFrameworks for heavy third-party code.
  9. Avoid huge storyboards, generated files, and R.swift-style code generation on every build.
  10. -Onone debug and skip dSYM generation in Debug (DEBUG_INFORMATION_FORMAT = dwarf not dwarf-with-dsym).
P1

App size — how do you reduce it?

# See what's big
xcrun bitcode_strip / otool -L App.app/App
# Or: Xcode Organizer -> App Store Connect -> App Size Report

Levers: App Thinning (app slicing per device, on-demand resources, bitcode is gone now); asset catalogs with correct scales only; HEIC/WebP instead of PNG for photos; SF Symbols instead of bundled icons; strip settings (STRIP_INSTALLED_PRODUCT = YES, Deployment Postprocessing); dead code stripping; -Osize instead of -O for code-size-sensitive targets; auditing dependencies (a single large SDK can add 10+ MB); On-Demand Resources for level packs or optional content; and removing unused localisations and old asset variants.

Also mention: the App Store cellular download limit (currently 200 MB) matters commercially — dropping under it measurably improves installs.

P1

What is a "hang" and how do you find them in production?

A hang is the main thread being blocked long enough that the UI stops responding. Apple's bands: >250 ms is a micro-hang, >500 ms is user-visible, >5 s risks a watchdog kill (0x8badf00d).

Finding them:

  • Thread Performance Checker (Scheme → Diagnostics) flags priority inversions and hangs in debug.
  • Instruments → Hangs / Time Profiler with the hang track.
  • Xcode Organizer → Hangs — real-world hang reports from users, with stack traces. This is the highest-signal source.
  • MetricKit MXHangDiagnostic.

Causes: synchronous network or disk I/O on main; DispatchQueue.sync to a busy queue; a lock held by a lower-priority thread (priority inversion); huge reloadData; JSON decoding a big payload on main; NSRegularExpression over long strings; image decode; and Core Data faults firing on the main context.

P1

Explain the main thread, run loop, and why UIKit is main-thread-only

The main thread runs a RunLoop that processes input sources (touches, timers, ports) and, at the end of each iteration, performs the Core Animation commit: layout (layoutSubviews), display (draw(_:)), prepare, and commit the layer tree to the render server.

UIKit isn't thread-safe because that would require locking every property of every view — expensive and deadlock-prone. Instead Apple made it single-threaded by contract. (Two documented exceptions: UIGraphicsImageRenderer drawing and some UIImage operations are safe off-main; and as of recent SDKs a few read-only properties are too, but don't rely on it.)

Run loop modes matter: .default vs .tracking (while scrolling) vs .common. A Timer added in .default mode stops firing while the user scrolls — a classic bug with the fix being:

RunLoop.main.add(timer, forMode: .common)
P2

Crash debugging: symbolication, dSYMs, and common crash types

Symbolication turns addresses into function names using the dSYM, matched to the binary by UUID.

# Find the UUID your crash needs
dwarfdump --uuid MyApp.app.dSYM
# Symbolicate manually
atos -o MyApp.app.dSYM/Contents/Resources/DWARF/MyApp -arch arm64 -l 0x100000000 0x1000abcde
# Or let Xcode do it
xcrun symbolicatecrash report.crash MyApp.app.dSYM > symbolicated.crash

Crash types you must recognise:

Signal / codeMeaning
EXC_BAD_ACCESS (SIGSEGV/SIGBUS)Dereferencing bad memory — over-released object, unowned after dealloc, buffer overrun
SIGABRTUncaught Objective-C/Swift exception, fatalError, failed assertion, unsatisfiable constraints in some cases
EXC_BREAKPOINT (SIGTRAP)Swift runtime trap — force-unwrapped nil, array index out of range, integer overflow, precondition failure
0x8badf00dWatchdog: "ate bad food" — the app took too long to launch/resume/terminate
0xdeadfa11User force-quit during a hang
0xc00010ffThermal kill ("cool down")
0xbaaaaaadStackshot, not a crash
EXC_RESOURCE / JetsamMemory or CPU limit exceeded

Enable DEBUG_INFORMATION_FORMAT = dwarf-with-dsym for Release, upload dSYMs to your crash reporter, and note that bitcode-recompiled builds need the dSYM from App Store Connect (mostly historical now).

For debugging: NSZombieEnabled for over-release, Address Sanitizer for memory corruption, Malloc Scribble/Guard Edges, and -com.apple.CoreData.ConcurrencyDebug 1 for Core Data threading violations.

P2

os_log, Logger, and structured logging

import OSLog

let logger = Logger(subsystem: Bundle.main.bundleIdentifier!, category: "Checkout")

logger.debug("Cart has \(items.count) items")             // not persisted, stripped in release
logger.info("Starting checkout")
logger.notice("Order \(orderID, privacy: .public) placed")
logger.error("Payment failed: \(error.localizedDescription, privacy: .private)")
logger.fault("Invariant violated")

// Read logs back from the device programmatically (iOS 15+)
let store = try OSLogStore(scope: .currentProcessIdentifier)
let entries = try store.getEntries(at: store.position(timeIntervalSinceLatestBoot: 0))

Key facts: os_log is far cheaper than print (it's lazy — the string isn't formatted unless something reads it), it's structured and filterable in Console.app, and interpolated values default to .private and show as <private> in release unless you mark them .public. Never mark PII public. print should not exist in shipped code.

P2

MetricKit — production performance data

final class MetricsSubscriber: NSObject, MXMetricManagerSubscriber {
    override init() { super.init(); MXMetricManager.shared.add(self) }

    func didReceive(_ payloads: [MXMetricPayload]) {
        for p in payloads {
            let launch = p.applicationLaunchMetrics?.histogrammedTimeToFirstDraw
            let hangs  = p.applicationResponsivenessMetrics?.histogrammedApplicationHangTime
            let memory = p.memoryMetrics?.peakMemoryUsage
            upload(p.jsonRepresentation())
        }
    }
    func didReceive(_ payloads: [MXDiagnosticPayload]) {
        // crashes, hangs, disk write exceptions, CPU exceptions — with call stacks
    }
}

Daily aggregated payloads covering launch time, hang time, memory, disk writes, CPU, animation hitches, and (in diagnostics) crash and hang stack traces. Free, privacy-preserving, and from real devices. Mentioning MetricKit in a performance answer is a strong senior marker because it shows you care about production, not just Instruments.

P2

Common Swift performance pitfalls

// 1. Array COW triggered by a stray copy
var items = bigArray
let snapshot = items          // now every mutation copies the whole buffer
items.append(x)               // O(n) per call

// 2. String concatenation in a loop — O(n²)
var s = ""
for w in words { s += w }             // bad
let s2 = words.joined()               // good

// 3. Reserving capacity
var out = [Int](); out.reserveCapacity(n)

// 4. reduce vs reduce(into:)
let bad  = items.reduce([Int]()) { $0 + [$1 * 2] }       // copies every iteration
let good = items.reduce(into: [Int]()) { $0.append($1 * 2) }

// 5. Existentials in hot loops
let shapes: [any Shape] = ...      // dynamic dispatch + possible boxing
func area<S: Shape>(_ s: [S])      // static dispatch, specialised

// 6. Formatters are expensive — never create per row
static let formatter: DateFormatter = { ... }()

// 7. `first(where:)` vs `filter { }.first` — the latter scans everything
// 8. Dictionary default subscript avoids double lookup
counts[key, default: 0] += 1
// 9. lazy for chained transforms on large sequences
// 10. @inlinable / final for cross-module hot paths
P3

Understanding the render pipeline: commit, render server, GPU

Per frame: your app runs layout → display → prepare → commit, then hands the layer tree to the render server (backboardd), a separate process, which uses the GPU to render and the display controller to scan out. Because the render server is separate, CAAnimations continue smoothly even when your main thread stalls.

Terms: hitch = a frame that misses its deadline; hitch ratio = ms of hitch per second of scrolling (Apple's target is <5 ms/s); offscreen pass = an extra render target; overdraw = the same pixel painted repeatedly (what Color Blended Layers shows).

P3

Energy and battery

Causes: frequent network requests (each radio wake keeps it powered for seconds after — batch requests!), location updates at high accuracy, background audio, timers that wake the CPU, unbounded animations, and busy-waiting.

Tools: Instruments Energy Log, Xcode Organizer → Battery Usage (real users), and os_signpost around suspicious work. Techniques: batch and coalesce network calls; use URLSession background sessions with isDiscretionary = true for non-urgent transfers; reduce location accuracy and use significant-change or region monitoring; pause work in sceneDidEnterBackground; and use .background QoS for anything the user isn't waiting on.

11

App Lifecycle, Background Work, Push & Extensions

13 questions
P0

Describe the application lifecycle and its states

States: Not running → Inactive → Active → Background → Suspended (→ terminated by the system without notice).

  • Inactive: running in the foreground but not receiving events — during a transition, an incoming call, Control Centre pull-down, or the app switcher.
  • Background: executing code but not visible. You get ~30 s by default to finish work; longer with a background mode or a beginBackgroundTask.
  • Suspended: in memory but not executing. The system can kill you here at any time with no callback. This is why you save state when you enter background, not when you're terminated.
// SceneDelegate (UIKit, iOS 13+)
func sceneWillEnterForeground(_ scene: UIScene) { }   // background -> inactive
func sceneDidBecomeActive(_ scene: UIScene) { }       // inactive -> active
func sceneWillResignActive(_ scene: UIScene) { }      // active -> inactive: pause, blur sensitive UI
func sceneDidEnterBackground(_ scene: UIScene) { }    // save state NOW
func sceneDidDisconnect(_ scene: UIScene) { }         // scene released

// AppDelegate keeps process-level duties
func application(_:didFinishLaunchingWithOptions:) -> Bool
func application(_:didRegisterForRemoteNotificationsWithDeviceToken:)
func applicationDidReceiveMemoryWarning(_:)
func application(_:handleEventsForBackgroundURLSession:completionHandler:)

// SwiftUI
@main struct MyApp: App {
    @Environment(\.scenePhase) private var scenePhase
    var body: some Scene {
        WindowGroup { RootView() }
            .onChange(of: scenePhase) { _, phase in
                switch phase {
                case .active:   resume()
                case .inactive: pause()
                case .background: save()
                @unknown default: break
                }
            }
    }
}

The thing they check: you must save state in sceneDidEnterBackground / .background, because applicationWillTerminate is not called when the system kills a suspended app — only when the user force-quits a running app or the app is not using scenes.

P0

How do you do work after the app goes to the background?

// 1. Short finite task — finish an upload, save a file. ~30 seconds.
func sceneDidEnterBackground(_ scene: UIScene) {
    var id: UIBackgroundTaskIdentifier = .invalid
    id = UIApplication.shared.beginBackgroundTask(withName: "SaveDraft") {
        // EXPIRATION HANDLER — you MUST end the task here or you get killed (0x8badf00d)
        UIApplication.shared.endBackgroundTask(id)
        id = .invalid
    }
    Task {
        await saveDraft()
        UIApplication.shared.endBackgroundTask(id)
        id = .invalid
    }
}
// 2. BGTaskScheduler (iOS 13+) — the modern scheduled-work API
// Info.plist: BGTaskSchedulerPermittedIdentifiers = ["com.app.refresh", "com.app.cleanup"]

func application(_ app: UIApplication, didFinishLaunchingWithOptions _: [UIApplication.LaunchOptionsKey: Any]?) -> Bool {
    BGTaskScheduler.shared.register(forTaskWithIdentifier: "com.app.refresh", using: nil) { task in
        self.handleRefresh(task as! BGAppRefreshTask)
    }
    return true
}

func scheduleRefresh() {
    let request = BGAppRefreshTaskRequest(identifier: "com.app.refresh")
    request.earliestBeginDate = Date(timeIntervalSinceNow: 15 * 60)
    try? BGTaskScheduler.shared.submit(request)
}

func handleRefresh(_ task: BGAppRefreshTask) {
    scheduleRefresh()                     // always reschedule the NEXT one first
    let work = Task {
        await syncEngine.pull()
        task.setTaskCompleted(success: true)
    }
    task.expirationHandler = { work.cancel() }   // system is reclaiming time
}

Task types: BGAppRefreshTaskRequest (short, ~30 s, keeps content fresh) and BGProcessingTaskRequest (minutes, can require network and external power — for DB maintenance, ML training, big cleanups). iOS 17 added BGHealthResearchTaskRequest; iOS 18 added BGContinuedProcessingTaskRequest for user-initiated long work with a Live Activity.

Say this: scheduling is opportunistic, not guaranteed. The system decides based on usage patterns, battery, network, and Low Power Mode. You cannot promise "sync every 15 minutes" — design for "sync when we can, and reconcile on foreground". Test it by forcing the launch from lldb.

P0

Background modes — which exist and what do they allow?

Declared in Signing & Capabilities → Background Modes:

  • Audio, AirPlay, Picture in Picture — keeps running while playing.
  • Location updates — continuous location; also significant-change and region monitoring, which relaunch the app.
  • Voice over IP — now requires PushKit + CallKit (you must report the call or you get terminated).
  • External accessory / Bluetooth LE central & peripheral.
  • Background fetch — legacy; use BGAppRefreshTask.
  • Remote notifications — silent push wake-ups.
  • Background processing — BGProcessingTask.
  • Background URLSession transfers (not a checkbox; use a background configuration).

App Review rejects apps that declare a mode they don't genuinely need — worth saying.

P0

Push notifications end to end

// 1. Register
func application(_ app: UIApplication, didFinishLaunchingWithOptions _: [UIApplication.LaunchOptionsKey: Any]?) -> Bool {
    UNUserNotificationCenter.current().delegate = self
    Task {
        let granted = try await UNUserNotificationCenter.current()
            .requestAuthorization(options: [.alert, .sound, .badge, .provisional])
        if granted { await MainActor.run { app.registerForRemoteNotifications() } }
    }
    return true
}

// 2. Receive the device token -> send to your server
func application(_ app: UIApplication,
                 didRegisterForRemoteNotificationsWithDeviceToken deviceToken: Data) {
    let token = deviceToken.map { String(format: "%02x", $0) }.joined()
    Task { await api.register(pushToken: token) }
}

// 3. Foreground presentation
extension AppDelegate: UNUserNotificationCenterDelegate {
    func userNotificationCenter(_ c: UNUserNotificationCenter,
        willPresent n: UNNotification) async -> UNNotificationPresentationOptions {
        [.banner, .sound, .list]     // without this, nothing shows in the foreground
    }

    // 4. Tap handling
    func userNotificationCenter(_ c: UNUserNotificationCenter,
        didReceive response: UNNotificationResponse) async {
        let info = response.notification.request.content.userInfo
        if let deepLink = info["deeplink"] as? String, let url = URL(string: deepLink) {
            await router.handle(url)
        }
    }
}

The payload:

{
  "aps": {
    "alert": { "title": "New message", "body": "Hi" },
    "sound": "default",
    "badge": 3,
    "thread-id": "chat-42",
    "mutable-content": 1,
    "content-available": 1,
    "interruption-level": "time-sensitive"
  },
  "deeplink": "myapp://chat/42"
}
  • content-available: 1 = silent push: wakes the app in the background to fetch. Delivery is throttled and best-effort, and it does not work if the user force-quit the app or Low Power Mode is on.
  • mutable-content: 1 = the Notification Service Extension may modify the payload before display (decrypt, download an image, localise).
  • interruption-level: passive, active, time-sensitive (breaks through Focus), critical (requires an Apple entitlement).

APNs transport: HTTP/2 with token-based auth (p8 key) — preferred, doesn't expire, one key for all your apps — or certificate-based (p12, expires yearly). Know the difference; it's a common question.

Notification Service Extension:

final class NotificationService: UNNotificationServiceExtension {
    var handler: ((UNNotificationContent) -> Void)?
    var content: UNMutableNotificationContent?

    override func didReceive(_ request: UNNotificationRequest,
                             withContentHandler h: @escaping (UNNotificationContent) -> Void) {
        handler = h
        content = request.content.mutableCopy() as? UNMutableNotificationContent
        guard let content else { return h(request.content) }
        Task {
            if let urlString = content.userInfo["image"] as? String,
               let attachment = try? await downloadAttachment(urlString) {
                content.attachments = [attachment]
            }
            h(content)
        }
    }

    override func serviceExtensionTimeWillExpire() {
        handler?(content ?? UNNotificationContent())   // ~30s limit — deliver SOMETHING
    }
}

Memory limit for this extension is tiny (~24 MB), so downloading a large image needs care — a good gotcha to mention.

P0

Deep links vs Universal Links — explain both and the pitfalls

Custom URL scheme (myapp://product/123): declared in Info.plist CFBundleURLTypes. Any app can claim any scheme, so it's spoofable and not secure. No fallback if the app isn't installed.

Universal Links (https://example.com/product/123): a real HTTPS URL. Requires:

  1. An apple-app-site-association (AASA) file served at https://example.com/.well-known/apple-app-site-association over HTTPS, no redirects, Content-Type: application/json, no .json extension.
  2. The Associated Domains capability with applinks:example.com.
{ "applinks": { "details": [
  { "appIDs": ["TEAMID.com.app.bundle"],
    "components": [ { "/": "/product/*", "comment": "product pages" } ] } ] } }

Advantages: can't be hijacked by another app, falls back to the website if the app isn't installed, and works from anywhere a link appears.

Handling both:

// Universal Link
func scene(_ scene: UIScene, continue userActivity: NSUserActivity) {
    guard userActivity.activityType == NSUserActivityTypeBrowsingWeb,
          let url = userActivity.webpageURL else { return }
    router.handle(url)
}
// Cold start:
func scene(_ scene: UIScene, willConnectTo session: UISceneSession,
           options: UIScene.ConnectionOptions) {
    if let activity = options.userActivities.first { /* universal link */ }
    if let url = options.urlContexts.first?.url { /* custom scheme */ }
}
// Warm start, custom scheme:
func scene(_ scene: UIScene, openURLContexts contexts: Set<UIOpenURLContext>) { }

// SwiftUI
RootView()
    .onOpenURL { url in router.handle(url) }
    .onContinueUserActivity(NSUserActivityTypeBrowsingWeb) { activity in
        if let url = activity.webpageURL { router.handle(url) }
    }

The pitfalls to name (this is what separates answers):

  • You must handle cold start and warm start separately, or links break in one of the two. Test both.
  • Universal Links do not work if the user typed the URL in Safari's address bar, or if they previously tapped "open in Safari" from the smart banner (iOS remembers the choice — long-press the link to get the app back).
  • AASA is cached by Apple's CDN; changes can take up to 24 h to propagate, and the app re-fetches it on install/update only.
  • Queue the link if the app isn't ready (not signed in, still restoring session) and replay it after.
@Observable final class Router {
    private var pending: URL?
    func handle(_ url: URL) {
        guard session.isReady else { pending = url; return }
        navigate(to: Route(url: url))
    }
    func sessionBecameReady() { if let p = pending { pending = nil; handle(p) } }
}
P1

App extensions — what types exist and what are the constraints?

Types: Share, Action, Today (deprecated), Notification Service, Notification Content, Widget (WidgetKit), Live Activity, Keyboard, Photo Editing, File Provider, Intents/App Intents, Safari Web Extension, Call Directory, Broadcast Upload (ReplayKit), Message Filter, Credential Provider (passwords/passkeys), App Clip (technically not an extension but related).

Constraints every extension shares:

  • Separate process, separate bundle, separate memory limit (much tighter — a Notification Service Extension is ~24 MB; a Share extension ~120 MB).
  • Cannot access UIApplication.shared.
  • Cannot use sharedApplication APIs, openURL directly (use extensionContext?.open(url)).
  • No background modes.
  • Shares data with the host app only through an App Group (shared UserDefaults suite, shared container directory, or shared Keychain access group).
let defaults = UserDefaults(suiteName: "group.com.app.shared")!
let container = FileManager.default
    .containerURL(forSecurityApplicationGroupIdentifier: "group.com.app.shared")!
P1

Widgets and Live Activities

struct FeedWidget: Widget {
    var body: some WidgetConfiguration {
        AppIntentConfiguration(kind: "FeedWidget", intent: FeedConfigIntent.self,
                               provider: FeedProvider()) { entry in
            FeedWidgetView(entry: entry)
                .containerBackground(.fill.tertiary, for: .widget)
        }
        .supportedFamilies([.systemSmall, .systemMedium, .accessoryRectangular])
    }
}

struct FeedProvider: AppIntentTimelineProvider {
    func placeholder(in: Context) -> FeedEntry { .placeholder }
    func snapshot(for intent: FeedConfigIntent, in: Context) async -> FeedEntry { .placeholder }

    func timeline(for intent: FeedConfigIntent, in: Context) async -> Timeline<FeedEntry> {
        let items = await sharedStore.latest()
        let entries = (0..<4).map { FeedEntry(date: .now.addingTimeInterval(Double($0) * 900),
                                              items: items) }
        return Timeline(entries: entries, policy: .after(.now.addingTimeInterval(3600)))
    }
}
// Push an update from the app:
WidgetCenter.shared.reloadTimelines(ofKind: "FeedWidget")

Constraints: widgets are not live — they render a timeline of pre-computed entries; the system decides when to refresh and enforces a budget (roughly 40–70 refreshes/day). No scrolling, no arbitrary animations, no network at render time (fetch in timeline(for:in:)). Interactivity is limited to Button(intent:) and Toggle(intent:) with App Intents (iOS 17+).

Live Activities (ActivityKit) for real-time, time-bounded events (delivery tracking, a match score). Updated via a local activity.update(...) or a push to the activity's own token with apns-push-type: liveactivity. Max ~8 h active, 12 h on the Lock Screen. Dynamic Island presentations: compact leading/trailing, minimal, expanded.

P1

App Clips, Handoff, Spotlight, App Intents / Siri

// App Intents — the modern Siri/Shortcuts/Spotlight surface
struct OpenProductIntent: AppIntent {
    static var title: LocalizedStringResource = "Open Product"
    @Parameter(title: "Product") var product: ProductEntity
    @MainActor func perform() async throws -> some IntentResult {
        router.navigate(to: .product(id: product.id))
        return .result()
    }
}

// Spotlight indexing
let attrs = CSSearchableItemAttributeSet(contentType: .text)
attrs.title = product.name
attrs.contentDescription = product.summary
let item = CSSearchableItem(uniqueIdentifier: product.id,
                            domainIdentifier: "products", attributeSet: attrs)
try await CSSearchableIndex.default().indexSearchableItems([item])

App Clips: under 15 MB uncompressed, launched from App Clip Codes / NFC / Maps / Safari banners, share an App Group with the full app so data carries over on install.

P2

Permissions — how do you request them well?

// Camera / mic
let granted = await AVCaptureDevice.requestAccess(for: .video)

// Photos — prefer limited access and PHPicker (which needs NO permission at all)
let status = await PHPhotoLibrary.requestAuthorization(for: .readWrite)

// Location
manager.requestWhenInUseAuthorization()
// later, after showing value, escalate:
manager.requestAlwaysAuthorization()

// Notifications
try await UNUserNotificationCenter.current().requestAuthorization(options: [.alert, .sound, .badge])

// Tracking (ATT) — must be called after the app is active
let status = await ATTrackingManager.requestTrackingAuthorization()

// Contacts, Calendar, HealthKit, Bluetooth, Speech, Motion, Local Network...

What interviewers want to hear:

  1. Every permission needs a purpose string in Info.plist (NSCameraUsageDescription, …) — a missing one is an instant crash, not a silent failure.
  2. Ask in context, after explaining the value — a pre-permission priming screen dramatically improves grant rates, and you only get one system prompt ever.
  3. Handle .denied gracefully with a path to Settings (UIApplication.openSettingsURLString).
  4. Prefer APIs that need no permission: PHPickerViewController instead of full photo library access, CNContactPickerViewController instead of Contacts access, .limited photo authorization.
  5. ATT must be requested when the app is active, not during launch, or the prompt silently doesn't appear.
P2

State restoration

// UIKit scene-based
func stateRestorationActivity(for scene: UIScene) -> NSUserActivity? {
    let activity = NSUserActivity(activityType: "com.app.browsing")
    activity.userInfo = ["productID": currentProductID]
    return activity
}
func scene(_ scene: UIScene, willConnectTo session: UISceneSession,
           options: UIScene.ConnectionOptions) {
    if let activity = session.stateRestorationActivity,
       let id = activity.userInfo?["productID"] as? String { restore(productID: id) }
}

// SwiftUI
@SceneStorage("selectedProductID") private var selectedProductID: String?

The principle: restore identifiers, not objects, and re-fetch. Never serialise view controllers.

P2

Localization and internationalization

// String Catalogs (.xcstrings) — the modern replacement for .strings/.stringsdict
Text("welcome_title")                       // auto-extracted
String(localized: "cart.items \(count)")    // with pluralization in the catalog

// Formatting — never hand-roll
let price = amount.formatted(.currency(code: "INR"))
let date  = Date.now.formatted(.dateTime.day().month(.wide).year())
let rel   = Date.now.addingTimeInterval(-3600).formatted(.relative(presentation: .named))
let list  = ["a","b","c"].formatted(.list(type: .and))

Points that matter: use leading/trailing, never left/right, so RTL works (UIView.semanticContentAttribute, .environment(\.layoutDirection, .rightToLeft) to test); never concatenate translated strings (word order differs); pluralisation rules differ by language so use the catalog's plural variations, not if count == 1; German and Arabic expand text by 30–40% so test with -AppleLanguages (de) and pseudolocalisation; and format numbers, dates and currency with FormatStyle, which handles locale, calendar and numeral systems.
