NSUserActivity and Handoff

let activity = NSUserActivity(activityType: "com.app.viewProduct")
activity.title = product.name
activity.userInfo = ["id": product.id]
activity.isEligibleForHandoff = true
activity.isEligibleForSearch = true
activity.isEligibleForPrediction = true
activity.webpageURL = product.webURL      // Handoff to Safari / other devices
view.userActivity = activity
activity.becomeCurrent()

One object drives Handoff, Spotlight, Siri prediction, and universal-link fallback — a neat thing to point out.

P3

Watch, CarPlay, visionOS, and multi-platform

Worth a sentence each if asked: WatchConnectivity (WCSession — sendMessage for live, transferUserInfo/updateApplicationContext for queued), CarPlay requires an Apple entitlement and only supports specific app categories with template UI (CPTemplateApplicationScene), visionOS adds volumetric windows and immersive spaces with SwiftUI as the only UI framework, and Mac Catalyst / SwiftUI multiplatform for Mac. The honest answer at 3 years is usually "I've shipped iOS; I know the shape of the others" — don't oversell.

12

Security & Privacy

11 questions
P0

How do you store sensitive data in an iOS app?

A ranked answer:

  1. Don't store it at all if you can avoid it. The safest token is one you fetch on demand.
  2. Keychain for credentials, tokens, keys — with the right accessibility class (see Chapter 8). kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly for anything that shouldn't migrate to a new device via backup.
  3. Keychain + Secure Enclave for private keys that should never leave the device:
let access = SecAccessControlCreateWithFlags(nil,
    kSecAttrAccessibleWhenPasscodeSetThisDeviceOnly,
    [.privateKeyUsage, .biometryCurrentSet], nil)!

let attrs: [String: Any] = [
    kSecAttrKeyType as String: kSecAttrKeyTypeECSECPrimeRandom,
    kSecAttrKeySizeInBits as String: 256,
    kSecAttrTokenID as String: kSecAttrTokenIDSecureEnclave,
    kSecPrivateKeyAttrs as String: [
        kSecAttrIsPermanent as String: true,
        kSecAttrApplicationTag as String: Data("com.app.signing".utf8),
        kSecAttrAccessControl as String: access
    ]
]
var error: Unmanaged<CFError>?
let privateKey = SecKeyCreateRandomKey(attrs as CFDictionary, &error)

The private key material never leaves the Secure Enclave — you can only ask it to sign or decrypt. .biometryCurrentSet invalidates the key if the user adds a new fingerprint/face, which is the strong option.

  1. File protection (.completeFileProtection) for files on disk.
  2. App-level encryption with CryptoKit when you need protection against backup extraction, with the key in the Keychain.

Never: UserDefaults, plist files, source code constants, or logs. And never commit an API key to git — say you'd use a build-time injected xcconfig, a secrets manager, or better, keep the secret server-side entirely.

P0

How do you secure network traffic?

  • HTTPS only. App Transport Security is on by default; an NSAllowsArbitraryLoads exception in Info.plist is an App Review question and a red flag. If you must allow a specific host, use NSExceptionDomains scoped to it.
  • TLS 1.2 minimum, 1.3 preferred (NSExceptionMinimumTLSVersion).
  • Certificate/public-key pinning for high-value apps (see Chapter 7), with backup pins and a remote kill switch.
  • Never put secrets in the client. An API key in the app binary is public — anyone can strings the binary or proxy the traffic. Sensitive operations must be authorised server-side by a user token.
  • Short-lived access tokens + refresh tokens, refresh token rotation, and server-side revocation.
  • Don't log request/response bodies in release, and mark log interpolations .private.
P0

Biometric authentication — implement it and name the pitfalls

import LocalAuthentication

func authenticate() async throws -> Bool {
    let context = LAContext()
    context.localizedFallbackTitle = "Use Passcode"

    var error: NSError?
    guard context.canEvaluatePolicy(.deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics, error: &error) else {
        // .biometryNotEnrolled, .biometryNotAvailable, .biometryLockout
        throw BiometricError(laError: error)
    }
    return try await context.evaluatePolicy(
        .deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics,
        localizedReason: "Unlock your wallet")
}

deviceOwnerAuthentication vs deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics: the former falls back to the device passcode automatically; the latter is biometrics only and fails with .userFallback so you handle it.

The critical pitfall: evaluatePolicy returning true is just a boolean from the app's own process — on a jailbroken device it can be patched. If the data actually matters, gate the data not the UI: store the secret in the Keychain with an SecAccessControl requiring .userPresence / .biometryCurrentSet, so the OS enforces authentication before the item is returned. That's the answer that shows real security thinking.

Also: LAContext caches a successful evaluation — reuse the same context and you may not re-prompt. Create a fresh LAContext per authentication, or set touchIDAuthenticationAllowableReuseDuration. And handle .biometryLockout (too many failures) by falling back to passcode.

P1

Privacy manifests, required-reason APIs, and tracking

Since 2024, Apple requires:

PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy in your app and in every third-party SDK, declaring:

  • NSPrivacyTracking — whether you track.
  • NSPrivacyTrackingDomains — domains used for tracking (blocked if ATT isn't granted).
  • NSPrivacyCollectedDataTypes — what you collect, why, and whether it's linked to identity.
  • NSPrivacyAccessedAPITypes — required-reason APIs with an approved reason code.

Required-reason API categories to know: file timestamps (creationDate, modificationDate), system boot time (systemUptime), disk space, active keyboards, and UserDefaults. Using any of these without declaring an approved reason gets your build rejected on upload.

<key>NSPrivacyAccessedAPITypes</key>
<array>
  <dict>
    <key>NSPrivacyAccessedAPIType</key>
    <string>NSPrivacyAccessedAPICategoryUserDefaults</string>
    <key>NSPrivacyAccessedAPITypeReasons</key>
    <array><string>CA92.1</string></array>   <!-- app's own defaults -->
  </dict>
</array>

Signed SDKs: commonly-used third-party SDKs must ship a privacy manifest and a code signature.

ATT (App Tracking Transparency): required before accessing the IDFA or linking user data with third-party data for ads. Without consent ASIdentifierManager.advertisingIdentifier returns all zeros. Alternatives: IDFV (UIDevice.identifierForVendor — same for all apps from one vendor, reset when the last one is uninstalled), SKAdNetwork for attribution without user-level tracking.

P1

Jailbreak detection, anti-tampering, obfuscation — what's realistic?

Be honest, because interviewers respect it: client-side protections raise the cost of an attack, they do not prevent it. Anything on the device can eventually be defeated by a determined attacker with a debugger.

Practical layers:

func isLikelyJailbroken() -> Bool {
    #if targetEnvironment(simulator)
    return false
    #else
    let paths = ["/Applications/Cydia.app", "/usr/sbin/sshd", "/bin/bash",
                 "/private/var/lib/apt", "/usr/libexec/cydia"]
    if paths.contains(where: { FileManager.default.fileExists(atPath: $0) }) { return true }
    // Can we write outside the sandbox?
    let probe = "/private/jailbreak_probe.txt"
    if (try? "x".write(toFile: probe, atomically: true, encoding: .utf8)) != nil {
        try? FileManager.default.removeItem(atPath: probe); return true
    }
    // Can we open a known-jailbreak URL scheme?
    if let url = URL(string: "cydia://"), UIApplication.shared.canOpenURL(url) { return true }
    return false
    #endif
}

Other measures: debugger detection (sysctl with P_TRACED, ptrace(PT_DENY_ATTACH)), binary integrity checks, string obfuscation, and SSL pinning. Server-side: DeviceCheck and App Attest are the genuinely strong options because verification happens on your server, backed by Apple's hardware attestation.

let service = DCAppAttestService.shared
guard service.isSupported else { return }
let keyID = try await service.generateKey()
let attestation = try await service.attestKey(keyID, clientDataHash: challengeHash)
// send to your server, which verifies with Apple

Say clearly: the correct architecture assumes the client is hostile. Enforce business rules on the server; never trust a client-side "isPremium" flag.

P1

OWASP Mobile Top 10 — the ones that matter on iOS

Worth being able to name a few with an iOS example:

  1. Improper credential usage — hardcoded API keys in the binary.
  2. Inadequate supply chain security — an unvetted SPM/CocoaPods dependency with network access.
  3. Insecure authentication/authorization — trusting client-side role checks.
  4. Insufficient input/output validation — injection into NSPredicate format strings or SQL.
  5. Insecure communication — ATS exceptions, no pinning on a sensitive app.
  6. Inadequate privacy controls — logging PII, over-broad analytics.
  7. Insufficient binary protections — no obfuscation on a high-value target.
  8. Security misconfiguration — debug flags or test endpoints shipped to production.
  9. Insecure data storage — tokens in UserDefaults.
  10. Insufficient cryptography — rolling your own, ECB mode, hardcoded IVs, arc4random for keys instead of SecRandomCopyBytes.
// Injection risk in Core Data — never interpolate user input into the format string
NSPredicate(format: "name CONTAINS[cd] %@", userInput)     // ✅ argument substitution
NSPredicate(format: "name CONTAINS[cd] '\(userInput)'")    // ❌ injectable
P2

CryptoKit basics

import CryptoKit

// Hashing
let digest = SHA256.hash(data: Data(input.utf8))
let hex = digest.map { String(format: "%02x", $0) }.joined()

// HMAC — message authentication
let mac = HMAC<SHA256>.authenticationCode(for: payload, using: symmetricKey)
let valid = HMAC<SHA256>.isValidAuthenticationCode(mac, authenticating: payload, using: symmetricKey)

// Symmetric encryption (AEAD — authenticated, always prefer this)
let sealed = try AES.GCM.seal(plaintext, using: key)
let plain  = try AES.GCM.open(sealed, using: key)
// or ChaChaPoly.seal/.open

// Key agreement + derivation
let priv = P256.KeyAgreement.PrivateKey()
let shared = try priv.sharedSecretFromKeyAgreement(with: theirPublicKey)
let key = shared.hkdfDerivedSymmetricKey(using: SHA256.self, salt: salt,
                                         sharedInfo: Data(), outputByteCount: 32)

// Signing
let signingKey = Curve25519.Signing.PrivateKey()
let signature = try signingKey.signature(for: message)

Rules to state: never design your own crypto or mode of operation; always use authenticated encryption (GCM/ChaChaPoly) so tampering is detected; never reuse a nonce with the same key; derive keys with HKDF/PBKDF2 rather than using a password directly; and generate randomness with SecRandomCopyBytes or CryptoKit's own generators, never arc4random or Int.random for key material.

P2

Authentication flows: OAuth 2.0 / PKCE, Sign in with Apple, passkeys

// OAuth with ASWebAuthenticationSession — the correct way; never a WKWebView
let session = ASWebAuthenticationSession(url: authURL, callbackURLScheme: "myapp") { url, error in
    guard let code = url?.queryItem("code") else { return }
    Task { try await exchange(code: code, verifier: pkceVerifier) }
}
session.presentationContextProvider = self
session.prefersEphemeralWebBrowserSession = false   // true = don't share Safari cookies
session.start()

PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange): the client generates a random code_verifier, sends code_challenge = base64url(SHA256(verifier)) with the auth request, and sends the verifier when exchanging the code. This prevents an attacker who intercepts the redirect from exchanging the code. Mandatory for public clients like mobile apps — never use the implicit flow, and never embed a client secret in the app.

Why ASWebAuthenticationSession and not a WKWebView: the system browser shows the real URL and padlock (anti-phishing), shares the session so the user may already be logged in, and providers increasingly block embedded webviews.

Sign in with Apple: required by App Review if you offer other third-party sign-in. Key details: you get the user's name and email only on the first authorization — store them then or lose them; ASAuthorizationAppleIDProvider.getCredentialState(forUserID:) on launch to detect revocation; and private relay emails need your sending domain registered with Apple.

Passkeys (ASAuthorizationPlatformPublicKeyCredentialProvider) — WebAuthn credentials in the Keychain, synced via iCloud, phishing-resistant because the credential is bound to the domain. Requires the same Associated Domains setup as universal links (webcredentials:example.com). Worth mentioning as "where auth is going".

P2

What are the App Review guidelines you actually hit?

Concrete ones that bite:

  • 3.1.1 In-App Purchase — digital goods must use IAP; you can't link out to your own payment (with narrow, region-specific exceptions). Physical goods must not use IAP.
  • 4.2 Minimum Functionality — a repackaged website gets rejected.
  • 5.1.1 Data collection — you must have a privacy policy, a purpose string for every permission, and an account-deletion path in-app if you support account creation.
  • 2.5.1 Private APIs — any use of a private selector is grounds for rejection; also don't use respondsToSelector tricks to reach them.
  • 4.7 / 2.5.2 — no downloading executable code that changes app behaviour (JS in a webview and JavaScriptCore have specific carve-outs).
  • 5.1.2 — ATT required before tracking.
  • 2.3 — accurate metadata and screenshots; no "beta" or "test" wording.
  • 1.2 / UGC — user-generated content needs filtering, reporting, blocking, and a published terms of use.

And the operational one: expedited review exists for critical fixes but don't cry wolf; phased release and App Store server notifications for subscription state changes.

P3

Secure coding details worth knowing

// 1. Constant-time comparison for secrets — avoid timing attacks
func constantTimeEquals(_ a: Data, _ b: Data) -> Bool {
    guard a.count == b.count else { return false }
    return zip(a, b).reduce(0) { $0 | ($1.0 ^ $1.1) } == 0
}

// 2. Redact sensitive UI in the app switcher snapshot
func sceneWillResignActive(_ scene: UIScene) {
    let blur = UIVisualEffectView(effect: UIBlurEffect(style: .systemMaterial))
    blur.frame = window!.bounds; blur.tag = 9999
    window?.addSubview(blur)
}
func sceneDidBecomeActive(_ scene: UIScene) {
    window?.viewWithTag(9999)?.removeFromSuperview()
}

// 3. Disable keyboard caching / autocorrect on sensitive fields
field.isSecureTextEntry = true
field.textContentType = .oneTimeCode      // and .password / .newPassword for AutoFill
field.autocorrectionType = .no

// 4. Prevent screenshots of sensitive content (best effort)
UIScreen.main.isCaptured   // observe UIScreen.capturedDidChangeNotification

isSecureTextEntry also prevents the field's content appearing in screenshots and screen recordings on iOS — which is why some apps wrap sensitive views in a hidden secure text field's layer. That's a hack; mention it as one.

P3

Supply chain and dependency security

Practices to name: pin exact versions (.exact("1.2.3")) or at least commit Package.resolved; review the diff on every dependency bump rather than auto-merging Dependabot; prefer source dependencies you can read over binary XCFrameworks; audit what network access and required-reason APIs a dependency declares in its privacy manifest; vendor small dependencies rather than taking a transitive tree; and keep a dependency budget — every SDK is code running with your app's full permissions.

13

Xcode, Build System, Dependencies & CI/CD

13 questions
P0

Targets, schemes, configurations, xcconfig — explain the build system

  • Target: a thing that gets built (app, framework, test bundle, extension). Has build settings, build phases, and a list of source files.
  • Scheme: a recipe for what to do — which targets to Build/Run/Test/Profile/Analyze/Archive, which configuration each action uses, launch arguments, environment variables, diagnostics.
  • Configuration: a named set of build settings — Debug, Release, and usually your own Staging, QA.
  • Build phases: Compile Sources → Link Binary With Libraries → Copy Bundle Resources → Run Script → Embed Frameworks.
  • xcconfig: plain-text build settings files. Use them so settings live in git-diffable text rather than in the .pbxproj (which is a merge-conflict machine).
// Base.xcconfig
SWIFT_VERSION = 6.0
IPHONEOS_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET = 17.0
SWIFT_STRICT_CONCURRENCY = complete

// Debug.xcconfig
#include "Base.xcconfig"
SWIFT_ACTIVE_COMPILATION_CONDITIONS = DEBUG
API_BASE_URL = https:/$()/api.staging.example.com   // $() escapes the // 
SWIFT_OPTIMIZATION_LEVEL = -Onone
SWIFT_COMPILATION_MODE = singlefile

// Release.xcconfig
#include "Base.xcconfig"
API_BASE_URL = https:/$()/api.example.com
SWIFT_OPTIMIZATION_LEVEL = -O
SWIFT_COMPILATION_MODE = wholemodule
VALIDATE_PRODUCT = YES

Then surface the value through Info.plist ($(API_BASE_URL)) and read it:

enum AppEnvironment {
    static var baseURL: URL {
        URL(string: Bundle.main.object(forInfoDictionaryKey: "API_BASE_URL") as! String)!
    }
}

Multiple environments — the two approaches and their trade-offs: separate configurations (one target, Debug/Staging/Release, one bundle ID unless you also vary PRODUCT_BUNDLE_IDENTIFIER) versus separate targets/schemes (more isolation, more maintenance). Configurations + xcconfig is the usual right answer; add a $(BUNDLE_ID_SUFFIX) so staging installs alongside production.

P0

SPM vs CocoaPods vs Carthage

Swift Package Manager — built into Xcode, no extra tooling, no project file mutation, supports binary targets, macros, plugins, resources, and per-target Swift settings. The default choice in 2026.

// swift-tools-version: 6.0
import PackageDescription

let package = Package(
    name: "Feature",
    platforms: [.iOS(.v17)],
    products: [
        .library(name: "Feature", targets: ["Feature"]),
        .library(name: "FeatureStatic", type: .static, targets: ["Feature"])
    ],
    dependencies: [
        .package(url: "https://github.com/apple/swift-collections", from: "1.1.0")
    ],
    targets: [
        .target(name: "Feature",
                dependencies: [.product(name: "Collections", package: "swift-collections")],
                resources: [.process("Resources")],
                swiftSettings: [
                    .swiftLanguageMode(.v6),
                    .enableUpcomingFeature("NonisolatedNonsendingByDefault")
                ]),
        .testTarget(name: "FeatureTests", dependencies: ["Feature"])
    ]
)

CocoaPods — mature, huge catalogue, supports build-time patching and complex integrations, but it rewrites your project into a workspace, is Ruby-based, and is now in maintenance mode. You'll meet it in legacy projects.

Carthage — builds frameworks and leaves integration to you. Mostly historical.

Things to mention: Package.resolved should be committed (it's your lockfile); prefer .upToNextMinor over branch: for reproducibility; static vs dynamic linking affects launch time (see Chapter 10); and local packages (.package(path: "../Domain")) are how you modularise without a separate repo.

P0

Code signing and provisioning — explain it end to end

The chain, in plain terms:

  1. A certificate (development or distribution) proves you are the developer. It's a public/private key pair; the private key lives in your Keychain.
  2. An App ID identifies the app (bundle identifier) and the capabilities it may use.
  3. A provisioning profile binds: certificate(s) + App ID + entitlements + (for development/ad-hoc) a list of device UDIDs. It's embedded in the app as embedded.mobileprovision.
  4. At build time, Xcode signs the bundle with the certificate and embeds the profile. At install/launch time, iOS verifies the signature and checks the profile allows this device and these entitlements.

Profile types: Development, Ad Hoc (specific devices, no App Store), App Store, Enterprise (in-house distribution, no device list).

Entitlements are the capabilities — push, App Groups, Associated Domains, HealthKit, Keychain sharing, iCloud. They must match between the .entitlements file, the App ID, and the profile, or you get the classic "Provisioning profile doesn't include the ... entitlement".

Automatic vs manual signing: automatic is fine for solo work; teams usually use manual signing with fastlane match, which stores certificates and profiles encrypted in a private git repo so every developer and CI machine uses the same identity.

Errors you should be able to diagnose:

  • "No signing certificate found" → missing private key (you imported the .cer but not the .p12).
  • "Profile doesn't match bundle identifier" → App ID mismatch or a wildcard profile with an explicit capability.
  • "The maximum number of devices has been reached" → 100 devices/year per type.
  • "App installation failed: This app cannot be installed because its integrity could not be verified" → device not in the profile, or the profile expired.
P0

How do you ship an app? (archive → TestFlight → App Store)

  1. Bump MARKETING_VERSION (CFBundleShortVersionString) and CURRENT_PROJECT_VERSION (build number).
  2. Archive with the Release configuration and a distribution profile.
  3. Validate and upload (xcodebuild -exportArchive, Transporter, or fastlane).
  4. Wait for processing, then distribute to a TestFlight group (internal groups are instant; external requires a Beta App Review).
  5. Submit for App Store review with release notes and, if wanted, phased release (1/2/5/10/20/50/100% over 7 days) and/or manual release.
  6. Monitor Xcode Organizer for crashes/hangs and App Store Connect for the review status.
xcodebuild -workspace App.xcworkspace -scheme App \
  -configuration Release -destination 'generic/platform=iOS' \
  -archivePath build/App.xcarchive archive

xcodebuild -exportArchive -archivePath build/App.xcarchive \
  -exportPath build/ -exportOptionsPlist ExportOptions.plist

xcrun altool --upload-app -f build/App.ipa -t ios \
  --apiKey $ASC_KEY_ID --apiIssuer $ASC_ISSUER_ID

Know that you can expire a TestFlight build (90 days max), that App Store Connect API keys are how CI authenticates without a human Apple ID + 2FA, and that a rejected build can often be fixed with metadata alone.

P1

fastlane — what does a real Fastfile look like?

default_platform(:ios)

platform :ios do
  desc "Run tests"
  lane :test do
    scan(scheme: "App", devices: ["iPhone 16"], code_coverage: true)
  end

  desc "Beta to TestFlight"
  lane :beta do
    setup_ci if ENV['CI']
    match(type: "appstore", readonly: true)          # certs & profiles from git
    increment_build_number(build_number: latest_testflight_build_number + 1)
    gym(scheme: "App", configuration: "Release", export_method: "app-store")
    pilot(skip_waiting_for_build_processing: true)   # upload to TestFlight
    slack(message: "New beta #{get_build_number} is up 🚀")
  end

  desc "Release"
  lane :release do
    match(type: "appstore", readonly: true)
    gym(scheme: "App")
    deliver(submit_for_review: true, automatic_release: false,
            force: true, precheck_include_in_app_purchases: false)
  end
end

Tools to name: match (shared signing), gym (build), scan (test), pilot (TestFlight), deliver (App Store metadata + screenshots), snapshot (localised screenshots), sigh, produce, setup_ci (temporary keychain on CI runners).

P1

Design a CI/CD pipeline for an iOS team

On every PR (must be fast — under 10 minutes):

  • Build the app and all modules
  • Unit + integration tests
  • SwiftLint / SwiftFormat in --strict mode
  • Danger for PR hygiene (changelog entry, PR size, no .only in tests)
  • Coverage ratchet

On merge to main:

  • Everything above, plus UI tests on 2 device sizes
  • Upload a build to an internal TestFlight group
  • Post to Slack

Nightly:

  • Full UI suite across the device matrix
  • Snapshot tests
  • Thread Sanitizer and Address Sanitizer runs
  • Dependency vulnerability scan
  • Build-time report

On tag:

  • Archive, sign, upload to TestFlight/App Store
  • Upload dSYMs to the crash reporter
  • Create a GitHub release with the changelog

Practical concerns to raise: macOS runners are expensive and slow — cache derived data and SPM checkouts, run the matrix in parallel, use -parallel-testing-enabled; keep secrets in the CI secret store and use App Store Connect API keys, not a human account with 2FA; make the pipeline reproducible by pinning the Xcode version explicitly.

P1

Git workflow and code review

Say what you actually do, with opinions:

  • Trunk-based with short-lived feature branches and feature flags for anything long-running, or GitFlow if you ship on a fixed cadence with release branches. Be able to argue for one.
  • Conventional commits so the changelog generates itself.
  • PR size — under ~400 lines changed; anything bigger gets reviewed superficially, and you should say that.
  • What I look for in review: correctness first, then tests for the new behaviour, then API design (is this the right seam?), then naming, then style (which should be automated away by SwiftFormat, not argued about in review).
  • Merge conflicts in .pbxproj — minimise by using SPM modules and xcconfig, and by not reordering the file. merge=union in .gitattributes helps a little but isn't safe in general.
P1

#if DEBUG, compilation conditions, and debug tooling

#if DEBUG
    let host = "staging.api.com"
#else
    let host = "api.com"
#endif

#if targetEnvironment(simulator)
    // simulator-only shortcuts
#endif

#if canImport(UIKit)
    import UIKit
#endif

@available(iOS 17, *)
func newAPI() { }

if #available(iOS 26, *) {
    view.glassEffect()
} else {
    view.background(.regularMaterial)
}

Worth mentioning: a debug menu (shake gesture or a hidden tap target) that lets QA switch environments, toggle feature flags, force a crash, view logs, and clear the database. Every team that ships fast has one, and building it is a nice thing to have done.

P2

LLDB — the commands you should know

po viewModel.state                      // print object description
p items.count                           // print with type info
v items                                 // variable inspection, no expression eval (faster/safer)
expression items.append(x)              // mutate live
e -l objc -- [[UIApplication sharedApplication] keyWindow]

breakpoint set --name viewDidLoad
breakpoint set --selector touchesBegan:withEvent:
br s -E swift -O                        // break on all Swift exceptions
watchpoint set variable self.count      // break when a value changes

bt                                      // backtrace
thread backtrace all
frame variable

// Symbolic breakpoints worth knowing:
UIViewAlertForUnsatisfiableConstraints  // Auto Layout conflicts
-[UIApplication main]
swift_willThrow                         // break whenever Swift throws
malloc_error_break

// View debugging from the console
po UIApplication.shared.keyWindow?.recursiveDescription()   // (needs -l objc)
po CATransaction.flush()                // force a redraw while paused

Also: conditional breakpoints (indexPath.row == 42), breakpoint actions (log + auto-continue = printf debugging with no rebuild), and the Debug > View Debugging > Capture View Hierarchy 3D view.

P2

SwiftLint / SwiftFormat and code style

# .swiftlint.yml
disabled_rules:
  - todo
opt_in_rules:
  - force_unwrapping
  - empty_count
  - first_where
  - unused_import
  - explicit_init
  - redundant_nil_coalescing
  - implicitly_unwrapped_optional
analyzer_rules:
  - unused_declaration
included: [Sources, Tests]
excluded: [.build, Generated]
line_length:
  warning: 120
  error: 160
type_body_length: 300
function_body_length: 60
cyclomatic_complexity:
  warning: 10
custom_rules:
  no_print:
    regex: '\bprint\('
    message: "Use Logger instead of print"
    severity: error

The opinion to express: formatting should be automated and never discussed in review; run SwiftFormat on commit via a git hook or a build phase, and run SwiftLint in --strict in CI so warnings can't accumulate. Also: a lint rule is only worth adding if it catches a real class of bug the team has actually hit.

P2

Modularisation mechanics in Xcode

Approaches, with trade-offs:

  • Local Swift packages — cleanest, fast, no .pbxproj churn, previews work, but cross-package resources and mixed ObjC need care.
  • Framework targets in the main project — familiar, but every change touches .pbxproj.
  • Static libraries — best for launch time, but can't carry resources without a bundle.
  • XCFrameworks — precompiled binaries for third-party distribution or to cut build time for rarely-changing code.

Also mention project generation (XcodeGen or Tuist) to eliminate .pbxproj merge conflicts entirely — you define the project in YAML/Swift and generate it, and .xcodeproj goes in .gitignore. That's a strong answer for "how do you manage a project with 20 engineers".

P3

Xcode Cloud vs GitHub Actions vs Bitrise

Xcode Cloud — native, zero signing setup, integrated with App Store Connect and TestFlight, pay by compute minutes. Weak at non-Apple steps and custom tooling. GitHub Actions — flexible, free tier, macOS runners are slow and expensive; great if the rest of your org lives there. Bitrise / CircleCI — mobile-focused, good caching and device farms, paid. Self-hosted Mac minis — fastest and cheapest at scale, but you own the maintenance, Xcode upgrades, and security.

The answer that lands: "It depends on where the rest of the team's tooling lives and how much custom scripting we need — I'd start with the one the org already uses, and the only hard requirement is that the pipeline is reproducible and signing is handled by match or Xcode Cloud, not a developer's laptop."

P3

Reading a .pbxproj and fixing a merge conflict

Know that project.pbxproj is an old-style plist with UUID-keyed objects: PBXBuildFile, PBXFileReference, PBXGroup, PBXNativeTarget, PBXSourcesBuildPhase, XCBuildConfiguration. A conflict is usually two people adding files, so the resolution is to keep both sides' entries in every section (build file, file reference, group children, sources phase) — the UUIDs are unique so union usually works. If it doesn't open afterwards, plutil -lint project.pbxproj will tell you why. The real fix is structural: fewer files in one project, or generate the project.

14

iOS System Design

12 questions
P0

The framework to use for every iOS system design question

1. Clarify requirements (3–5 min). Do not start drawing. Ask:

  • Scale: how many items, how often does data change, how many users?
  • Offline: must it work with no network? Read-only offline, or writes too?
  • Platform: minimum iOS version, iPad/Watch, SwiftUI or UIKit?
  • Real-time: push, polling, websockets?
  • Constraints: memory, battery, data usage, team size?
  • Non-goals: explicitly say what you're not designing.

2. Define the API surface / data model. What does the client need from the server, and what does the domain model look like?

3. Draw the layers. Presentation (View + ViewModel) → Domain (use cases) → Data (Repository → Remote + Local data sources) → Infrastructure (networking, persistence, cache).

4. Walk one flow end to end. "User opens the feed" — what happens, in order, including the cold-cache and offline variants.

5. Cover the hard parts unprompted: concurrency model, caching and invalidation, pagination, error handling and retries, cancellation, memory, testability.

6. Name trade-offs and what you'd do differently at 10× scale.

Say this early: "I'll design for correctness first, then make the caching and concurrency explicit, and I'll call out where I'd trade freshness for speed."

P0

Design an image loading and caching library

This is the most common iOS system design question. Know it cold.

Requirements: load images by URL for a scrolling list; never block the main thread; don't download the same image twice; cap memory; survive app restarts; cancel when a cell is reused.

Architecture:

ImageLoader (actor)
 ├── MemoryCache   (NSCache<NSString, UIImage>, cost-limited)
 ├── DiskCache     (actor, LRU by modification date, Library/Caches)
 ├── InFlight      ([URL: Task<UIImage, Error>]) — request coalescing
 └── Downloader    (URLSession) → Decoder (downsample off-main)
actor ImageLoader {
    static let shared = ImageLoader()

    private let memory = NSCache<NSURL, UIImage>()
    private let disk: DiskCache
    private let session: URLSession
    private var inFlight: [URL: Task<UIImage, Error>] = [:]

    init(disk: DiskCache = .default, session: URLSession = .shared) {
        self.disk = disk
        self.session = session
        memory.totalCostLimit = 100 * 1024 * 1024
    }

    func image(for url: URL, targetSize: CGSize) async throws -> UIImage {
        let key = cacheKey(url, targetSize)

        // L1 — memory
        if let cached = memory.object(forKey: key as NSURL) { return cached }

        // Coalesce: if someone is already fetching this, await their result
        if let existing = inFlight[url] { return try await existing.value }

        let task = Task<UIImage, Error> { [disk, session] in
            // L2 — disk
            if let data = try? await disk.data(for: key.absoluteString),
               let image = await Self.decode(data, targetSize: targetSize) {
                return image
            }
            // L3 — network
            try Task.checkCancellation()
            let (data, response) = try await session.data(from: url)
            guard let http = response as? HTTPURLResponse, 200..<300 ~= http.statusCode else {
                throw ImageError.badResponse
            }
            try? await disk.store(data, for: key.absoluteString)
            guard let image = await Self.decode(data, targetSize: targetSize) else {
                throw ImageError.decodeFailed
            }
            return image
        }

        inFlight[url] = task
        defer { inFlight[url] = nil }

        let image = try await task.value
        memory.setObject(image, forKey: key as NSURL, cost: image.byteSize)
        return image
    }

    // Decoding + downsampling OFF the main thread — the single biggest scroll-perf win
    @concurrent
    private static func decode(_ data: Data, targetSize: CGSize) async -> UIImage? {
        guard let src = CGImageSourceCreateWithData(data as CFData,
                [kCGImageSourceShouldCache: false] as CFDictionary) else { return nil }
        let maxPixel = max(targetSize.width, targetSize.height) * UIScreen.main.scale
        let options = [
            kCGImageSourceCreateThumbnailFromImageAlways: true,
            kCGImageSourceShouldCacheImmediately: true,
            kCGImageSourceCreateThumbnailWithTransform: true,
            kCGImageSourceThumbnailMaxPixelSize: maxPixel
        ] as CFDictionary
        guard let cg = CGImageSourceCreateThumbnailAtIndex(src, 0, options) else { return nil }
        return UIImage(cgImage: cg)
    }
}

Points to raise unprompted:

  • Cache key includes the target size — a 60 pt thumbnail and a 300 pt hero of the same URL are different entries.
  • Three tiers: memory (fast, volatile), disk (survives launch, LRU-trimmed), network.
  • Coalescing via the inFlight dictionary — two cells requesting the same avatar = one download.
  • Cancellation: the cell cancels its Task in prepareForReuse; because the loader's task is structured under it, cancellation propagates. (Careful: if two callers share one in-flight task, one cancelling shouldn't kill the other — mention that you'd wrap the shared task so only the last observer's cancellation tears it down, or simply let it complete into the cache.)
  • Prefetching via UITableViewDataSourcePrefetching / UICollectionViewDataSourcePrefetching at low priority.
  • Eviction: NSCache handles memory pressure; disk trims by LRU on a byte budget.
  • HTTP caching: honour ETag/Cache-Control so a re-fetch can 304.
  • Animated images / HEIC / progressive JPEG as extensions.
  • Testing: inject the URLSession (via URLProtocol) and the disk cache directory.
P0

Design a paginated, offline-capable feed (Instagram/Twitter style)

Requirements: infinite scroll, pull to refresh, works offline showing the last page, new items appear without losing scroll position, images load smoothly.

Data model and paging strategy. Push for cursor-based pagination, not offset:

struct Page<Item: Decodable>: Decodable {
    let items: [Item]
    let nextCursor: String?     // opaque; server-defined
}

Offset pagination breaks when items are inserted or deleted between requests (you see duplicates or skip items). Cursor pagination is stable. Saying this unprompted is a strong signal.

@Observable @MainActor
final class FeedViewModel {
    enum LoadState { case idle, loading, loadingMore, loaded, failed(Error) }

    private(set) var posts: [Post] = []
    private(set) var state: LoadState = .idle
    private var cursor: String?
    private var hasMore = true
    private var loadTask: Task<Void, Never>?

    private let repo: FeedRepository

    func refresh() async {
        loadTask?.cancel()
        cursor = nil; hasMore = true
        state = .loading
        do {
            let page = try await repo.feed(cursor: nil, policy: .networkFirst)
            posts = page.items
            cursor = page.nextCursor
            hasMore = page.nextCursor != nil
            state = .loaded
        } catch is CancellationError {
            // ignore
        } catch {
            // offline: fall back to whatever the local store has
            posts = (try? await repo.cachedFeed()) ?? []
            state = posts.isEmpty ? .failed(error) : .loaded
        }
    }

    func loadMoreIfNeeded(currentItem: Post) async {
        guard hasMore,
              case .loaded = state,
              let index = posts.firstIndex(of: currentItem),
              index >= posts.count - 5 else { return }   // trigger 5 rows from the end
        state = .loadingMore
        defer { if case .loadingMore = state { state = .loaded } }
        guard let page = try? await repo.feed(cursor: cursor, policy: .networkOnly) else { return }
        // de-duplicate — servers do return overlaps
        let existing = Set(posts.map(\.id))
        posts += page.items.filter { !existing.contains($0.id) }
        cursor = page.nextCursor
        hasMore = page.nextCursor != nil
    }
}

The repository with a cache policy:

enum CachePolicy { case cacheFirst, networkFirst, networkOnly, staleWhileRevalidate }

actor FeedRepository {
    func feed(cursor: String?, policy: CachePolicy) async throws -> Page<Post> {
        switch policy {
        case .staleWhileRevalidate:
            let cached = try? await local.page(cursor: cursor)
            Task { try? await refreshFromNetwork(cursor: cursor) }   // update in background
            if let cached { return cached }
            fallthrough
        case .networkFirst, .networkOnly:
            let page = try await remote.feed(cursor: cursor)
            try await local.upsert(page.items)
            return page
        case .cacheFirst:
            if let cached = try? await local.page(cursor: cursor) { return cached }
            return try await feed(cursor: cursor, policy: .networkFirst)
        }
    }
}

Things to cover unprompted:

  • Scroll position preservation on refresh: prepend new items and adjust contentOffset, or show a "N new posts" pill rather than jumping the user — a product-aware answer.
  • Diffable data source / stable IDs so inserts animate correctly.
  • Image prefetching for rows just below the fold.
  • Cell height estimation to avoid scroll-indicator jumps.
  • Deduplication and a stable sort key (createdAt + id tiebreak).
  • Memory: cap the in-memory array (e.g. keep 500 posts, drop the head when scrolling far), or you OOM on a long session.
  • Read state / analytics impressions with a visibility threshold and a debounce.
P0

Design a chat application

Requirements: 1:1 and group messages, real-time delivery, offline send, read receipts, typing indicators, media, message history.

Transport: WebSocket for the live channel, HTTP for history and media, push notifications as the fallback when the socket is down or the app is suspended. Say clearly that the socket dies on backgrounding, so push is not optional.

The local store is the source of truth. The UI reads from SQLite/Core Data; the network writes into it.

struct Message: Identifiable, Sendable {
    let id: String              // client-generated UUID = idempotency key
    let conversationID: String
    let senderID: String
    var body: String
    var createdAt: Date
    var serverSequence: Int64?  // server-assigned ordering, nil until acked
    var status: Status          // .sending, .sent, .delivered, .read, .failed
}

Optimistic send flow:

  1. Insert the message locally with a client UUID and status = .sending. UI shows it instantly.
  2. Enqueue in the outbox.
  3. Send over the socket (or HTTP if the socket is down).
  4. On ack, update serverSequence and status = .sent. Because the ID is client-generated, a retry can't duplicate.
  5. On failure, status = .failed with a retry affordance.

Ordering is the interesting problem. Local timestamps disagree across devices and clocks drift. Use a server-assigned monotonic sequence per conversation for canonical ordering; sort locally by (serverSequence ?? .max, createdAt) so pending messages sit at the bottom until acked.

Sync on reconnect: GET /conversations/{id}/messages?after=lastKnownSequence. Paginate backwards for history (before=). Keep a per-conversation lastSyncedSequence watermark.

Read receipts and typing: typing is ephemeral, throttled (send at most one "typing" every 3 s, auto-expire after 5 s) and never persisted. Read receipts are a watermark per user per conversation (lastReadSequence), not a per-message flag — far less data.

UI concerns: inverted list (scroll anchored at the bottom); keep the scroll pinned when a new message arrives only if the user is already at the bottom; load older pages when scrolling up without jumping; UICollectionView with a diffable data source or ScrollView + defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom) in SwiftUI.

Media: upload to object storage via a pre-signed URL, send the message with the resulting key; show a local thumbnail immediately; support resumable uploads for large videos.

Scale/edge cases to mention: message deletion and edits (tombstones + editedAt), end-to-end encryption if asked (key exchange, Secure Enclave, and the fact that server-side search becomes impossible), multi-device, and backfilling a conversation the user has never opened.

P1

Design an offline-first note-taking / to-do app with sync

Covered partly in Chapter 8. The system-design framing:

Local store is authoritative for the UI. Every entity carries id (client UUID), updatedAt, deletedAt (tombstone), and syncState.

Sync engine:

push: drain outbox (create/update/delete ops, each with an idempotency key)
pull: GET /changes?since=<cursor>  →  apply to local store  →  save new cursor

Conflict resolution — present options and pick one with reasons:

  • Last-write-wins on server timestamp: trivial, silently loses edits. Fine for a to-do checkbox.
  • Field-level merge: keep the newest value per field. Good for structured records.
  • Operational transform / CRDT: correct for collaborative text; heavy. Mention Automerge/Yjs-style if the interviewer pushes.
  • User-resolved: mark the entity conflicted and show both versions. Right for anything with real content.

Triggers: on app foreground, on local change (debounced), on silent push, and via BGAppRefreshTask. Plus a manual pull-to-refresh so the user has agency when sync is stuck.

Failure handling: exponential backoff per operation, a dead-letter state after N attempts with a visible "couldn't sync" indicator, and never block the UI on sync.

P1

Design an analytics SDK

Requirements: track events from anywhere in the app, don't block callers, don't lose events on crash or offline, batch to save battery, respect privacy.

public protocol AnalyticsTracking: Sendable {
    func track(_ event: String, properties: [String: AnalyticsValue])
}

public actor Analytics: AnalyticsTracking {
    private var buffer: [Event] = []
    private let store: EventStore          // SQLite/file — survives crash & relaunch
    private let uploader: EventUploader
    private let flushThreshold = 20
    private var flushTask: Task<Void, Never>?

    nonisolated public func track(_ name: String, properties: [String: AnalyticsValue]) {
        // Non-blocking from the caller's perspective
        Task { await self.enqueue(Event(name: name, properties: properties, timestamp: .now)) }
    }

    private func enqueue(_ event: Event) async {
        buffer.append(event)
        try? await store.append(event)                 // durable immediately
        if buffer.count >= flushThreshold { await flush() }
        else { scheduleFlush(after: .seconds(30)) }
    }

    func flush() async {
        let batch = try? await store.take(limit: 100)
        guard let batch, !batch.isEmpty else { return }
        do {
            try await uploader.upload(batch)
            try await store.delete(ids: batch.map(\.id))   // delete only after success
        } catch {
            // leave them in the store; retry with backoff next flush
        }
    }
}

Design points to state:

  • Fire-and-forget API — track must never block or throw; analytics failing must not break the app.
  • Durable queue before upload — write to disk first, delete only after a successful upload, so a crash doesn't lose events and a failed upload doesn't duplicate (server dedupes by event ID).
  • Batching with size + time triggers, and a flush on sceneDidEnterBackground inside a beginBackgroundTask.
  • Backpressure: cap the queue (e.g. 10k events) and drop oldest, or you fill the user's disk when offline for a week.
  • Session and common properties (app version, OS, device, session ID, locale) attached once, not per call site.
  • Privacy: no PII by default, a type-safe event enum rather than free strings, a privacy manifest declaration, and an opt-out that actually stops collection.
  • Testability: the protocol is injectable; a spy asserts events in unit tests.
  • Sampling for high-volume events.
P1

Design a file downloader with pause/resume and progress

actor DownloadManager: NSObject {
    struct Download { let id: UUID; var progress: Double; var state: State; var resumeData: Data? }

    private var downloads: [UUID: Download] = [:]
    private var tasks: [UUID: URLSessionDownloadTask] = [:]
    private lazy var session: URLSession = {
        let config = URLSessionConfiguration.background(withIdentifier: "com.app.downloads")
        config.sessionSendsLaunchEvents = true
        config.isDiscretionary = false
        return URLSession(configuration: config, delegate: self, delegateQueue: nil)
    }()

    func start(_ url: URL) -> UUID { ... }
    func pause(_ id: UUID) async {
        tasks[id]?.cancel(byProducingResumeData: { data in
            Task { await self.storeResumeData(data, for: id) }
        })
    }
    func resume(_ id: UUID) async {
        guard let data = downloads[id]?.resumeData else { return }
        tasks[id] = session.downloadTask(withResumeData: data)
        tasks[id]?.resume()
    }
}

Cover: background session so downloads survive suspension and termination; resumeData for pause/resume (and note it's only produced for servers supporting range requests); moving the file out of the temp location synchronously in the delegate; disk space checks before starting; a concurrency cap; progress via task.progress or the delegate; and what happens on app relaunch (the session is recreated with the same identifier and delivers pending events).

P2

Design a design system / component library for a large app

Layers: Tokens (colour, typography, spacing, radius, elevation — semantic names like surface.primary, not blue500) → Primitives (Button, TextField, Card) → Patterns (EmptyState, Banner, ListRow) → Screens.

Practicalities: ship it as its own SPM module so features can't reach around it; support Dark Mode and Dynamic Type at the token level so every component inherits it; SwiftUI ButtonStyle/LabelStyle + environment for theming rather than parameters everywhere; snapshot tests per component across themes/sizes/RTL; a catalog app (or #Preview gallery) so designers can review; and a deprecation policy so the old button can actually be removed.

Versioning question they may ask: how do you roll out a breaking component change across 40 screens? Answer: add the new API alongside, @available(*, deprecated, renamed:) the old one, migrate screen by screen behind the compiler's warnings, then delete.

P2

Design a feature flag / experimentation system

Covered in Chapter 6; the system-design depth: a local cache of the last-known config so launch never blocks on the network; a default compiled into the app for every flag; fetch on launch and on foreground with a TTL; assign variants by a stable hash of (userID, experimentID) so a user always sees the same bucket and assignment is computable offline; log exposure events (the user actually saw the variant) separately from assignment; and a kill switch that can disable a feature without an app update.

P2

Design a video/audio playback experience

Points: AVPlayer + AVPlayerItem + AVPlayerLayer/VideoPlayer; HLS for adaptive bitrate streaming (and note that it's required by App Review for cellular video over 10 minutes); preloading the next item with AVQueuePlayer; AVAssetDownloadTask for offline with FairPlay for DRM; KVO on timeControlStatus and isPlaybackLikelyToKeepUp for buffering UI; addPeriodicTimeObserver for scrubbing; AVAudioSession category (.playback for background audio) and interruption/route-change notifications; and MPNowPlayingInfoCenter + MPRemoteCommandCenter for Lock Screen and Control Centre.

Memory/battery: one player instance reused rather than one per cell, player.replaceCurrentItem(with:), and pausing offscreen video in a feed.

P3

Design an A/B-testable, dynamically-configured screen (server-driven UI)

Worth discussing because it's fashionable and has sharp edges. The server sends a JSON description of components; the client renders known component types.

enum Component: Decodable {
    case text(TextSpec), image(ImageSpec), button(ButtonSpec), stack(StackSpec)

    init(from decoder: Decoder) throws {
        let c = try decoder.container(keyedBy: CodingKeys.self)
        switch try c.decode(String.self, forKey: .type) {
        case "text":  self = .text(try TextSpec(from: decoder))
        ...
        default: self = .unknown        // FORWARD COMPATIBILITY — never throw on unknown types
        }
    }
}

Trade-offs to state: you get instant iteration and remote A/B tests without a release, but you lose compile-time safety, native feel drifts, accessibility and Dynamic Type need extra care, debugging is harder, and App Review 4.7 / 2.5.2 constrains how much behaviour you can change remotely. Forward compatibility is mandatory: old app versions must ignore unknown component types rather than crash. My rule: server-driven for merchandising and marketing surfaces, native for core flows.

P3

Design for very large data sets (100k+ rows)

Mention: never load the whole set — SQLite with LIMIT/OFFSET or a cursor, or Core Data's fetchBatchSize + NSFetchedResultsController; index every sort/filter column; use UICollectionViewDiffableDataSource with NSManagedObjectIDs so cells hold IDs, not objects; compute search server-side or with an FTS index (FTS5) rather than filtering in memory; virtualise section indexes; and stream large JSON payloads with JSONDecoder over an AsyncSequence of bytes rather than materialising a 50 MB Data.

15

Machine Coding Round (Build a Feature in 60–90 Minutes)

11 questions
P0

How to run a machine coding round

Minute 0–5: clarify and state your plan out loud. "I'll do a URLSession service behind a protocol, an @Observable view model with a state enum, a SwiftUI list, and an actor-based image cache. I'll stub the API if the network is unavailable. I'll skip persistence unless you want it."

Minute 5–15: scaffold everything with stubs so it compiles and runs immediately. An app that runs with fake data at minute 15 is worth more than a half-finished perfect one at minute 60.

Minute 15–55: fill in layer by layer, running after every step.

Minute 55–70: edge cases — empty state, error state, loading state, cancellation, no network.

Minute 70–85: one or two meaningful unit tests, and a short README or comment block listing what you'd do with more time.

Rules: commit often if it's a repo. Never spend more than 5 minutes stuck — stub it and move on, saying "I'd come back to this". Narrate constantly; silence reads as being lost.

P0

Task: Searchable, paginated list with images (the classic)

The single most common machine coding task. Here is a complete, compiling solution you should be able to reproduce.

// MARK: - Domain

struct Repo: Identifiable, Equatable, Sendable, Decodable {
    let id: Int
    let name: String
    let description: String?
    let stars: Int
    let ownerAvatarURL: URL?

    enum CodingKeys: String, CodingKey { case id, name, description
        case stars = "stargazers_count", owner }
    enum OwnerKeys: String, CodingKey { case avatarURL = "avatar_url" }

    init(from decoder: Decoder) throws {
        let c = try decoder.container(keyedBy: CodingKeys.self)
        id = try c.decode(Int.self, forKey: .id)
        name = try c.decode(String.self, forKey: .name)
        description = try c.decodeIfPresent(String.self, forKey: .description)
        stars = try c.decodeIfPresent(Int.self, forKey: .stars) ?? 0
        let owner = try c.nestedContainer(keyedBy: OwnerKeys.self, forKey: .owner)
        ownerAvatarURL = try owner.decodeIfPresent(URL.self, forKey: .avatarURL)
    }
}

struct SearchPage: Sendable { let items: [Repo]; let hasMore: Bool }

// MARK: - Service (protocol first, so it's mockable)

protocol RepoSearching: Sendable {
    func search(query: String, page: Int) async throws -> SearchPage
}

struct GitHubSearchService: RepoSearching {
    private struct Response: Decodable { let items: [Repo]; let total_count: Int }
    private let session: URLSession
    private let pageSize = 30
    init(session: URLSession = .shared) { self.session = session }

    func search(query: String, page: Int) async throws -> SearchPage {
        var comps = URLComponents(string: "https://api.github.com/search/repositories")!
        comps.queryItems = [
            .init(name: "q", value: query),
            .init(name: "page", value: String(page)),
            .init(name: "per_page", value: String(pageSize))
        ]
        var request = URLRequest(url: comps.url!)
        request.setValue("application/vnd.github+json", forHTTPHeaderField: "Accept")

        let (data, response) = try await session.data(for: request)
        guard let http = response as? HTTPURLResponse else { throw SearchError.invalidResponse }
        guard 200..<300 ~= http.statusCode else { throw SearchError.http(http.statusCode) }

        let decoded = try JSONDecoder().decode(Response.self, from: data)
        return SearchPage(items: decoded.items,
                          hasMore: page * pageSize < min(decoded.total_count, 1000))
    }
}

enum SearchError: LocalizedError {
    case invalidResponse, http(Int)
    var errorDescription: String? {
        switch self {
        case .invalidResponse: "Unexpected response."
        case .http(403):       "Rate limited — try again in a minute."
        case .http(let code):  "Server error (\(code))."
        }
    }
}

// MARK: - ViewModel

@Observable
@MainActor
final class RepoSearchViewModel {
    enum State: Equatable {
        case idle, loading, loaded([Repo]), empty, failed(String)
    }

    var query: String = "" { didSet { queryDidChange() } }
    private(set) var state: State = .idle
    private(set) var isLoadingMore = false

    private let service: RepoSearching
    private var page = 1
    private var hasMore = true
    private var searchTask: Task<Void, Never>?

    init(service: RepoSearching) { self.service = service }

    private func queryDidChange() {
        searchTask?.cancel()                      // cancel the previous in-flight search
        let q = query.trimmingCharacters(in: .whitespaces)
        guard !q.isEmpty else { state = .idle; return }

        searchTask = Task {
            try? await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(350))     // debounce
            guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
            await performSearch(q)
        }
    }

    private func performSearch(_ q: String) async {
        state = .loading
        page = 1; hasMore = true
        do {
            let result = try await service.search(query: q, page: 1)
            guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
            hasMore = result.hasMore
            state = result.items.isEmpty ? .empty : .loaded(result.items)
        } catch is CancellationError {
            // a newer search superseded this one — do nothing
        } catch {
            guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
            state = .failed(error.localizedDescription)
        }
    }

    func loadMoreIfNeeded(current repo: Repo) async {
        guard case .loaded(let items) = state,
              hasMore, !isLoadingMore,
              items.suffix(5).contains(repo) else { return }
        isLoadingMore = true
        defer { isLoadingMore = false }
        page += 1
        guard let next = try? await service.search(query: query, page: page) else { page -= 1; return }
        let existing = Set(items.map(\.id))
        state = .loaded(items + next.items.filter { !existing.contains($0.id) })
        hasMore = next.hasMore
    }

    func retry() async {
        await performSearch(query.trimmingCharacters(in: .whitespaces))
    }
}

// MARK: - View

struct RepoSearchView: View {
    @State private var viewModel: RepoSearchViewModel

    init(service: RepoSearching = GitHubSearchService()) {
        _viewModel = State(initialValue: RepoSearchViewModel(service: service))
    }

    var body: some View {
        NavigationStack {
            content
                .navigationTitle("Repositories")
                .searchable(text: Bindable(viewModel).query, prompt: "Search GitHub")
        }
    }

    @ViewBuilder private var content: some View {
        switch viewModel.state {
        case .idle:
            ContentUnavailableView("Search repositories", systemImage: "magnifyingglass",
                                   description: Text("Type to begin."))
        case .loading:
            ProgressView().frame(maxWidth: .infinity, maxHeight: .infinity)
        case .empty:
            ContentUnavailableView.search(text: viewModel.query)
        case .failed(let message):
            ContentUnavailableView {
                Label("Something went wrong", systemImage: "exclamationmark.triangle")
            } description: { Text(message) } actions: {
                Button("Retry") { Task { await viewModel.retry() } }
            }
        case .loaded(let repos):
            List {
                ForEach(repos) { repo in
                    RepoRow(repo: repo)
                        .task { await viewModel.loadMoreIfNeeded(current: repo) }
                }
                if viewModel.isLoadingMore {
                    HStack { Spacer(); ProgressView(); Spacer() }
                }
            }
            .listStyle(.plain)
        }
    }
}

struct RepoRow: View {
    let repo: Repo
    var body: some View {
        HStack(spacing: 12) {
            CachedAsyncImage(url: repo.ownerAvatarURL, size: CGSize(width: 44, height: 44))
                .frame(width: 44, height: 44)
                .clipShape(.rect(cornerRadius: 8))
            VStack(alignment: .leading, spacing: 4) {
                Text(repo.name).font(.headline).lineLimit(1)
                if let description = repo.description {
                    Text(description).font(.subheadline).foregroundStyle(.secondary).lineLimit(2)
                }
                Label("\(repo.stars)", systemImage: "star.fill")
                    .font(.caption).foregroundStyle(.orange)
            }
        }
        .padding(.vertical, 4)
    }
}

struct CachedAsyncImage: View {
    let url: URL?
    let size: CGSize
    @State private var image: UIImage?

    var body: some View {
        Group {
            if let image { Image(uiImage: image).resizable().scaledToFill() }
            else { Rectangle().fill(.quaternary) }
        }
        .task(id: url) {
            guard let url else { return }
            image = try? await ImageLoader.shared.image(for: url, targetSize: size)
        }
    }
}

// MARK: - Tests

@Suite @MainActor
struct RepoSearchViewModelTests {
    struct StubService: RepoSearching {
        var pages: [Int: SearchPage] = [:]
        var error: Error?
        func search(query: String, page: Int) async throws -> SearchPage {
            if let error { throw error }
            return pages[page] ?? SearchPage(items: [], hasMore: false)
        }
    }

    @Test func emptyQueryReturnsToIdle() async {
        let vm = RepoSearchViewModel(service: StubService())
        vm.query = "   "
        #expect(vm.state == .idle)
    }

    @Test func successfulSearchLoads() async throws {
        let repos = [Repo.sample]
        let vm = RepoSearchViewModel(service: StubService(pages: [1: .init(items: repos, hasMore: false)]))
        vm.query = "swift"
        try await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(600))   // past the debounce
        #expect(vm.state == .loaded(repos))
    }

    @Test func failureSurfacesMessage() async throws {
        let vm = RepoSearchViewModel(service: StubService(error: SearchError.http(403)))
        vm.query = "swift"
        try await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(600))
        #expect(vm.state == .failed("Rate limited — try again in a minute."))
    }
}

Narrate these choices while you build them: protocol-first service so it's mockable; State as an enum so impossible states can't exist; debounce + cancel on every keystroke; de-duplication on pagination; a real error message per case; ContentUnavailableView for empty/error states; .task(id:) so image loads cancel on reuse.

P0

Task: Debounced search with cancellation (the sub-problem they extract)

If they ask only for this piece, here are both idioms:

// async/await
private var searchTask: Task<Void, Never>?
func search(_ text: String) {
    searchTask?.cancel()
    searchTask = Task {
        try? await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(300))
        guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
        results = (try? await api.search(text)) ?? []
    }
}

// Combine
$query
    .debounce(for: .milliseconds(300), scheduler: DispatchQueue.main)
    .removeDuplicates()
    .map { [api] q in api.searchPublisher(q).catch { _ in Just([]) } }
    .switchToLatest()                                // cancels the previous request
    .receive(on: DispatchQueue.main)
    .assign(to: &$results)

// AsyncAlgorithms
for await q in queryStream.debounce(for: .milliseconds(300)).removeDuplicates() {
    results = try await api.search(q)
}

debounce vs throttle: debounce waits for a quiet period and emits the last value (typing); throttle emits at most once per interval (scroll position, analytics). Know the difference — it gets asked directly.

P1

Task: Build an LRU cache

final class LRUCache<Key: Hashable, Value> {
    private final class Node {
        let key: Key
        var value: Value
        var prev: Node?
        weak var next: Node?          // one direction weak? NO — see note below
        init(key: Key, value: Value) { self.key = key; self.value = value }
    }

    private var map: [Key: Node] = [:]
    private var head: Node?           // most recently used
    private var tail: Node?           // least recently used
    private let capacity: Int
    private let lock = NSLock()

    init(capacity: Int) { precondition(capacity > 0); self.capacity = capacity }

    func value(for key: Key) -> Value? {
        lock.withLock {
            guard let node = map[key] else { return nil }
            moveToHead(node)
            return node.value
        }
    }

    func set(_ value: Value, for key: Key) {
        lock.withLock {
            if let node = map[key] {
                node.value = value
                moveToHead(node)
                return
            }
            let node = Node(key: key, value: value)
            map[key] = node
            addToHead(node)
            if map.count > capacity, let lru = tail {
                remove(lru)
                map[lru.key] = nil
            }
        }
    }

    // MARK: doubly-linked list ops — all O(1)
    private func addToHead(_ node: Node) {
        node.prev = nil
        node.next = head
        head?.prev = node
        head = node
        if tail == nil { tail = node }
    }
    private func remove(_ node: Node) {
        node.prev?.next = node.next
        node.next?.prev = node.prev
        if head === node { head = node.next }
        if tail === node { tail = node.prev }
        node.prev = nil; node.next = nil
    }
    private func moveToHead(_ node: Node) { remove(node); addToHead(node) }
}

Careful — the retain-cycle discussion they want: a doubly-linked list with both pointers strong is a cycle between every adjacent pair. The usual fixes: make prev weak (nodes are kept alive by the dictionary and by next), or keep both strong and explicitly break links on removal (as above, node.prev = nil; node.next = nil). Actually stating this trade-off out loud is the point of the question. The dictionary-owns-nodes + strong next + weak prev design is the cleanest.

Simpler answer if they'll accept it: an ordered dictionary — [Key: Value] plus a [Key] recency array — is O(n) on access because of the array removal. Say that you could do it, and that the linked list exists to make everything O(1).

P1

Task: Thread-safe counter / rate limiter

// Token bucket rate limiter — "max N requests per interval"
actor RateLimiter {
    private let capacity: Int
    private let refillInterval: Duration
    private var tokens: Int
    private var lastRefill: ContinuousClock.Instant

    init(capacity: Int, per interval: Duration) {
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.refillInterval = interval
        self.tokens = capacity
        self.lastRefill = .now
    }

    func acquire() async throws {
        while true {
            refill()
            if tokens > 0 { tokens -= 1; return }
            let wait = refillInterval - (ContinuousClock.now - lastRefill)
            try await Task.sleep(for: max(wait, .milliseconds(10)))
        }
    }

    private func refill() {
        let elapsed = ContinuousClock.now - lastRefill
        guard elapsed >= refillInterval else { return }
        let periods = Int(elapsed / refillInterval)
        tokens = min(capacity, tokens + periods * capacity)
        lastRefill = .now
    }
}
P1

Task: Build a simple dependency injection container

final class Container: @unchecked Sendable {
    private var factories: [ObjectIdentifier: () -> Any] = [:]
    private var singletons: [ObjectIdentifier: Any] = [:]
    private let lock = NSLock()

    func register<T>(_ type: T.Type, scope: Scope = .transient, factory: @escaping () -> T) {
        lock.withLock {
            factories[ObjectIdentifier(type)] = factory
            if case .singleton = scope { singletons[ObjectIdentifier(type)] = nil }
        }
    }

    func resolve<T>(_ type: T.Type = T.self) -> T {
        lock.withLock {
            let key = ObjectIdentifier(type)
            if let existing = singletons[key] as? T { return existing }
            guard let factory = factories[key], let value = factory() as? T else {
                fatalError("No registration for \(type)")
            }
            if singletons.index(forKey: key) != nil { singletons[key] = value }
            return value
        }
    }
    enum Scope { case transient, singleton }
}

Then immediately say why you'd not ship this: it resolves at runtime so a missing registration is a crash instead of a compile error, it hides dependencies, and it makes tests order-dependent. Initializer injection with a hand-written composition root is compile-time safe and usually better. Showing the code and the judgement is the answer.

P2

Task: Infinite scrolling with a UICollectionView (UIKit variant)

final class FeedViewController: UIViewController {
    private lazy var collectionView = makeCollectionView()
    private lazy var dataSource = makeDataSource()
    private let viewModel: FeedViewModel
    private var isLoadingPage = false

    override func viewDidLoad() {
        super.viewDidLoad()
        collectionView.delegate = self
        collectionView.prefetchDataSource = self
        Task { await load() }
    }

    private func apply(_ posts: [Post], animated: Bool) {
        var snapshot = NSDiffableDataSourceSnapshot<Int, Post.ID>()
        snapshot.appendSections([0])
        snapshot.appendItems(posts.map(\.id))
        dataSource.apply(snapshot, animatingDifferences: animated)
    }
}

extension FeedViewController: UICollectionViewDelegate {
    func collectionView(_ cv: UICollectionView, willDisplay cell: UICollectionViewCell,
                        forItemAt indexPath: IndexPath) {
        guard indexPath.item >= viewModel.posts.count - 5, !isLoadingPage else { return }
        isLoadingPage = true
        Task { await viewModel.loadNextPage(); isLoadingPage = false }
    }
}

extension FeedViewController: UICollectionViewDataSourcePrefetching {
    func collectionView(_ cv: UICollectionView, prefetchItemsAt indexPaths: [IndexPath]) {
        for ip in indexPaths {
            guard let url = viewModel.posts[safe: ip.item]?.imageURL else { continue }
            Task.detached(priority: .low) { _ = try? await ImageLoader.shared.image(for: url, targetSize: .thumb) }
        }
    }
    func collectionView(_ cv: UICollectionView, cancelPrefetchingForItemsAt indexPaths: [IndexPath]) {
        // cancel the prefetch tasks you started
    }
}
P2

Task: Form validation with live feedback

enum ValidationRule {
    case nonEmpty(field: String)
    case minLength(Int, field: String)
    case email
    case matches(other: String, field: String)

    func validate(_ value: String) -> String? {
        switch self {
        case .nonEmpty(let f):       value.isEmpty ? "\(f) is required" : nil
        case .minLength(let n, let f): value.count < n ? "\(f) must be at least \(n) characters" : nil
        case .email:                 value.contains(/^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$/) ? nil : "Enter a valid email"
        case .matches(let other, let f): value == other ? nil : "\(f) doesn't match"
        }
    }
}

@Observable @MainActor
final class SignUpViewModel {
    var email = "" { didSet { validate(.email, email, into: \.emailError) } }
    var password = "" { didSet { validate(.minLength(8, field: "Password"), password, into: \.passwordError) } }

    private(set) var emailError: String?
    private(set) var passwordError: String?
    private(set) var hasSubmitted = false

    var isValid: Bool { emailError == nil && passwordError == nil && !email.isEmpty && !password.isEmpty }

    private func validate(_ rule: ValidationRule, _ value: String,
                          into keyPath: ReferenceWritableKeyPath<SignUpViewModel, String?>) {
        // Only show errors after the first submit, or after the field loses focus —
        // showing "required" as soon as the user starts typing is hostile UX.
        guard hasSubmitted else { self[keyPath: keyPath] = nil; return }
        self[keyPath: keyPath] = rule.validate(value)
    }
}

The UX point — don't show errors while the user is still typing the first time — is the kind of detail that makes an interviewer nod.

P2

Task: Pull-to-refresh + empty + error + loading states done properly

enum LoadState<Value> {
    case idle
    case loading                     // first load, show a spinner
    case refreshing(Value)           // pull-to-refresh, keep showing old data
    case loaded(Value)
    case empty
    case failed(Error, retry: () async -> Void)

    var value: Value? {
        switch self {
        case .loaded(let v), .refreshing(let v): v
        default: nil
        }
    }
}

The insight: refreshing carries the old value so the list doesn't blank out during a pull-to-refresh. Most candidates model this as isLoading: Bool and end up flashing an empty screen. Distinguishing "first load" from "refresh" and "load more" is what makes the UI feel right.

P3

Task: Implement a simple image editor / drawing canvas

Talking points if this comes up: PencilKit (PKCanvasView) if freehand drawing is the goal — it's free and pressure-sensitive; otherwise a UIView subclass accumulating UIBezierPaths from touchesMoved, drawing into a CAShapeLayer per stroke (not redrawing all strokes in draw(_:), which gets slower per stroke); flatten to a bitmap every N strokes to bound cost; CIFilter/CIContext for filters with a downsampled preview and full-res only on export; undo via a stack of commands, not snapshots.

P3

What to say when you run out of time

Never go silent. Say: "With the remaining time I'd add X, Y, Z — here's what I'd do for each." Then actually list them concretely: "persist the last page to disk so a cold launch shows something; add a URLProtocol-based test for the service; move the image cache to a two-tier memory+disk design; add accessibility identifiers and Dynamic Type support." A clear statement of what's missing and why reads as senior; pretending it's finished reads as junior.
