Data Structures & Algorithms in Swift

14 questions
P0

Complexity of Swift's standard collections

OperationArrayDictionary / SetString
Access by index/keyO(1)O(1) average, O(n) worstO(n) — indices aren't integers
AppendO(1) amortisedO(1) averageO(1) amortised
Insert/remove at frontO(n)—O(n)
containsO(n)O(1) averageO(n)
SortO(n log n)——
removeFirst()O(n)—O(n)

Swift-specific facts worth stating: Array.removeFirst() is O(n) — use an index pointer or Deque from swift-collections for a queue. reserveCapacity avoids repeated reallocation. Copy-on-write means passing an array is O(1) until mutated. Set/Dictionary use Hasher with a per-process random seed, so iteration order is not stable across runs — never rely on it.

P0

Arrays and Two Pointers

// Two Sum — O(n) with a hash map
func twoSum(_ nums: [Int], _ target: Int) -> [Int] {
    var seen: [Int: Int] = [:]                 // value -> index
    for (i, n) in nums.enumerated() {
        if let j = seen[target - n] { return [j, i] }
        seen[n] = i
    }
    return []
}

// Container With Most Water — two pointers, O(n)
func maxArea(_ height: [Int]) -> Int {
    var l = 0, r = height.count - 1, best = 0
    while l < r {
        best = max(best, min(height[l], height[r]) * (r - l))
        if height[l] < height[r] { l += 1 } else { r -= 1 }
    }
    return best
}

// Remove duplicates from a SORTED array, in place — O(n), O(1) space
func removeDuplicates(_ nums: inout [Int]) -> Int {
    guard !nums.isEmpty else { return 0 }
    var write = 1
    for read in 1..<nums.count where nums[read] != nums[write - 1] {
        nums[write] = nums[read]; write += 1
    }
    return write
}

// Merge intervals — O(n log n)
func merge(_ intervals: [[Int]]) -> [[Int]] {
    guard !intervals.isEmpty else { return [] }
    let sorted = intervals.sorted { $0[0] < $1[0] }
    var out = [sorted[0]]
    for interval in sorted.dropFirst() {
        if interval[0] <= out[out.count - 1][1] {
            out[out.count - 1][1] = max(out[out.count - 1][1], interval[1])
        } else { out.append(interval) }
    }
    return out
}
P0

Sliding Window

// Longest substring without repeating characters — O(n)
func lengthOfLongestSubstring(_ s: String) -> Int {
    var lastIndex: [Character: Int] = [:]
    var start = 0, best = 0
    for (i, ch) in Array(s).enumerated() {          // Array(s) makes indexing O(1)
        if let seen = lastIndex[ch], seen >= start { start = seen + 1 }
        lastIndex[ch] = i
        best = max(best, i - start + 1)
    }
    return best
}

// Maximum sum subarray of size k — fixed window
func maxSum(_ nums: [Int], _ k: Int) -> Int {
    guard nums.count >= k else { return 0 }
    var windowSum = nums[0..<k].reduce(0, +)
    var best = windowSum
    for i in k..<nums.count {
        windowSum += nums[i] - nums[i - k]
        best = max(best, windowSum)
    }
    return best
}

// Kadane's — maximum subarray sum, O(n)
func maxSubArray(_ nums: [Int]) -> Int {
    var current = nums[0], best = nums[0]
    for n in nums.dropFirst() {
        current = max(n, current + n)
        best = max(best, current)
    }
    return best
}
P0

Hash maps and frequency counting

// Group anagrams — O(n · k log k)
func groupAnagrams(_ strs: [String]) -> [[String]] {
    var groups: [String: [String]] = [:]
    for s in strs { groups[String(s.sorted()), default: []].append(s) }
    return Array(groups.values)
}

// Top K frequent elements — bucket sort, O(n)
func topKFrequent(_ nums: [Int], _ k: Int) -> [Int] {
    var counts: [Int: Int] = [:]
    for n in nums { counts[n, default: 0] += 1 }
    var buckets = [[Int]](repeating: [], count: nums.count + 1)
    for (n, c) in counts { buckets[c].append(n) }
    var out: [Int] = []
    for c in stride(from: buckets.count - 1, through: 1, by: -1) {
        out += buckets[c]
        if out.count >= k { return Array(out.prefix(k)) }
    }
    return out
}

// First non-repeating character
func firstUniqChar(_ s: String) -> Int {
    let chars = Array(s)
    var counts: [Character: Int] = [:]
    for c in chars { counts[c, default: 0] += 1 }
    return chars.firstIndex { counts[$0] == 1 } ?? -1
}

Swift note they may probe: counts[n, default: 0] += 1 is a single hash lookup with in-place mutation, not three lookups. Knowing that shows you understand the _modify accessor.

P0

Strings

func isPalindrome(_ s: String) -> Bool {
    let cleaned = s.lowercased().filter(\.isLetter)      // or .isLetter || .isNumber
    return cleaned == String(cleaned.reversed())
}
// O(1) space version with two pointers over an Array(s)

func reverseWords(_ s: String) -> String {
    s.split(separator: " ", omittingEmptySubsequences: true).reversed().joined(separator: " ")
}

func isAnagram(_ s: String, _ t: String) -> Bool {
    guard s.count == t.count else { return false }
    var counts: [Character: Int] = [:]
    for c in s { counts[c, default: 0] += 1 }
    for c in t {
        guard let n = counts[c], n > 0 else { return false }
        counts[c] = n - 1
    }
    return true
}

// String compression — "aabcccccaaa" -> "a2b1c5a3"
func compress(_ s: String) -> String {
    var out = "", chars = Array(s), i = 0
    while i < chars.count {
        let c = chars[i]; var run = 0
        while i < chars.count && chars[i] == c { run += 1; i += 1 }
        out += "\(c)\(run)"
    }
    return out.count < s.count ? out : s
}

Always mention: convert to Array(s) when you need random access, because String indices are not integers and s[s.index(s.startIndex, offsetBy: i)] inside a loop turns an O(n) algorithm into O(n²). This is the Swift-specific string gotcha interviewers listen for.

P1

Linked lists

final class ListNode {
    var val: Int
    var next: ListNode?
    init(_ val: Int, _ next: ListNode? = nil) { self.val = val; self.next = next }
}

func reverseList(_ head: ListNode?) -> ListNode? {
    var prev: ListNode?, current = head
    while let node = current {
        current = node.next
        node.next = prev
        prev = node
    }
    return prev
}

// Cycle detection — Floyd's tortoise and hare
func hasCycle(_ head: ListNode?) -> Bool {
    var slow = head, fast = head
    while fast != nil && fast?.next != nil {
        slow = slow?.next
        fast = fast?.next?.next
        if slow === fast { return true }      // === identity, not ==
    }
    return false
}

func middleNode(_ head: ListNode?) -> ListNode? {
    var slow = head, fast = head
    while fast != nil && fast?.next != nil { slow = slow?.next; fast = fast?.next?.next }
    return slow
}

func mergeTwoLists(_ l1: ListNode?, _ l2: ListNode?) -> ListNode? {
    let dummy = ListNode(0)
    var tail = dummy, a = l1, b = l2
    while let x = a, let y = b {
        if x.val <= y.val { tail.next = x; a = x.next } else { tail.next = y; b = y.next }
        tail = tail.next!
    }
    tail.next = a ?? b
    return dummy.next
}

Swift-specific: use === for node identity. And a linked list built with strong next and strong prev leaks — this connects straight back to the ARC chapter, and interviewers love the link.

P1

Trees

final class TreeNode {
    var val: Int; var left: TreeNode?; var right: TreeNode?
    init(_ val: Int) { self.val = val }
}

// DFS traversals
func inorder(_ root: TreeNode?, into out: inout [Int]) {
    guard let root else { return }
    inorder(root.left, into: &out); out.append(root.val); inorder(root.right, into: &out)
}

// BFS / level order — the pattern you'll actually be asked for
func levelOrder(_ root: TreeNode?) -> [[Int]] {
    guard let root else { return [] }
    var result: [[Int]] = [], queue: [TreeNode] = [root]
    while !queue.isEmpty {
        var level: [Int] = [], next: [TreeNode] = []
        for node in queue {
            level.append(node.val)
            if let l = node.left  { next.append(l) }
            if let r = node.right { next.append(r) }
        }
        result.append(level)
        queue = next                     // swapping arrays avoids O(n) removeFirst()
    }
    return result
}

func maxDepth(_ root: TreeNode?) -> Int {
    guard let root else { return 0 }
    return 1 + max(maxDepth(root.left), maxDepth(root.right))
}

func isValidBST(_ root: TreeNode?, _ low: Int = .min, _ high: Int = .max) -> Bool {
    guard let root else { return true }
    guard root.val > low && root.val < high else { return false }
    return isValidBST(root.left, low, root.val) && isValidBST(root.right, root.val, high)
}

func lowestCommonAncestor(_ root: TreeNode?, _ p: TreeNode, _ q: TreeNode) -> TreeNode? {
    guard let root else { return nil }
    if root === p || root === q { return root }
    let left = lowestCommonAncestor(root.left, p, q)
    let right = lowestCommonAncestor(root.right, p, q)
    if left != nil && right != nil { return root }
    return left ?? right
}

The iOS connection to mention: the view hierarchy is a tree, so "find the first responder", "find all subviews of type T", and "compute a view's depth" are the same traversals.

extension UIView {
    func firstSubview<T: UIView>(of type: T.Type) -> T? {
        for sub in subviews {
            if let match = sub as? T { return match }
            if let nested = sub.firstSubview(of: type) { return nested }
        }
        return nil
    }
}
P1

Sorting and searching

// Binary search — know the exact boundary conditions
func binarySearch(_ nums: [Int], _ target: Int) -> Int {
    var lo = 0, hi = nums.count - 1
    while lo <= hi {
        let mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2          // avoids overflow
        if nums[mid] == target { return mid }
        if nums[mid] < target { lo = mid + 1 } else { hi = mid - 1 }
    }
    return -1
}

// Lower bound (first index >= target) — the version that's actually useful
func lowerBound(_ nums: [Int], _ target: Int) -> Int {
    var lo = 0, hi = nums.count
    while lo < hi {
        let mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2
        if nums[mid] < target { lo = mid + 1 } else { hi = mid }
    }
    return lo
}

// Custom sorting in Swift
people.sorted { ($0.lastName, $0.firstName) < ($1.lastName, $1.firstName) }   // tuple comparison
people.sorted(using: [KeyPathComparator(\.age, order: .reverse), KeyPathComparator(\.name)])

sorted(by:) uses introsort (hybrid quicksort/heapsort/insertion) and is not stable. If you need stability, decorate with the original index. That's a real Swift gotcha.

P2

Graphs, BFS/DFS, and topological sort

func numIslands(_ grid: [[Character]]) -> Int {
    var grid = grid, count = 0
    let rows = grid.count, cols = grid[0].count

    func sink(_ r: Int, _ c: Int) {
        guard r >= 0, r < rows, c >= 0, c < cols, grid[r][c] == "1" else { return }
        grid[r][c] = "0"
        sink(r+1, c); sink(r-1, c); sink(r, c+1); sink(r, c-1)
    }
    for r in 0..<rows { for c in 0..<cols where grid[r][c] == "1" { count += 1; sink(r, c) } }
    return count
}

// Topological sort (Kahn's) — "course schedule", dependency resolution
func topologicalSort(nodes: [Int], edges: [(Int, Int)]) -> [Int]? {
    var adjacency = [Int: [Int]](), inDegree = [Int: Int]()
    nodes.forEach { inDegree[$0] = 0 }
    for (from, to) in edges { adjacency[from, default: []].append(to); inDegree[to, default: 0] += 1 }

    var queue = nodes.filter { inDegree[$0] == 0 }
    var order: [Int] = []
    var head = 0
    while head < queue.count {
        let node = queue[head]; head += 1        // index pointer, not removeFirst()
        order.append(node)
        for next in adjacency[node] ?? [] {
            inDegree[next]! -= 1
            if inDegree[next] == 0 { queue.append(next) }
        }
    }
    return order.count == nodes.count ? order : nil      // nil == cycle detected
}

iOS relevance: topological sort is exactly how a build system orders targets and how you'd resolve a dependency graph of Operations.

P2

Dynamic programming — the ones that appear

// Climbing stairs / Fibonacci — O(n) time, O(1) space
func climbStairs(_ n: Int) -> Int {
    guard n > 1 else { return 1 }
    var a = 1, b = 1
    for _ in 2...n { (a, b) = (b, a + b) }
    return b
}

// Coin change — minimum coins
func coinChange(_ coins: [Int], _ amount: Int) -> Int {
    var dp = [Int](repeating: amount + 1, count: amount + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    for value in 1...max(amount, 1) {
        for coin in coins where coin <= value {
            dp[value] = min(dp[value], dp[value - coin] + 1)
        }
    }
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount]
}

// Longest common subsequence
func lcs(_ a: String, _ b: String) -> Int {
    let x = Array(a), y = Array(b)
    var dp = [[Int]](repeating: [Int](repeating: 0, count: y.count + 1), count: x.count + 1)
    for i in 1...max(x.count, 1) where !x.isEmpty {
        for j in 1...max(y.count, 1) where !y.isEmpty {
            dp[i][j] = x[i-1] == y[j-1] ? dp[i-1][j-1] + 1 : max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1])
        }
    }
    return dp[x.count][y.count]
}

// Memoization with a dictionary
func fibMemo(_ n: Int, _ memo: inout [Int: Int]) -> Int {
    if n <= 1 { return n }
    if let cached = memo[n] { return cached }
    let result = fibMemo(n-1, &memo) + fibMemo(n-2, &memo)
    memo[n] = result
    return result
}
P2

Stacks, queues, and heaps in Swift

// Stack — just an Array
var stack: [Int] = []
stack.append(1); stack.popLast()

// Queue — Array.removeFirst() is O(n)! Use an index-based queue:
struct Queue<T> {
    private var storage: [T] = []
    private var head = 0
    var isEmpty: Bool { head >= storage.count }
    mutating func enqueue(_ x: T) { storage.append(x) }
    mutating func dequeue() -> T? {
        guard head < storage.count else { return nil }
        defer {
            head += 1
            if head > 50 && head * 2 >= storage.count {   // compact occasionally
                storage.removeFirst(head); head = 0
            }
        }
        return storage[head]
    }
}

// Heap — Swift has no built-in one. Use swift-collections' Heap, or write it:
struct MinHeap<T: Comparable> {
    private var items: [T] = []
    var isEmpty: Bool { items.isEmpty }
    var min: T? { items.first }

    mutating func insert(_ x: T) {
        items.append(x)
        var i = items.count - 1
        while i > 0 {
            let parent = (i - 1) / 2
            guard items[i] < items[parent] else { break }
            items.swapAt(i, parent); i = parent
        }
    }

    mutating func removeMin() -> T? {
        guard !items.isEmpty else { return nil }
        items.swapAt(0, items.count - 1)
        let result = items.removeLast()
        var i = 0
        while true {
            let l = 2*i + 1, r = 2*i + 2
            var smallest = i
            if l < items.count && items[l] < items[smallest] { smallest = l }
            if r < items.count && items[r] < items[smallest] { smallest = r }
            if smallest == i { break }
            items.swapAt(i, smallest); i = smallest
        }
        return result
    }
}

Mentioning swift-collections (Deque, Heap, OrderedSet, OrderedDictionary, BitSet) is a good move — it's Apple's package and knowing it says you've read the ecosystem.

P2

Valid parentheses, stack problems

func isValid(_ s: String) -> Bool {
    let pairs: [Character: Character] = [")": "(", "]": "[", "}": "{"]
    var stack: [Character] = []
    for ch in s {
        if let opening = pairs[ch] {
            guard stack.popLast() == opening else { return false }
        } else { stack.append(ch) }
    }
    return stack.isEmpty
}

// Min stack — O(1) getMin
struct MinStack {
    private var stack: [(value: Int, min: Int)] = []
    mutating func push(_ x: Int) { stack.append((x, Swift.min(x, stack.last?.min ?? x))) }
    mutating func pop() { stack.removeLast() }
    var top: Int? { stack.last?.value }
    var min: Int? { stack.last?.min }
}

Bit manipulation

func singleNumber(_ nums: [Int]) -> Int { nums.reduce(0, ^) }        // XOR cancels pairs
func countBits(_ n: Int) -> Int { n.nonzeroBitCount }                 // built in!
func isPowerOfTwo(_ n: Int) -> Bool { n > 0 && n & (n - 1) == 0 }
func reverseBits(_ n: UInt32) -> UInt32 { UInt32(bitPattern: Int32(n)).byteSwapped }
// Swift built-ins worth knowing:
// n.leadingZeroBitCount, n.trailingZeroBitCount, n.nonzeroBitCount, n.bitWidth
// Int.max, Int.min, &+ &- &* (wrapping), n.multipliedReportingOverflow(by:)
P3

Recursion, backtracking, and how to talk about complexity

func permutations<T>(_ items: [T]) -> [[T]] {
    guard items.count > 1 else { return [items] }
    var result: [[T]] = []
    for (i, item) in items.enumerated() {
        var rest = items; rest.remove(at: i)
        for perm in permutations(rest) { result.append([item] + perm) }
    }
    return result
}

func subsets<T>(_ nums: [T]) -> [[T]] {
    var result: [[T]] = [[]]
    for n in nums { result += result.map { $0 + [n] } }
    return result
}

How to state complexity in an interview: always give time and space, say what n is, and mention the worst case separately from the average. "This is O(n) time and O(n) space, where n is the number of characters — worst case the hash map holds every distinct character, which for Unicode is bounded but large." That level of precision is what distinguishes a mid-level answer.

17

Objective-C & Interoperability

11 questions
P0

What's the difference between strong, weak, copy, assign, retain?

@property (nonatomic, strong) NSMutableArray *items;   // retains
@property (nonatomic, weak) id<MyDelegate> delegate;   // zeroing weak, no retain
@property (nonatomic, copy) NSString *title;           // copies on set
@property (nonatomic, assign) NSInteger count;         // primitives, no memory management
@property (nonatomic, unsafe_unretained) id legacy;    // like Swift's unowned(unsafe)
@property (atomic, strong) id threadSafeIsh;           // getter/setter are atomic, NOT thread-safe
  • strong (= retain under ARC): +1 retain count.
  • weak: no retain, zeroed on dealloc — same as Swift's weak.
  • assign: for scalars (NSInteger, BOOL, CGFloat). Using it on an object gives you a dangling pointer.
  • copy: the interview question. Always use copy for NSString, NSArray, NSDictionary, and blocks. Why:
NSMutableString *name = [NSMutableString stringWithString:@"Alice"];
person.title = name;             // with `strong`, person.title IS name
[name appendString:@" Smith"];   // person.title silently changed!
                                 // with `copy`, person.title is an immutable snapshot
  • atomic vs nonatomic: atomic (the default!) makes the individual getter/setter calls thread-safe by locking, but gives you no guarantee about the object's overall state — array being atomic doesn't make [array addObject:] safe. It costs performance, so virtually all real code uses nonatomic. Saying "atomic is not thread safety, it's just an atomic read/write of the pointer" is the answer they want.
P0

How does objc_msgSend / message passing work?

Objective-C doesn't "call methods", it sends messages. [obj doThing] compiles to objc_msgSend(obj, @selector(doThing)), which at runtime:

  1. Looks up obj's isa pointer to find its class.
  2. Checks the class's method cache for the selector.
  3. On a miss, walks the class's method list, then the superclass chain.
  4. If nothing is found, enters message forwarding.

Message forwarding — the three chances:

+ (BOOL)resolveInstanceMethod:(SEL)sel;    // 1. add an implementation dynamically
- (id)forwardingTargetForSelector:(SEL)sel;// 2. hand the message to another object (fast)
- (void)forwardInvocation:(NSInvocation *)inv;  // 3. full NSInvocation, slow but powerful
- (NSMethodSignature *)methodSignatureForSelector:(SEL)sel;  // required for step 3
// If all fail -> doesNotRecognizeSelector: -> "unrecognized selector sent to instance" crash

Why this matters: it's why sending a message to nil is safe and returns 0/nil (Swift's optional chaining is the direct descendant), why KVO and swizzling work, why respondsToSelector: exists, and why Objective-C is much more dynamic — and slower per call — than Swift's static dispatch.

Consequence for Swift: a Swift method is only visible to the ObjC runtime if it's @objc, and only dynamically dispatched if it's @objc dynamic. That's why #selector, KVO, and NSCoding require @objc.

P0

Bridging: Swift ↔ Objective-C

Using ObjC from Swift: add a bridging header (ProjectName-Bridging-Header.h) in an app target and #import the ObjC headers. In a framework, import the umbrella header via a module map — the header must be Public.

Using Swift from ObjC: import the generated header:

#import "ProductName-Swift.h"

Only @objc-exposed Swift appears in it. A Swift class must inherit from NSObject (or be @objc and @objcMembers) to be usable.

@objc final class Analytics: NSObject {
    @objc func track(_ name: String) { }
    @objc(trackEventWithName:properties:)      // control the ObjC selector name
    func track(name: String, properties: [String: Any]) { }
}
@objcMembers final class Everything: NSObject { }   // exposes all members

What cannot be exposed to ObjC: structs (other than a small bridged set), enums with associated values, generics, tuples, protocols with associated types, Any existentials of Swift-only protocols, nested types, typed throws, and most Swift-only features. Know this list roughly — "why can't my Swift enum be seen from ObjC?" is a real question.

Nullability annotations matter enormously: un-annotated ObjC pointers come into Swift as implicitly unwrapped optionals (String!), which crash later. Annotate the ObjC headers:

NS_ASSUME_NONNULL_BEGIN
@interface User : NSObject
@property (nonatomic, copy) NSString *name;              // -> String
@property (nonatomic, copy, nullable) NSString *nickname; // -> String?
- (nullable User *)parent;
NS_ASSUME_NONNULL_END

Other bridging annotations: NS_SWIFT_NAME, NS_NOESCAPE, NS_REFINED_FOR_SWIFT, NS_ERROR_ENUM, NS_SWIFT_UNAVAILABLE, and NS_ENUM/NS_OPTIONS (which bridge to Swift enum and OptionSet respectively — NS_ENUM vs a bare enum is a classic question).

P1

Blocks vs closures, and __block / __weak

// Block syntax: returnType (^name)(params)
void (^completion)(NSError * _Nullable) = ^(NSError *error) { NSLog(@"%@", error); };

// Retain cycle and the fix — the weak/strong dance
__weak typeof(self) weakSelf = self;
self.completion = ^{
    __strong typeof(weakSelf) strongSelf = weakSelf;   // pin it for the block's duration
    if (!strongSelf) return;
    [strongSelf doSomething];
    [strongSelf doSomethingElse];       // both calls see the same non-nil object
};

The weakSelf/strongSelf dance is the ObjC equivalent of [weak self] in guard let self else { return }. The reason it exists: without pinning, weakSelf could become nil between two statements inside the block.

__block makes a captured variable mutable inside the block (and, pre-ARC, was also used to avoid retaining). Blocks capture by value by default and are stack-allocated until copied to the heap — hence the copy property attribute for blocks.

P1

KVO and KVC

// KVC — string-keyed access
[object setValue:@"Alice" forKey:@"name"];
id value = [object valueForKeyPath:@"address.city"];

// KVO
[object addObserver:self forKeyPath:@"progress"
            options:NSKeyValueObservingOptionNew | NSKeyValueObservingOptionOld
            context:&MyContext];
- (void)observeValueForKeyPath:(NSString *)keyPath ofObject:(id)object
                        change:(NSDictionary *)change context:(void *)context { }
[object removeObserver:self forKeyPath:@"progress" context:&MyContext];  // or CRASH on dealloc

How KVO works under the hood — a great "do you know the runtime" question: when you first observe an object, the runtime dynamically creates a subclass (NSKVONotifying_MyClass), swizzles the setter in it to call willChangeValueForKey:/didChangeValueForKey:, and swaps the object's isa pointer to that subclass (isa-swizzling). It also overrides -class to lie and return the original class, so you don't notice.

Modern Swift KVO:

let observation = player.observe(\.timeControlStatus, options: [.new]) { player, change in
    print(change.newValue as Any)
}
// Requires: @objc dynamic var on the observed property, class must inherit NSObject

The classic bugs: forgetting removeObserver before dealloc (crash: "was deallocated while key value observers were still registered"), observing a property that isn't @objc dynamic (silently never fires), and not using a context pointer so a superclass's observation and yours collide.

P1

NSObject lifecycle: alloc/init, dealloc, copy, isEqual/hash

- (instancetype)initWithName:(NSString *)name {
    self = [super init];                 // must assign — super may return a different object
    if (self) { _name = [name copy]; }
    return self;
}

- (BOOL)isEqual:(id)object {
    if (self == object) return YES;
    if (![object isKindOfClass:[User class]]) return NO;
    return [self.name isEqualToString:((User *)object).name];
}
- (NSUInteger)hash { return self.name.hash; }

- (id)copyWithZone:(NSZone *)zone {      // NSCopying
    User *copy = [[[self class] allocWithZone:zone] init];
    copy->_name = [_name copy];
    return copy;
}

- (void)dealloc {
    [[NSNotificationCenter defaultCenter] removeObserver:self];
    // no [super dealloc] under ARC
}

isKindOfClass: (class or subclass) vs isMemberOfClass: (exact class) is a small but frequently-asked distinction. Same contract as Swift: equal objects must have equal hashes.

P2

@synthesize, @dynamic, ivars, and categories vs extensions

// Category — add methods to an existing class, even one you don't own
@interface NSString (Trimming)
- (NSString *)trimmed;
@end

// Class extension — private interface, only in the .m file
@interface MyViewController ()
@property (nonatomic, strong) UILabel *privateLabel;   // ivars can be declared here too
@end

Category vs extension: a category can be in any file and cannot add stored properties (only methods, and "properties" backed by associated objects); an extension must be in the class's own implementation file at compile time and can add ivars and properties. Two categories defining the same method have undefined resolution order — a real source of bugs and a good thing to mention.

Associated objects are how you fake a stored property in a category:

objc_setAssociatedObject(self, &kMyKey, value, OBJC_ASSOCIATION_RETAIN_NONATOMIC);
id value = objc_getAssociatedObject(self, &kMyKey);

@dynamic tells the compiler the accessors will be provided at runtime (Core Data uses it). @synthesize is automatic since Xcode 4.4; you only write it to rename the backing ivar.

P2

Method swizzling — how, and why you shouldn't

+ (void)load {
    static dispatch_once_t onceToken;
    dispatch_once(&onceToken, ^{
        Class class = [self class];
        Method original = class_getInstanceMethod(class, @selector(viewDidAppear:));
        Method swizzled = class_getInstanceMethod(class, @selector(xxx_viewDidAppear:));

        BOOL added = class_addMethod(class, @selector(viewDidAppear:),
                                     method_getImplementation(swizzled),
                                     method_getTypeEncoding(swizzled));
        if (added) {
            class_replaceMethod(class, @selector(xxx_viewDidAppear:),
                                method_getImplementation(original),
                                method_getTypeEncoding(original));
        } else {
            method_exchangeImplementations(original, swizzled);
        }
    });
}

- (void)xxx_viewDidAppear:(BOOL)animated {
    [self xxx_viewDidAppear:animated];   // NOT recursion — this now calls the original
    [Analytics track:NSStringFromClass([self class])];
}

Why the class_addMethod dance: if the method is inherited rather than defined on this class, plain exchange would modify the superclass's implementation for everyone.

Argue against it: ordering between libraries is undefined, it breaks when Apple changes an implementation, it makes crash reports confusing, it's invisible to anyone reading the code, and two SDKs swizzling the same method can infinitely recurse. Legitimate uses are analytics/APM SDKs and test instrumentation. In app code, prefer subclassing, composition, or a UIViewController base class.

P2

Common Foundation classes and their Swift equivalents

Objective-CSwift
NSString / NSMutableStringString (value type, always mutable via var)
NSArray / NSMutableArrayArray
NSDictionaryDictionary
NSNumberInt, Double, Bool, NSNumber when bridging
NSDataData
NSDateDate
NSError **throws
NSNullnil (but NSNull is a real object used in JSON!)
idAny / AnyObject
SELSelector
ClassAnyClass
NSRangeRange<String.Index> (needs conversion — see Chapter 1)
NS_ENUMenum with raw value
NS_OPTIONSOptionSet

The NSNull one catches people: decoding JSON with JSONSerialization gives you NSNull for null, not nil, so dict["key"] as? String returns nil but dict["key"] != nil is true.

P3

NSCoding, NSSecureCoding, and archiving

final class Token: NSObject, NSSecureCoding {
    static var supportsSecureCoding: Bool { true }
    let value: String

    func encode(with coder: NSCoder) { coder.encode(value, forKey: "value") }
    required init?(coder: NSCoder) {
        guard let v = coder.decodeObject(of: NSString.self, forKey: "value") as String? else { return nil }
        value = v
    }
}
let data = try NSKeyedArchiver.archivedData(withRootObject: token, requiringSecureCoding: true)
let restored = try NSKeyedUnarchiver.unarchivedObject(ofClass: Token.self, from: data)

NSSecureCoding exists because plain NSCoding decoding instantiates whatever class the archive names, which is a remote-code-execution risk with untrusted data — secure coding requires you to declare the expected class up front. Use Codable for anything new; NSSecureCoding only when interoperating with ObjC APIs that require it (NSUserActivity, some UIDocument flows, UIPasteboard items).

P3

Mixing Swift and ObjC in one project — practical issues

Things that bite in real migrations:

  • Circular imports: an ObjC class can't inherit from a Swift class; Swift can't subclass an ObjC class that itself imports the Swift header. Break the cycle with protocols.
  • Build time: the generated -Swift.h header means changing any @objc Swift API rebuilds the ObjC side.
  • Swift-only features stop at the boundary — you'll end up writing @objc wrapper shims. Keep them thin and in one file.
  • Optionals: annotate every ObjC header before consuming it, or you're one nil away from a crash with no compiler warning.
  • Error handling: an ObjC method with NSError ** bridges to throws only if it follows the convention (returns BOOL or a nullable object and takes the error as the last parameter).
  • Migration strategy: migrate leaf classes first (models, utilities), then services, and leave view controllers for last since they have the most ObjC entanglement. Never rewrite and refactor in the same commit.
18

Behavioural & Experience Questions (3 Years)

17 questions
P0

The STAR structure, and how to not sound rehearsed

Situation (1–2 sentences of context) → Task (what was your specific responsibility) → Action (what you did, with technical specifics) → Result (a number, ideally).

The two failure modes: too much Situation (three minutes of company background) and no Result ("and then it was fine"). Aim for 15% situation, 15% task, 50% action, 20% result. Total 2–3 minutes.

Prepare six stories and you can answer almost any behavioural question by reframing one:

  1. A hard technical problem you solved (performance, crash, concurrency bug).
  2. A time you disagreed with someone and how it resolved.
  3. A production incident you handled.
  4. A project you owned end to end.
  5. A mistake you made and what changed afterwards.
  6. A time you influenced a decision without authority.

Write them down. Rehearse the structure, not the words.

P0

"Tell me about yourself"

The 90-second version, in this order: what you do now → the shape of your experience → one or two things you're known for → why you're here.

"I'm an iOS developer with about three years of experience, currently working on [app] — a [category] app with roughly [N] users. Most of my work has been in Swift with [UIKit/SwiftUI], and over the last year I've owned [specific area, e.g. the checkout flow and the offline sync layer]. The work I'm proudest of is [one concrete thing with a number — 'cutting our cold launch time from 2.1s to 800ms' or 'migrating our data layer to async/await and Swift 6 strict concurrency']. I'm looking for a role where I can take more ownership of architecture decisions, which is what drew me to this position."

Don't: recite your CV chronologically, start from college, or list every technology you've touched.

P0

"Tell me about a technically challenging problem you solved"

Use the performance or concurrency story. Structure:

S: "Our product feed was dropping to about 35 fps on an iPhone SE, and we were getting one-star reviews mentioning lag." T: "I owned the feed, so I picked it up." A: "I profiled in Release on a physical SE with the Animation Hitches instrument and found two things. First, we were decoding full-resolution images on the main thread inside cellForRow — a 12 MP photo decoding into a 60-point thumbnail. Second, we called reloadData on every pagination response, which rebuilt all visible cells. I moved decoding off-main with CGImageSourceCreateThumbnailAtIndex and downsampled to the display size, added an actor-based loader with an in-flight request map so duplicate URLs only downloaded once, and replaced reloadData with a diffable data source so only new rows were inserted." R: "Hitch ratio went from 42 ms per second of scroll to under 4, which is inside Apple's target. Memory during a long scroll dropped from about 340 MB to 90 MB. The review complaints stopped, and I wrote it up as a scrolling-performance checklist that the team now uses in code review."

Notice what makes this good: a measurement before and after, a specific tool, a specific API, and a systemic outcome (the checklist) beyond the fix.

P0

"Tell me about a production incident"

They want to see: did you stop the bleeding first, did you find the real cause, did you prevent recurrence, and did you communicate.

S: "We shipped a release and within two hours our crash-free rate dropped from 99.7% to 94%." T: "I was on release duty." A: "First priority was stopping the damage — I checked Crashlytics, saw the crash was isolated to a single screen behind a feature flag, and turned the flag off remotely, which took the crash rate back to normal within about twenty minutes without needing an app update. Then I symbolicated the top stack: a force-unwrap on a field the backend had started returning as null for a subset of accounts. The root cause wasn't really the force unwrap — it was that our DTO didn't distinguish between 'missing' and 'required', and we had no contract test against the backend." R: "The immediate fix shipped as a point release the next day. Longer term I made that field optional with a sensible default, added a lint rule blocking force-unwraps outside of tests and outlets, and we added a nightly contract test that decodes real staging responses against our DTOs. We haven't had a decoding crash since. I also wrote the postmortem — blameless, and the action items were all systemic rather than 'be more careful'."

The phrase "stop the bleeding first, then find the cause" is worth saying explicitly. Also: mention the remote kill switch, because it shows you build for operability.

P0

"Tell me about a time you disagreed with a colleague or manager"

The trap is sounding either combative or spineless. The shape that works: disagree on substance, escalate to data, commit to the outcome.

S: "A senior engineer wanted to adopt TCA across the whole app. I thought it was the wrong call for our team at that point." T: "I was the one who'd have to migrate two of the three main flows." A: "Rather than argue in the abstract, I asked if we could timebox a spike — I'd migrate one medium-complexity screen to TCA, he'd review it, and we'd look at the actual diff, the build-time impact, and how long it took me as someone who didn't know the framework. It took me most of a week for a screen that would have taken a day in our existing MVVM, and our incremental build time on that module went up noticeably. I also acknowledged what was genuinely better: the tests were much more precise. We ended up adopting the parts that solved our actual pain — a stricter state enum and a testable reducer for the two most complex screens — without the full framework." R: "We shipped on time, and six months later that decision still looks right for a team of four. What I took from it is that 'let's measure it on our codebase' resolves most architecture arguments faster than debating principles."

Then explicitly add the disagree-and-commit line: "If the decision had gone the other way, I'd have gone all in on it — the worst outcome is a half-migrated codebase because someone was sulking."

P1

"Tell me about a mistake you made"

Pick a real one with real consequences — a trivially small mistake reads as evasive. Own it fully, no blame-shifting, and land on what changed structurally.

"I shipped a migration that dropped data for about 2% of users. We'd added a field to our Core Data model and I assumed lightweight migration would handle it — it did in my testing, because I tested by updating from the previous version. But a chunk of our users were on a version from two releases earlier, where the entity had a different relationship shape, and that path needed a mapping model. Users on that path got a failed migration and my error handling deleted and recreated the store, so their local drafts were gone.

I owned it publicly in the incident channel the same day. We couldn't recover the data, which was the genuinely bad part — we emailed affected users. What I changed: I wrote a migration test harness that seeds a store from every shipped model version and asserts the migration succeeds and preserves row counts, and it runs in CI. And I changed the failure path so a migration failure keeps the old store file and reports rather than deleting. I'm much more careful now about the difference between 'it works from the previous version' and 'it works from every version in the wild'."

P1

"How do you handle tight deadlines / being asked to cut corners?"

The mature answer distinguishes deliberate technical debt from accidental.

"I try to make the trade-off explicit rather than silently absorbing it. If we need to ship in a week and the clean solution takes three, I'll say: here's what I can do in a week, here's specifically what we're deferring, here's the risk, and here's what it costs to fix later. Usually that turns into a real conversation about scope — often the answer is to ship a smaller feature properly rather than the full feature badly.

When we do take on debt deliberately, I make it visible: a ticket with the context written down while it's fresh, a // TODO: that references the ticket, and I try to get it into the next sprint rather than 'later'. What I try never to do is skip tests on the risky part or ship without a way to turn it off — a feature flag costs an hour and buys you the ability to not do a hotfix at 11pm."

P1

"How do you keep up with iOS?"

Be specific, and mention things that are actually current (this signals you're not reciting a list from 2019).

"WWDC is the anchor — I watch the What's New in Swift and SwiftUI sessions in June and then the deeper ones through the year. Day to day I read Swift by Sundell, Donny Wals, Antoine van der Lee, and the Swift Forums evolution threads, which are the best place to understand why something was designed a certain way. I follow the swift-evolution proposals for anything that affects us — I read through SE-0461 and the approachable concurrency proposals properly when we were planning our Swift 6 migration. And I learn most by building: I migrated a side project to Swift 6 strict concurrency before proposing it at work, so I'd hit the real problems first."

Have an opinion ready about one recent change, because they'll follow up with "what do you think about X?" Good candidates in 2026: Swift 6.2's default MainActor isolation, @Observable replacing ObservableObject, Swift Testing replacing XCTest, Liquid Glass adoption, and SwiftData's maturity.

P1

"Why are you leaving?" / "Why this company?"

Never criticise your current employer or manager. Frame it as moving toward something.

"I've learned a lot where I am and I've taken the iOS side about as far as I can — I'm the only iOS engineer, so there's nobody to review my architecture decisions or push back on me. I'm looking for a team where I can work alongside more experienced iOS engineers and take on bigger systems. What interested me about this role specifically is [something real and researched — the scale, a specific technical challenge, the product itself, something from the JD]."

Do fifteen minutes of research so the "why this company" part is concrete. "I read your engineering blog post about X" is worth a lot.

P1

"Where do you see yourself in 3–5 years?"

Avoid both "I want your job" and "I don't know". Show direction without over-committing.

"In the next couple of years I want to be the person who owns architecture for a substantial part of an app — making the calls on the data layer and the module boundaries, and being the one others come to for iOS depth. Beyond that I'm more interested in the staff-engineer direction than management, though I'd like to mentor, and I've been doing some of that already with our junior developer. Mostly I want to be somewhere I'm still learning, which is why the technical depth of this team matters to me."

P2

"Describe a time you had to learn something quickly"

Show a method, not just an outcome.

"When we decided to adopt Swift 6 strict concurrency, nobody on the team had done it. I gave myself a week. My approach was: read the primary sources first — the evolution proposals for Sendable and region-based isolation rather than blog summaries, because the proposals explain the reasoning. Then I did the smallest possible real thing: I turned on SWIFT_STRICT_CONCURRENCY = complete on one leaf module and fixed every warning, writing down each error category and what actually resolved it. That gave me a migration playbook. Then I ran a 45-minute session for the team walking through the five error types they'd see and the fix for each. The migration took about six weeks across the app and we did it without a big bang, module by module."

P2

"Tell me about a time you had to work with a difficult person / gave difficult feedback"

Emphasise assuming good intent, private conversation, and specific behaviour rather than character.

"A teammate's PRs were consistently large — 1500+ lines — which meant reviews were shallow and we were catching bugs in QA instead of in review. My first instinct was that he was being careless, but when I actually asked him about it, it turned out he found it hard to know where to break things up and he felt that opening a PR before the feature worked end to end would look bad.

So rather than making it about discipline, we paired on decomposing the next feature into four PRs before he started — model, service, view model, UI. I reviewed each within a couple of hours so the stack didn't stall him. After two features he was doing it himself. I also proposed we add a soft PR-size warning via Danger so it wasn't one person nagging another. The thing I took away is that 'why is this happening' usually has a more useful answer than 'this shouldn't happen'."

P2

"How do you approach code reviews?"

"As a reviewer, I try to separate blocking from non-blocking, and I say which is which — a comment like 'nit: naming' shouldn't hold up a merge, while 'this force-unwraps a server value' should. I look in order at correctness, then whether the behaviour is tested, then the API design — is this the right seam, will the next person extend it easily — and style last, because style should be automated. I try to ask questions rather than issue instructions when I'm not sure: 'what happens if this returns nil?' opens a conversation, 'this is wrong' closes one. And I try to say what I liked, because reviews that are only criticism make people defensive.

As an author, I keep PRs small, write a description explaining why not just what, and I self-review the diff before I request review — it's surprising how much you catch."

P2

"How do you estimate work?"

"I break it down until the pieces are things I've done before — if a piece is more than about two days I don't trust the estimate and I decompose it further. I estimate the unknowns separately and explicitly; if there's something I genuinely don't know, I ask for a timeboxed spike rather than putting a made-up number on it. I add buffer for the things that always take longer than people expect: code review, QA cycles, edge cases, and App Store review. And I communicate early when an estimate is slipping — a day-two warning is useful, a day-nine warning isn't."

P2

"Have you mentored anyone?"

Even if you haven't formally, you almost certainly have informally. Name it.

"Not formally, but I've been the main reviewer for our junior developer for about a year. What I've found works better than answering questions directly is answering with the approach — when she asked why a table view was flickering, instead of telling her it was the diffable snapshot, I walked her through how I'd narrow it down. She solved the next two similar bugs herself. I also started doing short informal sessions when I learn something the team needs — the Swift 6 migration one, and one on Instruments."

P3

Questions to ask them (always have four)

Asking nothing signals no interest. Have these ready and pick based on who's in the room:

  • "What does the iOS codebase look like — how much UIKit vs SwiftUI, and where are you in the Swift 6 migration?"
  • "How does a change get from a designer's Figma to the App Store here? Who decides what ships?"
  • "What's the most painful part of the codebase right now, and is there appetite to fix it?"
  • "How do you handle on-call and production incidents for mobile?"
  • "What does the review and release process look like — release cadence, feature flags, phased rollout?"
  • "What would you want someone in this role to have accomplished in their first six months?"
  • "How is technical decision-making done — who decides architecture, and how are disagreements resolved?"
  • "What made the last person who was successful in this role successful?"

To a hiring manager: ask about growth, ownership, and how performance is evaluated. To an engineer: ask about the codebase and the day-to-day. To a skip-level: ask about strategy and where mobile sits in it.

P3

Negotiation — the short version

Don't give a number first if you can avoid it: "I'd rather understand the role and the level first — what's the range you have budgeted for this position?" If pressed, give a researched range for your level and market, anchored slightly high, and say it's flexible for the right role.

When the offer comes: thank them, ask for it in writing, take 24–48 hours, and negotiate on the basis of the market and what you bring rather than personal need. Remember total compensation — base, bonus, equity, and the things that are often easier to move: level/title, joining bonus, and start date. And be genuinely willing to accept; negotiating hard on an offer you'll decline burns a bridge in a small industry.

19

Rapid Fire, Cheat Sheets & Study Plan

8 questions
P0

120 rapid-fire questions with one-line answers

These are screening-round questions. Answer each in 10–30 seconds.

Swift language

  1. struct vs class? Value vs reference semantics; struct has no identity, inheritance or deinit.
  2. Is a struct always on the stack? No — it's heap-allocated when captured by an escaping closure, stored in a class, or boxed in an existential.
  3. What's an optional? An enum with .some(Wrapped) and .none.
  4. if let vs guard let? guard requires an early exit and keeps the binding in the outer scope.
  5. What is copy-on-write? Deferred buffer copy, triggered when isKnownUniquelyReferenced is false.
  6. map vs compactMap vs flatMap? Transform; transform + drop nils; flatten one level (or chain optionals).
  7. reduce vs reduce(into:)? The latter passes the accumulator inout, avoiding a copy per element.
  8. What is lazy on a sequence? Defers and fuses the operations; nothing is computed until consumed.
  9. some vs any? Opaque single concrete type (static dispatch) vs existential box (dynamic dispatch).
  10. Why is AnyView slow? It erases structural identity so SwiftUI can't diff and rebuilds the subtree.
  11. What's a protocol extension's dispatch rule? Requirements dispatch dynamically; extension-only methods dispatch statically on the declared type.
  12. static vs class func? class can be overridden; static is final class.
  13. What does final buy you? Static dispatch, and it's a prerequisite for a Sendable class.
  14. What is @escaping? The closure may outlive the function call, so it's retained.
  15. @autoclosure? Wraps an argument expression in a closure for lazy evaluation — how ?? works.
  16. inout — pass by reference? No: copy-in/copy-out, with exclusive access enforced.
  17. mutating — what does it do? Allows reassigning self on a value type.
  18. What's defer? Runs on scope exit, in reverse order of declaration.
  19. throws vs Result? Control flow now vs storing/deferring the outcome.
  20. rethrows? Only throws if the closure argument throws.
  21. Typed throws? throws(MyError) — the error type is part of the signature (Swift 6).
  22. What's indirect enum for? Boxes the payload so the enum can be recursive.
  23. Access levels? open > public > package > internal > fileprivate > private.
  24. What does package mean? Visible across modules in the same Swift package.
  25. private scope? The declaration plus its extensions in the same file.
  26. Equatable/Hashable contract? Equal values must have equal hashes.
  27. When is Hashable not synthesised? Classes, and when any stored property isn't Hashable.
  28. What's a property wrapper? A type that factors out a property's get/set; $ exposes projectedValue.
  29. What's a result builder? Transforms a statement list into nested buildBlock/buildEither calls — powers @ViewBuilder.
  30. What's a macro? Compile-time code generation via SwiftSyntax; @Observable, #Preview, #expect.
  31. Key path types? KeyPath, WritableKeyPath, ReferenceWritableKeyPath, PartialKeyPath, AnyKeyPath.
  32. Why is String.count O(n)? Characters are variable-width grapheme clusters.
  33. ~Copyable? A noncopyable type — linear ownership, enforced at compile time.
  34. borrowing vs consuming? A read-only loan vs taking ownership.
  35. What's @frozen? Promises an enum/struct won't gain cases/fields, enabling client-side optimisation.

Memory

  1. What is ARC? Compile-time inserted retain/release; deterministic, no cycle collector.
  2. weak vs unowned? Zeroed optional (side table) vs non-zeroed, crashes if used after dealloc.
  3. When is unowned correct? When the referenced object is guaranteed to outlive the reference.
  4. Does every closure need [weak self]? No — only escaping closures stored by self (or with an unbounded lifetime).
  5. Leak vs abandoned memory? Unreachable vs reachable-but-never-used; Leaks finds only the first.
  6. How do you find a retain cycle? Memory Graph Debugger, deinit logging, Instruments Leaks.
  7. Why is a delegate weak? The parent owns the child; a strong back-pointer makes a cycle.
  8. When do you need autoreleasepool? Tight loops over bridged Foundation APIs that return autoreleased objects.
  9. Decoded size of a 12 MP image? ~48 MB (4032×3024×4), not the JPEG's 3 MB.
  10. What's NSCache better at than a Dictionary? Thread safety and automatic eviction under memory pressure.

Concurrency

  1. Serial vs concurrent queue? One item at a time vs many; orthogonal to sync/async.
  2. Why does DispatchQueue.main.sync on main deadlock? The thread blocks waiting for work that can only run on that thread.
  3. What's a barrier? A block that runs exclusively on a concurrent queue — the readers-writer pattern.
  4. DispatchGroup vs semaphore? Wait for N tasks vs limit concurrency / signal.
  5. What replaced them? Task groups and actors.
  6. What happens at await? The function may suspend; the thread is released; resumption may be on another thread.
  7. Does await mean it runs in parallel? No — sequential awaits are sequential. Use async let or a task group.
  8. Structured vs unstructured? Child tasks scoped to the parent (auto cancellation/error propagation) vs Task {}.
  9. What does Task.detached inherit? Nothing — no actor, no priority, no task-locals.
  10. How does cancellation work? Cooperatively — Task.isCancelled / try Task.checkCancellation().
  11. What is an actor? A reference type that serialises access to its mutable state.
  12. What is actor reentrancy? Other calls can enter while the actor is suspended at an await.
  13. How do you prevent duplicate work in an actor cache? Store an in-flight Task and await it.
  14. What is @MainActor? A global actor whose executor is the main queue.
  15. What is Sendable? A marker that a type is safe to cross isolation boundaries.
  16. How do you make a class Sendable? final + all immutable Sendable properties, or global-actor isolated, or @unchecked with your own locking.
  17. What is sending? Transfers a non-Sendable value across a boundary, backed by region isolation.
  18. What changed in Swift 6? Data-race safety errors became compile errors.
  19. What is approachable concurrency (6.2)? nonisolated async runs on the caller's actor by default; opt out with @concurrent.
  20. What is default actor isolation = MainActor? Every declaration is implicitly @MainActor unless marked otherwise.
  21. Data race vs race condition? Unsynchronised memory access vs timing-dependent logic bug.
  22. What is thread explosion? GCD spawning threads when all are blocked; Swift's pool is fixed-width.
  23. Why not block inside a Task? It starves the cooperative pool and can deadlock the runtime.
  24. How do you wrap a callback API? withCheckedThrowingContinuation, resumed exactly once.
  25. AsyncStream vs AsyncThrowingStream? The latter can finish with an error.
  26. What's @TaskLocal? A value propagated to child tasks (but not detached ones).
  27. Combine vs async/await? Streams of values over time vs a single eventual value.

UIKit

  1. viewDidLoad vs viewWillAppear? Once vs every appearance.
  2. Where do you set a CAGradientLayer frame? viewDidLayoutSubviews — layers don't auto-resize.
  3. frame vs bounds? Superview's coordinate space vs its own.
  4. What's frame when a transform is applied? The bounding box — treat it as undefined.
  5. setNeedsLayout vs layoutIfNeeded? Mark dirty vs force a synchronous pass now.
  6. How do you animate a constraint change? Change constant, then layoutIfNeeded() inside the animation block.
  7. Hugging vs compression resistance? Resist growing vs resist shrinking.
  8. Which label truncates? The one with lower compression resistance.
  9. How does cell reuse work? A pool of (visible + buffer) cells; reset state in prepareForReuse.
  10. Why does the wrong image appear in a cell? Async load completes after reuse — check identity and cancel.
  11. Why diffable data source? Correct animations and no "invalid number of rows" crashes.
  12. reloadItems vs reconfigureItems? Dequeue a new cell vs update the existing one in place.
  13. What makes self-sizing cells work? An unbroken constraint chain top to bottom, plus estimatedRowHeight.
  14. What is hit testing? hitTest(_:with:) walks front-to-back depth-first calling point(inside:).
  15. Why doesn't a button outside its superview's bounds respond? The superview's hit test rejects the point first.
  16. What causes offscreen rendering? Masking + corner radius, shadows without shadowPath, layer.mask, rasterisation.
  17. Model vs presentation layer? UIView.animate changes the model; CAAnimation animates the presentation only.
  18. What are contentLayoutGuide and frameLayoutGuide? Scroll view content size vs visible window.
  19. When does a Timer stop firing? When added in .default mode and the user scrolls — use .common.

SwiftUI

  1. What is a SwiftUI View? An immutable value describing UI, not the UI itself.
  2. What is view identity? Structural (tree position) or explicit (.id); it controls state preservation.
  3. @State vs @StateObject? Value-type/owned state vs an ObservableObject created once per identity.
  4. @StateObject vs @ObservedObject? Owns and persists vs doesn't own — @ObservedObject with an initialiser recreates it on every update.
  5. What replaced ObservableObject? @Observable, with per-key-path tracking.
  6. When do you need @Bindable? To get $ bindings from an @Observable object you don't own.
  7. How does SwiftUI layout work? Parent proposes, child chooses, parent places.
  8. Why is GeometryReader a trap? It accepts the full proposed size and top-leading aligns its content.
  9. What replaced it? onGeometryChange, containerRelativeFrame, visualEffect.
  10. What is a PreferenceKey? Child-to-parent data flow, reduced across siblings.
  11. .task vs .onAppear? .task is async and auto-cancels on disappear.
  12. Why not use array indices in ForEach? Identity breaks on insertion, so diffs and animations go wrong.
  13. List vs LazyVStack? List reuses rows; LazyVStack only creates lazily and never discards.
  14. How do you find excessive re-renders? Self._printChanges() and the SwiftUI instrument.
  15. What does .drawingGroup() do? Flattens the subtree into one Metal-rendered layer.
  16. How do you embed UIKit? UIViewRepresentable / UIViewControllerRepresentable with a Coordinator.
  17. What is .glassEffect()? The iOS 26 Liquid Glass material modifier.

Networking / data / testing / tooling

  1. 401 vs 403? Not authenticated vs authenticated but not permitted.
  2. Which methods are idempotent? GET, PUT, DELETE, HEAD — safe to retry.
  3. Why jitter in backoff? So clients don't retry in lockstep and stampede.
  4. How do you avoid a token-refresh stampede? Single-flight: store the in-flight refresh Task and await it.
  5. Why pin the public key, not the certificate? Certificates rotate annually; keys usually don't.
  6. How do you test networking? URLProtocol stubbing with a real URLSession.
  7. Where do you store a token? Keychain, with the right accessibility class. Never UserDefaults.
  8. Do Keychain items survive app deletion? Yes — clear them on first launch after install.
  9. Core Data threading rule? Contexts are queue-bound; use perform; pass NSManagedObjectID, never objects.
  10. What's the Core Data N+1? Relationship faults firing per row — fix with relationshipKeyPathsForPrefetching.
  11. SwiftData vs Core Data? Modern Swift API vs migration control, FRC, and maturity.
  12. #expect vs #require? Record and continue vs throw and abort.
P0

The "what would you say" cheat card

Phrases that make you sound senior, used honestly:

  • "Let me state my assumptions first…" (before any design answer)
  • "The trade-off here is…"
  • "I'd measure before optimising — specifically I'd use [tool] and look at [metric]."
  • "That works, but it breaks when…"
  • "I'd model that as an enum so the impossible states can't compile."
  • "I'd put that behind a protocol so I can inject a fake in tests."
  • "That's a policy decision, not a correctness one."
  • "I don't know that one. My mental model is X — am I close?"
  • "In production we hit exactly this, and what we did was…"
  • "With more time I'd also add…" (closing any coding exercise)
P1

Common trick questions and their traps

QuestionThe trapThe answer
"Are structs always faster than classes?"Saying yesNo — large structs copy; COW adds checks; existential boxing allocates. Measure.
"Does weak self prevent all leaks?"Saying yesNo — it prevents cycles through that closure. Timers, observers, and retained tasks still leak.
"Is atomic thread safe?"Saying yesNo — only the individual accessor is atomic; compound operations still race.
"Does async mean it runs on a background thread?"Saying yesNo — it means it can suspend. With approachable concurrency it runs on the caller's actor.
"Do actors prevent race conditions?"Saying yesThey prevent data races. Reentrancy still allows logic race conditions.
"Is DispatchQueue.main.async from the main thread a deadlock?"Saying yesNo, that's sync. async just defers to the next run loop pass.
"Can you use weak on a struct?"Saying yesNo — weak requires a class (AnyObject).
"Will deinit run on the main thread?"Saying yesNo — whichever thread released the last reference.
"Does viewWillAppear fire when returning from background?"Saying yesNo — that's sceneDidBecomeActive.
"Is UserDefaults encrypted?"Saying yesNo. It's a plist.
"Is Set iteration order stable?"Saying yesNo — Hasher is seeded per process.
"Is sorted(by:) stable?"Saying yesNo — it's introsort. Decorate with an index if you need stability.
"Can a protocol extension method be overridden?"Saying yesOnly if it's declared as a protocol requirement.
"Does try? handle the error?"Saying yesIt discards it, which is usually worse than handling it.
P1

Version-by-version: what shipped when (so you don't sound dated)

  • Swift 5.5 / iOS 15 — async/await, actors, @MainActor, AsyncSequence, structured concurrency.
  • Swift 5.7 / iOS 16 — any existentials, primary associated types, if let x shorthand, regex literals, Layout protocol, NavigationStack, Charts.
  • Swift 5.9 / iOS 17 — macros, @Observable, ~Copyable, parameter packs, if/switch expressions, SwiftData, ContentUnavailableView, onChange(of:) two-param form, visualEffect, phaseAnimator, @Entry-era environment work.
  • Swift 6.0 / iOS 18 — Swift 6 language mode (strict concurrency errors), typed throws, Synchronization (Mutex, Atomic), Swift Testing, count(where:).
  • Swift 6.2 / iOS 26 / Xcode 26 — Approachable Concurrency: nonisolated(nonsending) by default, @concurrent, Default Actor Isolation = MainActor, InferIsolatedConformances; Liquid Glass design system (glassEffect, GlassEffectContainer, buttonStyle(.glass), tabBarMinimizeBehavior).
  • Swift 6.3 — module selectors (::), @specialized, @c for C export, @inline(always), Swift Testing cancellation and attachments.
  • Swift 6.4 (WWDC26) — borrow/mutate accessors, Iterable for borrowing loops, @diagnose, anyAppleOS availability, Ref/MutableRef, UniqueArray/UniqueBox, Continuation with compile-time single-resume checking, any/some without parentheses.

If an interviewer asks "what's new in Swift", pick two and explain what problem they solve — don't list everything.

P1

Your 14-day study plan

Days 1–2 — P0 sweep. Read every P0 in this booklet. Don't code, just read and note what you can't explain out loud. Expect ~90 questions.

Days 3–4 — Concurrency (Chapter 3). Type out every code sample. Build a tiny app with an actor-backed image cache. Be able to explain reentrancy, Sendable, and approachable concurrency without notes.

Day 5 — Memory + ARC (Chapter 2). Build a deliberate retain cycle, find it in the Memory Graph Debugger, fix it. Do the same with a Timer and with a NotificationCenter block observer.

Days 6–7 — Your weaker UI framework. If you're a UIKit dev, do Chapter 5 and build one SwiftUI screen with @Observable, a state enum, and pagination. If you're a SwiftUI dev, do Chapter 4 and build a diffable-data-source collection view with compositional layout.

Day 8 — Architecture + testing (Chapters 6, 9). Take one screen you've written and refactor it to MVVM with an injected protocol and three unit tests. This becomes an interview story.

Day 9 — Machine coding (Chapter 15). Set a 75-minute timer and build the searchable paginated list from scratch, no copy-paste. Repeat until you can do it in 60.

Day 10 — System design (Chapter 14). Talk through the image loader and the chat app out loud, to a wall or a recording. Time yourself: 45 minutes each.

Day 11 — DSA (Chapter 16). Two hours of LeetCode Easy/Medium in Swift. Focus on two pointers, sliding window, hash maps, BFS.

Day 12 — Networking + persistence (Chapters 7, 8). Write the network layer from memory. Then write the token-refresh single-flight actor.

Day 13 — Behavioural (Chapter 18). Write out your six stories in STAR form. Say them out loud. Cut each to under three minutes.

Day 14 — Weak spots + mock interview. Go back to everything you flagged on days 1–2. Do a mock with a friend or record yourself answering 20 random P0s.

Every day: 20 minutes of rapid-fire (Chapter 19) out loud, not in your head. Speaking is a separate skill from knowing.

P2

The day before and the day of

Day before: skim only the P0s and your six stories. Don't learn anything new — it displaces what you know. Check your setup if it's remote: Xcode open with a blank project, a stable connection, a charged laptop, water. Sleep.

During: repeat the question back if it's ambiguous. Think out loud — silence is scored as being stuck. If you blank, say "let me think about that for a second" and actually think; it reads as considered, not slow. If you realise mid-answer that you're wrong, say so and correct yourself — that scores higher than being right first time, because it shows you self-correct.

After: write down every question they asked within an hour. That list is worth more than any booklet for your next interview, including the one at the company that just rejected you.

P2

Red flags that sink otherwise-good candidates

  • Blaming a previous employer, manager, or teammate for anything.
  • Claiming to know something and then being unable to go one level deeper. Interviewers always probe; the mismatch is what kills you, not the gap.
  • Answering a "why" question with a "what" answer (reciting the definition instead of the mechanism).
  • Not asking any questions at the end.
  • Writing code in silence for ten minutes.
  • Over-engineering a simple task to show off — a machine coding round doesn't need VIPER.
  • Saying "we" for everything so the interviewer can't tell what you did.
  • No numbers in any story.
P2

One-page final checklist

Before you walk in, you should be able to, without notes:

  • Explain struct vs class in terms of semantics, not storage
  • Write a retain cycle and three ways to fix it
  • Explain what happens at an await and why the thread may change
  • Write an actor-backed cache that coalesces in-flight requests
  • Explain Sendable and how to make a class conform
  • Explain approachable concurrency and default MainActor isolation
  • Explain the UIKit layout cycle and animate a constraint change
  • Fix the async-image-in-a-reused-cell bug
  • Explain SwiftUI view identity and @State vs @StateObject
  • Explain why @Observable re-renders less than ObservableObject
  • Write a generic async network layer with typed errors
  • Write retry with exponential backoff and jitter
  • Explain single-flight token refresh
  • Explain Core Data's threading rules and the N+1 fault problem
  • Write a unit test with an injected mock, in Swift Testing
  • Diagnose scroll jank with a named tool and a named metric
  • Design an image cache and a paginated feed out loud in 45 minutes
  • Tell six STAR stories in under three minutes each
  • Ask four good questions

Good luck. You know more than you think — the interview is mostly about showing it clearly.

No questions match that filter. Try clearing the search or re-enabling a priority.
